### [1] [Bi-MoDe: Bilateral Control-based Imitation Learning via Modifier-Conditioned Decoding for Modulation of Execution Speed and Contact Intensity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16040)
*Takumi Kobayashi,Masato Kobayashi,Yuki Uranishi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Bi-MoDe is a modifier-conditioned decoding framework for bilateral control-based imitation learning that injects a constrained latent into every Transformer decoder layer via adaLN-Zero, enabling operators to specify how learned tasks are executed (e.g., slowly/fast, gently/firmly), improving physical directive following on a whiteboard wiping task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bilateral control-based imitation learning captures position and force information, making it suitable for contact-rich manipulation, but existing approaches give operators limited means to specify how a learned task should be executed at inference (e.g., slowly vs quickly, gently vs firmly).

Method: Bi-MoDe is a modifier-conditioned decoding framework that injects a constrained latent into every layer of the Transformer action decoder via adaLN-Zero, so behavioral directives directly influence action-chunk generation. It is evaluated on a real-world whiteboard wiping task with combinations of temporal and physical modifiers, plus an ablation on decoder conditioning and latent-space composition.

Result: Bi-MoDe improves physical directive following over the action-chunking baseline while maintaining comparable temporal control. The ablation shows that decoder conditioning and latent-space composition interact, and their combination is important for accurate physical directive following.

Conclusion: Injecting constrained modifier latents across all Transformer decoder layers via adaLN-Zero enables effective behavioral directive control, with combined decoder conditioning and latent-space composition being key for accurate physical directive following.

Abstract: Bilateral control-based imitation learning captures both position and force information, making it well suited to contact-rich manipulation. However, existing approaches provide limited means for an operator to specify how a learned task should be executed at inference time, such as slowly or quickly, gently or firmly. We propose Bi-MoDe, a modifier-conditioned decoding framework that injects a constrained latent into every layer of the Transformer action decoder via adaLN-Zero, allowing behavioral directives to directly influence action-chunk generation. We evaluate the method on a real-world whiteboard wiping task with combinations of temporal and physical modifiers. Bi-MoDe improves physical directive following over the action-chunking baseline while maintaining comparable temporal control. An ablation further shows that decoder conditioning and latent-space composition interact, and that their combination is important for accurate physical directive following. Additional material is available at the https://mertcookimg.github.io/bi-mode/

</details>


### [2] [MR-GLi: Mixed Reality-Based Gripper-Linked Overlays for Underwater Robot Arm Teleoperation via Bilateral Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16041)
*Masashi Sasago,Masato Kobayashi,Yuki Uranishi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MR-GLi spatially registers a reaction torque indicator and wrist-camera image onto the robot gripper in mixed reality, matching 2D monitor torque-regulation performance while reducing perceived attention-shifting burden in underwater bilateral teleoperation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual torque feedback aids underwater bilateral teleoperation, but whether mixed reality (MR) outperforms conventional 2D monitor presentation for delivering this feedback is unclear.

Method: An MR interface (MR-GLi) that spatially registers a reaction torque indicator and wrist-camera image to the robot gripper. Twenty participants performed lift and pick-and-place tasks with rigid and compliant objects in a counterbalanced within-subject comparison against a 2D monitor, using identical visual-feedback content and four-channel bilateral control.

Result: MR-GLi provided gripper-linked access to visual feedback while maintaining torque-regulation performance similar to the 2D monitor. Subjective evaluation indicated reduced perceived burden of shifting attention between the workspace and visual feedback.

Conclusion: Gripper-linked MR overlays are feasible for underwater bilateral teleoperation, and information access—not only task performance—should be considered when evaluating teleoperation interfaces.

Abstract: Visual torque feedback supports underwater bilateral teleoperation, but the benefit of mixed reality (MR) over conventional monitor presentation remains unclear. We present MR-GLi, an MR interface that spatially registers a reaction torque indicator and wrist-camera image to the robot gripper. Twenty participants performed lift and pick-and-place tasks with rigid and compliant objects in a counterbalanced within-subject comparison with a 2D monitor, using identical visual-feedback content and four-channel bilateral control. MR-GLi provided gripper-linked access to visual feedback while maintaining a similar level of torque-regulation performance to the 2D monitor. Subjective evaluation further indicated reduced perceived burden associated with shifting attention between the workspace and visual feedback. These results demonstrate the feasibility of gripper-linked MR overlays for underwater bilateral teleoperation and highlight the importance of considering information access in addition to task performance. Additional material: https://mertcookimg.github.io/mr-gli/

</details>


### [3] [World-Action Models for Robot Learning and Control: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16074)
*Zuxing Lu,Hongjia Zhai,Guanzhi Wang,Huajian Zeng,Jiaqi Yang,Jingyu Liu,Lei Cheng,Yuantai Zhang,Yuheng Qiu,Zezhou Cheng,Ivan Laptev,Danfei Xu,Benjamin Riviere,Giuseppe Loianno,Eric Xing,Xingxing Zuo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This survey provides a robotics-oriented review of World-Action Models (WAMs), which couple future world prediction with executable action generation, offering a unified taxonomy, application review, evaluation resources, and open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots in open environments operate under partial observability, physical constraints, and dynamic task contexts. They must anticipate how candidate actions affect future states and task-relevant outcomes, not merely map observations and language to actions. Recent advances in world models, video generation, and VLA policies motivate WAMs, but a robotics-focused synthesis is lacking.

Method: A survey that (1) clarifies WAM scope relative to conventional world models, model-based RL, action-conditioned video generation, and reactive VLA policies; (2) organizes methods via a unified taxonomy spanning representations, transition modeling, action interfaces, architectures, training pipelines, data modalities, and scaling strategies; (3) reviews applications in manipulation, navigation, and autonomous driving; and (4) summarizes datasets, benchmarks, metrics, and protocols.

Result: The survey delivers a structured taxonomy of WAM methods, an application review across manipulation, navigation, and autonomous driving, and a consolidated account of evaluation resources (datasets, benchmarks, metrics, protocols) for WAM systems.

Conclusion: Key challenges are identified: action alignment, world-action factorization, spatial and multi-view consistency, long-horizon memory, neural simulation for closed-loop policy learning, and efficient inference. The survey aims to provide a technical foundation for integrating predictive world modeling with action generation toward reliable embodied robot intelligence.

Abstract: Robots operating in open environments act under partial observability, physical constraints, and dynamic task contexts. Beyond mapping observations and language instructions to actions, they must anticipate how candidate actions may affect future states and task-relevant outcomes. Recent advances in world models, video generation, and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies have motivated the development of World-Action Models (WAMs), which couple future world prediction with executable action generation. This survey provides a robotics-oriented review of WAMs. We clarify their scope relative to conventional world models, model-based reinforcement learning, action-conditioned video generation, and reactive VLA policies, and organize existing methods through a unified taxonomy covering representations, transition modeling, action interfaces, architectures, training pipelines, data modalities, and scaling strategies. We further review applications of WAMs in manipulation, navigation, and autonomous driving, and we summarize the datasets, benchmarks, metrics, and protocols used to evaluate WAM systems. Finally, we discuss key challenges in action alignment, world-action factorization, spatial and multi-view consistency, long-horizon memory, neural simulation for closed-loop policy learning, and efficient inference. Taken together, this survey aims to provide a concise technical foundation for integrating predictive world modeling with action generation, toward more reliable embodied robot intelligence. Project page: https://rcl-robotics.github.io/Awesome-World-Action-Models.

</details>


### [4] [AssemblyGrid v1: A Benchmark for Multi-Robot Production with Temporary Coalitions, Local Information, and Geometric Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16075)
*Fouad Bahrpeyma,David Heik,Dirk Reichelt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces AssemblyGrid v1, a reproducible benchmark for multi-robot flexible production formulated as cooperative multi-agent decision making under partial observability and resource contention, and validates it via conformance checks and centralized, structured-decentralized, and MARL experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flexible robotic production requires tightly coupled joint decisions—process progression, material routing, resource assignment, temporary cooperation, and concurrent execution—where each choice constrains the feasibility of others. Decentralized control compounds this difficulty: robots act on bounded local information while system-level progress hinges on collective decisions, shared resources, material state, and workspace compatibility. Existing benchmarks rarely capture these coupled, task-level properties in one formulation.

Method: The authors design AssemblyGrid v1, a task-level benchmark merging explicit process progression, decentralized observations, material transfer, temporary multi-robot coalitions, productive concurrency, and geometry-dependent feasibility. It offers Flow, Coalition, and Concurrency workload families, each with three scenario levels. Success and evaluation metrics are defined independently of learning reward and solution method, so learning-based and non-learning approaches solve the same problem. Evaluation combines executable conformance checks, mechanism studies, and algorithmic experiments using a privileged centralized reference, structured decentralized controllers, and MARL methods (IPPO, MAPPO, QMIX).

Result: Executable conformance checks and mechanism studies confirm the benchmark's intended structure. Productive execution is achieved under both centralized and decentralized control. MARL experiments show decentralized policies can learn effective production behavior purely from local observations and actions.

Conclusion: AssemblyGrid v1 provides a controlled, reproducible testbed for studying cooperative decision making in flexible robotic production, bridging process-level production requirements with decentralized multi-agent learning under partial observability and resource contention.

Abstract: Flexible robotic production requires joint decisions on process progression, material routing, resource assignment, temporary cooperation, and simultaneous execution, since each decision can affect the feasibility of the others. The challenge is greater under decentralized control, where each robot acts from bounded local information while system progress depends on collective decisions, shared resources, material state, and workspace compatibility. These properties closely match cooperative multi-agent decision making under partial observability and resource contention. This paper introduces AssemblyGrid v1, a reproducible benchmark for repeated multi-robot production that combines explicit process progression, decentralized observations, material transfer, temporary multi-robot coalitions, productive concurrency, and geometry-dependent feasibility within one task-level formulation. The benchmark includes Flow, Coalition, and Concurrency workload families, each with three scenario levels. Task success and evaluation measures are defined independently of learning reward and solution method, allowing learning-based and non-learning methods to address the same production problem. AssemblyGrid v1 is evaluated through executable conformance checks, mechanism studies, and algorithmic experiments using a privileged centralized reference, structured decentralized controllers, and MARL methods including IPPO, MAPPO, and QMIX. Results demonstrate productive execution under centralized and decentralized control. The MARL experiments further show that decentralized policies can learn effective production behavior from local observations and actions, supporting AssemblyGrid as a controlled benchmark for studying cooperative decision making in flexible robotic production.

</details>


### [5] [Structure-Preserving Quantum Circuit Architectures for Robot Kinematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16089)
*Andrea Morghen,Pierluigi Arpenti,Roberto Schiattarella,Giovanni Acampora,Bruno Siciliano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A quantum representation and circuit architecture encodes rigid-body transformations, specialized to Denavit-Hartenberg kinematics of serial open-chain manipulators, with Bloch-vector direction encoding, magnitude-weighted readout, and numerically negligible reconstruction errors in ideal simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured spatial data require quantum encodings that preserve geometric relations, expose measurable observables, and remain implementable on finite-depth hardware. Existing quantum encodings for spatial or kinematic data often lack geometric interpretability, measurable readout interfaces, and hardware implementability.

Method: The work introduces a quantum representation and circuit architecture for rigid-body transformations, specialized to Denavit-Hartenberg kinematics of serial open-chain manipulators. Each translational contribution is factorized into a classical metric magnitude and a signed unit direction encoded by a single-qubit Bloch vector; parameterized rotations reproduce ordered propagation of frame directions. A selector register prepares probabilities proportional to contribution magnitudes, and the reduced state of a designated readout qubit encodes their normalized weighted sum. The retained classical scale reconstructs the metric end-effector position. Two additional readout qubits encode terminal-frame axes, giving a compact pose interface. Measured Pauli observables reproduce classical kinematic quantities at the ideal expectation-value level. Alternative circuit architectures realize the same representation with different tradeoffs in qubit count, circuit depth, controlled operations, and measurement requirements.

Result: Validation on a serial manipulator yields numerically negligible position and orientation reconstruction errors under ideal simulation. Finite-shot simulations, noisy executions, transpilation analysis, and a hardware demonstration characterize statistical error, noise sensitivity, and implementation overhead, without asserting computational advantage.

Conclusion: The proposed quantum encoding provides a geometrically interpretable and implementable representation of rigid-body and Denavit-Hartenberg kinematics, with measurable Pauli observables reproducing classical kinematic quantities and a compact pose interface. Practical characterization covers statistical error, noise sensitivity, and implementation overhead; no computational advantage is claimed.

Abstract: Structured spatial data require quantum encodings that preserve geometric relations, expose measurable observables, and remain implementable on finite-depth hardware. This work introduces a quantum representation and circuit architecture for rigid-body transformations and specializes it to Denavit--Hartenberg kinematics of serial open-chain manipulators. Each translational contribution is factorized into a classical metric magnitude and a signed unit direction encoded by a single-qubit Bloch vector, while parameterized rotations reproduce the ordered propagation of frame directions. A selector register prepares probabilities proportional to the contribution magnitudes, and the reduced state of a designated readout qubit encodes their normalized weighted sum. The retained classical scale then reconstructs the metric end-effector position. Two additional readout qubits encode terminal-frame axes, providing a compact and geometrically interpretable pose interface. At the ideal expectation-value level, measured Pauli observables reproduce the corresponding classical kinematic quantities. Alternative circuit architectures realize the same representation with different tradeoffs in qubit count, circuit depth, controlled operations, and measurement requirements. Validation on a serial manipulator yields numerically negligible position and orientation reconstruction errors under ideal simulation. Finite-shot simulations, noisy executions, transpilation analysis, and a hardware demonstration further characterize statistical error, noise sensitivity, and implementation overhead without asserting computational advantage.

</details>


### [6] [Occupancy Network-Guided Autonomous Robotic Partial Nephrectomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16186)
*Ethan Kilmer,Pit Henrich,Jiawei Ge,Paul M. Scheikl,Laura Connolly,Soum D. Lokeshwar,Joseph Chen,Justin D. Opfermann,Kaitlyn Kumar,Lauren Shepard,Ahmed Ghazi,Nirmish Singla,Richard J. Cha,Kevin Cleary,Franziska Mathis-Ullrich,Axel Krieger*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The authors present the first vision-guided autonomous system for complete soft-tissue tumor resection in partial nephrectomy, using simulation-trained conditional occupancy networks for real-time 3-D anatomy inference from partial point clouds, validated on patient-derived hydrogel phantoms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous soft-tissue cancer surgery has been limited to organ-surface interventions because existing systems cannot perceive or adapt to anatomy once tissue deforms or is cut. Full tumor resection with margin control requires continuous intraoperative perception and adaptive execution, which prior systems lack.

Method: The system integrates conditional occupancy networks trained entirely in a physics-based simulation to infer full 3-D anatomy (tumor, margin tissue, kidney) from single-view partial point clouds, maintaining intraoperative tracking under cutting and deformation to support adaptive planning. The platform combines a depth camera for point-cloud capture, dual robotic arms for electrosurgical cutting and vacuum-based tissue manipulation, and an autonomous resection control strategy. Evaluation used patient-derived hydrogel phantoms in an open partial nephrectomy setting.

Result: The robot performed eight consecutive autonomous tumor resections comprising 77 electrosurgical cuts. All cuts achieved negative surgical margins, with a mean absolute margin error of 1.61 ± 0.48 mm.

Conclusion: The work establishes a foundation for supervised autonomous, closed-loop, imaging-driven, margin-negative tumor removal in soft-tissue phantoms, demonstrating feasibility beyond organ-surface interventions.

Abstract: Autonomous soft-tissue cancer surgery has been limited to interventions on organ surfaces, because current systems cannot perceive and adapt to anatomy once it deforms or is cut. We introduce the first vision-guided autonomous system capable of performing complete tumor resections for partial nephrectomy. Our system integrates conditional occupancy networks, trained entirely in a physics-based simulation, that infer full 3-D anatomy (tumor, margin tissue, and kidney) from single-view partial point clouds. These occupancy networks maintain intraoperative tracking even as tissue is cut and deformed, enabling adaptive planning and execution. The surgical platform combines a depth camera for capturing surface point clouds, dual robotic arms for electrosurgical cutting and vacuum-based tissue manipulation, and an autonomous control strategy for tumor resection. In patient-derived hydrogel phantoms under an open partial nephrectomy setting, the robot performed eight consecutive autonomous tumor resections comprising 77 electrosurgical cuts, with all cuts achieving negative surgical margins and 1.61 $\pm$ 0.48 mm mean absolute margin error. This work demonstrates, for the first time, a foundation for supervised autonomous closed-loop, imaging-driven, margin-negative tumor removal in phantoms.

</details>


### [7] [Tendon-Driven Continuum Robot with Modular Stiffness and In-Situ Self Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16256)
*Guo Ning,Sue,Zheng Cao,Junzhe Hu,Xiangyun Bu,David Quinn,Tiancheng Wu,Zackory Erickson,Carmel Majidi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A self-contained, modular continuum robot with interchangeable joints and onboard magnetic proprioception; a single per-joint learned model generalizes across reconfigurations, validated in real-world experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing continuum robots are task-specific and rely on external sensing (e.g., external tracking), which limits adaptability and practical deployment in real-world, confined environments.

Method: The platform uses interchangeable continuum joints with analytically precomputed stiffness for rapid assembly and shape programming. Proprioception combines magnetic sensors with a modular learning-based framework: one model is trained per joint and reused across different robot configurations.

Result: Real-world experiments demonstrate self-sensing capabilities and shape adaptation of the reconfigurable platform without external tracking infrastructure.

Conclusion: Mechanical reconfigurability combined with modular onboard pose estimation yields a self-contained continuum robotic platform that is adaptable and deployable without external sensing.

Abstract: Continuum robots enable smooth shape morphing and safe interaction in confined environments. However, most existing systems are task-specific and depend on external sensing infrastructure, limiting their adaptability and real-world deployment. This paper presents a self-contained modular continuum robotic platform that combines mechanical reconfigurability with onboard pose estimation. The robot is constructed from interchangeable continuum joints with analytically precomputed stiffness, allowing rapid assembly and direct programming of the robot shape. Proprioceptive sensing is achieved using magnetic sensors and a modular learning-based framework, where a single model is trained per joint and reused across configurations. The system is experimentally validated in real world, demonstrating self-sensing capabilities and adaptation without external tracking.

</details>


### [8] [ConGraspXL: Controllable Constraint-Conditioned Dexterous Grasping Motion Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16319)
*Hui Zhang,Mirko Meboldt,Jie Song*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ConGraspXL extends GraspXL with controllable, constraint-conditioned grasp motion synthesis, enabling precise and flexible control over diverse task-driven constraints and their combinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous grasping is typically task-specific, imposing heterogeneous constraints (approach directions, contact regions, wrist trajectories, functional poses). Prior work GraspXL enables scalable grasp motion synthesis for diverse objects and hand morphologies but lacks controllability under such task-driven constraints.

Method: The authors introduce a hierarchical constraint formulation, a masked residual interface for flexible constraint composition, and enhanced control precision via dynamic hand centers and feed-forward wrist guidance.

Result: ConGraspXL achieves precise and flexible controllability for individual constraints and their combinations while preserving GraspXL's strong generalization, functioning as a plug-and-play low-level grasp controller.

Conclusion: ConGraspXL supports downstream applications such as whole-body grasp completion, functional grasping, and human-motion imitation.

Abstract: Dexterous grasping is usually conducted for specific tasks, leading to heterogeneous constraints such as specific approach directions, desired contact regions, specified wrist trajectories, and functional hand poses. Our previous work, GraspXL, achieves scalable grasping motion synthesis for diverse objects and hand morphologies, while lacking controllability for synthesis under such various task-driven constraints. In this paper, we propose ConGraspXL, which extends GraspXL with controllable constraint-conditioned grasp motion synthesis that accommodates diverse task-driven constraints and their combinations. We introduce a hierarchical constraint formulation, enable flexible constraint composition with a masked residual interface, and improve control precision with dynamic hand centers and feed-forward wrist guidance. Without losing the strong generalization capabilities of GraspXL, ConGraspXL enables precise and flexible controllability for various individual constraints and their combinations, providing a plug-and-play low-level grasp controller for downstream applications such as whole-body grasp completion, functional grasping, and human-motion imitation.

</details>


### [9] [ManiSkillFormer: Demonstration-Free Compositional Manipulation via Task-Conditioned Geometric Contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16331)
*Peiqi Yu,Mosam Dabhi,Shangtao Li,Bowei Li,Laszlo Jeni,Changliu Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ManiSkillFormer is a neuro-symbolic, demonstration-free framework for compositional robotic manipulation that uses task-conditioned geometric contracts to bridge perception and action, outperforming baselines and ablations across pick-and-place, functional, and long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end visuomotor policies require per-object fine-tuning and robot demonstrations, limiting composability and reuse. The authors aim for a demonstration-free approach that enables composable and reusable manipulation across objects and tasks.

Method: The framework introduces task-conditioned geometric contracts that explicitly structure the perception-action interface. Each skill declares semantic geometric primitives (e.g., object keypoints, surface normals). LLM agents generate these contracts and motion templates for different objects and task contexts. Contracts guide the perception module to ground task-relevant 3D primitives from observations, which instantiate reusable motion templates in a skill library.

Result: On a Galaxea R1-Lite dual-arm robot, ManiSkillFormer exceeds baselines and two ablated pipelines: 88.24% success for zero-shot pick-and-place over 8 categories and 30 instances, 75.00% average success on functional manipulation (unscrewing, pouring, pressing, folding), and 50–80% completion on 3 long-horizon tasks.

Conclusion: The design enables composable and reusable manipulation across objects and tasks without per-object policy fine-tuning or additional robot demonstrations.

Abstract: We present ManiSkillFormer, a neuro-symbolic framework for demonstration-free and compositional robotic manipulation. Instead of learning end-to-end visuomotor policies, ManiSkillFormer introduces task-conditioned geometric contracts that explicitly structure the interface between perception and action. Each manipulation skill declares the semantic geometric primitives required for execution, such as object keypoints and surface normals. Building on human-defined skill structures, LLM agents generate these contracts and corresponding motion templates for different objects and task contexts. These contracts guide the perception module to ground task-relevant 3D primitives from observations, which are then used to instantiate reusable motion templates stored in a skill library. We evaluate ManiSkillFormer on Galaxea R1-Lite dual-arm robot across three settings: zero-shot pick-and-place over 8 object categories with 30 different instances, functional manipulation tasks including unscrewing, pouring, pressing, and folding, and 3 long-horizon tasks. ManiSkillFormer achieves higher average success rates than the evaluated baselines and two ablated pipelines: 88.24% for demonstration-free pick-and-place, 75.00% average success on functional manipulation and 50--80% completion rates across the long-horizon tasks. These results show that our design enables composable and reusable manipulation across objects and tasks without per-object policy fine-tuning or additional robot demonstrations.

</details>


### [10] [Auto-HSI: Personalized human control of a robot swarm on demand by using LLMs for online automatic code generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16346)
*Alessandro Nazzari,Nathan Cerisara,Dorian Tonnis,Raina Zakir,Lorenzo Labarile,Weixu Zhu,Marco Dorigo,Mary Katherine Heinrich*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Auto-HSI generates personalized human-swarm interaction interfaces on demand: untrained operators describe desired collective behaviors in natural language and via gesture demonstrations, and the system auto-generates state-machine code that maps gestures to robot control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Untrained operators need intuitive, personalized ways to control robot swarms, but hand-coding bespoke gesture-driven interfaces is labor-intensive and inaccessible to non-programmers.

Method: Auto-HSI takes natural language descriptions and gesture demonstrations as input and automatically generates personalized state-machine code. The prototype supports centralized control with one- and two-handed gestures for teleoperating motion, formation shape, and shape deformation. Gesture tracking and code generation were benchmarked, then the full prototype was tested in live experiments with real operators controlling 50 simulated robots in a physics-based simulator under nominal and noisy conditions, plus live-interface updates and real-robot operation.

Result: Benchmarks validated the gesture tracking and code generation components. In live trials, operators teleoperated 50 simulated robots to score a goal, traverse a maze requiring shape deformation, and score two simultaneous goals by splitting into groups. A real operator also made live updates to the personalized interface during operation (in simulation), and the system demonstrated live operation of real robots.

Conclusion: Auto-HSI enables untrained users to rapidly create, use, and modify personalized gesture-based swarm control interfaces, with demonstrated effectiveness across simulated and real-robot scenarios.

Abstract: This paper presents Auto-HSI, a method for generating personalized human-swarm interaction (HSI) interfaces on demand. The objective is to enable untrained operators to use natural language descriptions and gesture demonstrations to explain how they want the robots to collectively behave in response to their gestures. Based on these inputs, the code should automatically be generated for personalized state machines that will control the robots as desired, in response to the desired gesture inputs. In the developed Auto-HSI prototype, the generated code produces a personalized interface for centralized control using one- and two-handed gestures, enabling a user to teleoperate the robots' motion, formation shape, and shape deformation. We test the gesture tracking and code generation components of Auto-HSI against performance benchmarks. We then test the full Auto-HSI prototype in ``live'' operation experiments, in which real human operators centrally control 50 simulated robots in a physics-based simulator, under nominal and noisy conditions. In these experiments, robots are teleoperated to: score a goal, traverse a maze that requires shape deformation, and score two simultaneous goals by splitting into two groups. We also demonstrate a real human operator making live updates to their personalized Auto-HSI interface during operation (in simulation). Finally, we demonstrate live operation of real robots.

</details>


### [11] [UDAV: Uncertainty-Driven Adaptive VLM Waypoint Planner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16368)
*Ghazal Farhani,Shabnam Shabani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UDAV is an uncertainty-driven adaptive VLM waypoint planner that uses stochastic trajectory sampling, medoid selection, and dispersion-based uncertainty to improve UAV-guided UGV off-road navigation planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models can generate routes from aerial imagery for off-road navigation, but their predictions lack any indication of reliability, making it difficult to trust or selectively correct them.

Method: UDAV draws multiple stochastic trajectory predictions, selects their medoid as a self-consistent nominal route, and estimates predictive uncertainty from spatial dispersion. When maximum uncertainty across interior waypoints exceeds a threshold, a reconsideration stage is invoked; otherwise the medoid is returned directly.

Result: Evaluated on 400 held-out trajectory queries from two UAV flights, stochastic medoid selection reduced mean ADE from 147.4 pixels (deterministic) to 115.9 pixels. The full planner achieved mean ADE of 110.4 pixels, a 25.1% reduction, with valid trajectories for all queries. It also produced the lowest 90th- and 95th-percentile errors among all configurations, including a K=10 consensus baseline, reducing them from 225.3 and 326.0 pixels (K=5 medoid) to 199.0 and 290.8 pixels.

Conclusion: Stochastic VLM predictions provide both a stronger nominal route and an actionable uncertainty signal for selectively mitigating large planning errors in UAV-guided UGV navigation.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can generate routes directly from aerial imagery for off-road navigation, but their predictions provide no indication of reliability. We present UDAV, an Uncertainty-Driven Adaptive VLM Waypoint Planner for UAV-guided UGV navigation. UDAV draws multiple stochastic trajectory predictions, selects their medoid as a self-consistent nominal route, and estimates predictive uncertainty from their spatial dispersion. When the maximum uncertainty across interior waypoints exceeds a threshold, UDAV invokes a reconsideration stage; otherwise, it returns the medoid directly. We evaluate UDAV on 400 held-out trajectory queries from two UAV flights. Stochastic medoid selection reduces the mean average displacement error (ADE) from 147.4 pixels for a deterministic prediction to 115.9 pixels. The complete planner achieves a mean ADE of 110.4 pixels, a 25.1% reduction relative to deterministic planning, while producing valid trajectories for all queries. UDAV also yields the lowest 90th- and 95th-percentile errors among all evaluated configurations, including a higher-budget K=10 consensus baseline. Relative to the K=5 medoid, UDAV reduces these errors from 225.3 and 326.0 pixels to 199.0 and 290.8 pixels, respectively. These results demonstrate that stochastic VLM predictions provide both a stronger nominal route and an actionable uncertainty signal for selectively mitigating large planning errors.

</details>


### [12] [Geometry vs Structure: Graph-Based Diagnostics for LiDAR Point-Cloud Simulation Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16378)
*Ghazal Farhani,Taufiq Rahman*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a graph-based framework using Louvain community detection and a bounded graph-spectral metric r_λ to evaluate structural fidelity of simulated LiDAR point clouds, complementing geometric metrics like Chamfer distance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital twins are scalable, cost-effective tools for validating autonomous-driving and ADAS sensor pipelines, but quantifying their fidelity is hard, especially for 3D LiDAR point clouds. Conventional geometric metrics such as Chamfer distance overlook connectivity, topology, and object-level organization.

Method: Graphs are built from real and simulated point clouds; Louvain community detection identifies spatially coherent subgraphs, which are matched via centroid proximity. For each matched pair, r_λ, a bounded graph-spectral metric motivated by Weyl's inequality, is computed and compared with density-aware Chamfer distance (CDC) as a geometric baseline. Controlled perturbations test invariance and sensitivity. Evaluation uses 50 paired real (Velodyne VLP-32C) and simulated (CARLA) LiDAR scans over four classes.

Result: r_λ is invariant to rigid transformations, robust to sensor noise, and sensitive to structural deformation. The dataset yields over 1,000 matched communities across vehicles, vegetation, trees, and building walls. Geometric and structural measures capture complementary aspects of simulation fidelity.

Conclusion: Graph-spectral analysis provides an additional diagnostic layer for validating digital twins in ADAS and autonomous-driving applications, complementing conventional geometric metrics.

Abstract: Digital twins provide a scalable and cost-effective complement to real-world testing for validating autonomous-driving and advanced driver-assistance system (ADAS) sensor pipelines. However, quantifying their fidelity remains challenging, particularly for 3D LiDAR point clouds, where conventional geometric metrics may overlook important structural discrepancies. We present a graph-based framework for evaluating the structural fidelity of simulated LiDAR point clouds against real-world scans. While scan-level metrics such as Chamfer distance capture point-wise geometric similarity, they do not explicitly represent connectivity, topology, or object-level organization. Our framework constructs graphs from real and simulated point clouds, applies Louvain community detection to identify spatially coherent subgraphs, and matches corresponding communities using centroid proximity. For each matched pair, we compute $r_λ$, a bounded graph-spectral metric motivated by Weyl's inequality, and compare it with density-aware Chamfer distance (CDC) as a geometric baseline. Controlled perturbation experiments demonstrate that $r_λ$ is invariant to rigid transformations and robust to sensor noise while remaining sensitive to structural deformation. We evaluate the framework on 50 paired real and simulated LiDAR scans acquired using a Velodyne VLP-32C sensor and CARLA, respectively. The dataset contains more than 1,000 matched communities across four representative classes: vehicles, vegetation, trees, and building walls. The results show that geometric and structural measures capture complementary aspects of simulation fidelity, supporting graph-spectral analysis as an additional diagnostic layer for validating digital twins in ADAS and autonomous-driving applications.

</details>


### [13] [Collision-Aware Humanoid Whole-Body Control under Imperfect Tracking Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16405)
*Mohitvishnu S. Gadde,Ashish Malik,Pranay Dugar,Aayam Kumar Shrestha,Alan Fern*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RECAL is a Robot-Environment Cross-Attention Layer that wraps a blind whole-body controller (WBC) to enable collision-aware tracking of floating-base, end-effector, and held-object commands using external scene geometry, validated in simulation and on a real Digit V3 humanoid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most whole-body controllers track motion targets while maintaining balance but are blind to scene geometry, so geometrically unsafe targets arising from perception, planning, or teleoperation errors can cause collisions.

Method: RECAL wraps a blind WBC and trades off target tracking against collision avoidance using external scene geometry. It represents the robot, held objects, and environment as point clouds and applies cross-attention between robot/object points and environment points to produce geometry-aware control features. It supports collision-aware tracking of floating-base and end-effector commands, including avoidance for held objects.

Result: In simulation, RECAL improves collision avoidance while preserving target-tracking performance across frozen-arm and adaptive-arm locomotion, object-carrying, and standing-manipulation scenarios compared to alternative geometry-aware WBC architectures. It is also demonstrated on a real Digit V3 humanoid robot.

Conclusion: RECAL effective
ly augments a blind WBC with scene-geometry awareness via cross-attention, enabling collision-aware whole-body control for humanoids both in simulation and on real hardware.

Abstract: Humanoid robots often execute motion commands through whole-body controllers (WBCs) that track targets while maintaining balance and stability. However, most WBCs are blind to scene geometry, which can lead to collisions from imperfect target motions that are geometrically unsafe due to perception, planning, or teleoperation errors. We propose RECAL, a Robot--Environment Cross-Attention Layer that wraps a blind WBC to trade off target tracking against collision avoidance using external scene geometry. RECAL supports collision-aware tracking of floating-base and end-effector commands, including collision avoidance for held objects. It represents the robot, held objects, and environment as point clouds, using cross-attention between robot/object points and the environment to produce geometry-aware control features. In simulation, RECAL improves collision avoidance while preserving target-tracking performance across frozen-arm and adaptive-arm locomotion, object-carrying, and standing-manipulation scenarios relative to alternative geometry-aware WBC architectures. We further demonstrate the controller on a real Digit V3 humanoid robot.

</details>


### [14] [XRoboToolKit-T: Teleoperation with High Stability and Precision with Tactile Sensing for Contact-rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16437)
*Xiwen Dengxiong,Xueting Wang,Ke Jing,Rui Li,Yunbo Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces XRoboToolKit-T, a teleoperation system with tactile-driven assistance that enables stable, high-frequency contact-rich manipulation and improves data collection efficiency over tactile-free state-of-the-art teleoperation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collecting high-quality robot data for contact-rich manipulation is essential for real-world skill acquisition, but existing data collection solutions often fail to provide stable, high-frequency tactile feedback, limiting their effectiveness in contact-rich scenarios.

Method: The system uses a tactile-informed force control architecture comprising two modules: a stabilizer haptic module that rapidly analyzes normal force distribution and infers pseudo shear force for real-time tactile-based assistance, and a refiner haptic module that integrates a vision-language-action model to predict and refine manipulation actions from tactile sensing data and task descriptions.

Result: Applied to challenging tasks—grasping a deformable rubber pipette for liquid transfer and inserting a medical syringe into a vascular training pad—the system achieved higher data collection efficiency and improved manipulation stability compared to state-of-the-art teleoperation without tactile assistance.

Conclusion: Tactile-informed force control in teleoperation enables high-frequency, stable contact-rich manipulation, making it a more effective solution for collecting high-quality robot data in contact-rich tasks.

Abstract: Collecting high-quality robot data for contact-rich manipulation tasks is essential for enabling robots to acquire real-world skills. However, existing data collection solutions often lack the capability to obtain stable and high-frequency tactile feedback, limiting their effectiveness in contact-rich manipulation scenarios. In this work, we propose a versatile teleoperation system with tactile-driven assistance to enable high-frequency and stable contact-rich manipulation. The proposed XRoboToolKit-T teleoperation system incorporates a tactile-informed force control architecture, designed to ensure both stable and precise force control in contact-rich manipulation during teleoperation. The stabilizer haptic module rapidly analyzes the normal force distribution and infers pseudo shear force, enabling real-time tactile-based assistance during manipulation. The refiner haptic module integrates a vision-language-action model to predict and refine manipulation actions based on tactile sensing data and task descriptions. We apply the proposed teleoperation system to challenging contact-rich manipulation tasks, including grasping a deformable rubber pipette for liquid transfer and inserting a medical syringe into a vascular training pad, to demonstrate the effectiveness of tactile-informed force control. Furthermore, the system achieves higher data collection efficiency and improved manipulation stability compared to state-of-the-art teleoperation without tactile assistance.

</details>


### [15] [The Neverwhere Visual Parkour Benchmark Suite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16443)
*Ziyu Chen,Henghui Bao,Haoran Chang,Alan Yu,Ran Choi,Kai McClennen,Gio Huh,Kevin Yang,Ri-Zhao Qiu,Yajvan Ravan,John J. Leonard,Xiaolong Wang,Phillip Isola,Ge Yang,Yue Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents the Neverwhere Benchmark Suite, a set of over sixty hyper-photorealistic 3D Gaussian Splatting reconstructions of urban indoor and outdoor scenes, to close the train/evaluation gap for visual locomotion controllers and enable large-scale, reproducible robot evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art visual locomotion controllers handle increasingly complex visual environments, making it hard to evaluate their real-world performance before deployment. A gap exists between training and evaluation conditions.

Method: The authors develop a collection of hyper-photorealistic, closed-loop evaluation environments based on over sixty 3D Gaussian Splatting reconstructions of urban indoor and outdoor scenes. They also provide policy checkpoints trained over multiple Neverwhere scenes and evaluate them on novel scenes.

Result: Analysis of the trained policy checkpoints evaluated in novel scenes underscores the potential pitfalls of relying exclusively on 3D Gaussian-generated data for training, illustrating the necessity of sourcing diverse data to ensure performance.

Conclusion: The Neverwhere Benchmark Suite promotes large-scale, reproducible robot evaluation by easing the creation and integration of Gaussian splat-based reconstructions into simulated continuous testing setups. Code and data are available at the project page.

Abstract: State-of-the-art visual locomotion controllers are increasingly capable at handling complex visual environments, making evaluating their real-world performance before deployment increasingly difficult. This work intends to narrow this train/evaluation gap by developing a collection of hyper-photo-realistic, closed-loop evaluation environments - The Neverwhere Benchmark Suite - comprised of over sixty 3D Gaussian Splatting reconstructions of urban indoor and outdoor scenes. Our goal is to encourage large-scale and reproducible robot evaluation by making it easier to create and integrate Gaussian splats-based reconstructions into simulated continuous testing setups. We also underscore the potential pitfalls of relying exclusively on 3D Gaussian-generated data for training, by providing policy checkpoints trained over multiple Neverwhere scenes and their performance when evaluated in novel scenes. Our analysis illustrates the necessity of sourcing diverse data to ensure performance. Code and data are available on the project page: https://ziyc.github.io/neverwhere-bench/.

</details>


### [16] [Dense to MoE Adaptation for Compact Vision Language Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16503)
*Muchun Niu,Shuang Chen,Yuzhou Wu,Linfeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AdaDE converts dense FFN blocks into MoE layers and dynamically deactivates experts using router statistics derived during fine-tuning, retaining 95.1% success on LIBERO and 42.0% on RobotWin2.0 while deactivating 40% of LLM parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA policies are growing in parameter count, which makes deployment on resource-constrained robot platforms difficult. The goal is to reduce the LLM-side parameters retained in the deployed policy while preserving downstream task performance.

Method: AdaDE adapts selected dense feed-forward blocks into mixture-of-experts (MoE) layers and derives expert retention masks from router statistics during fine-tuning. The Dense2MoE conversion preserves the original dense FFN function at initialization, allowing expert deactivation to begin without a separate recovery stage. Expert masks are updated dynamically from router usage statistics rather than a fixed shutdown rule, with staged training and expert protection to prevent early collapse.

Result: With 40% of LLM parameters deactivated, AdaDE retains 95.1% average success on LIBERO and 42.0% average success across all 50 RobotWin2.0 tasks.

Conclusion: Dense-to-MoE adaptation with dynamic expert deactivation is a practical direction for reducing active VLA model size without severe performance loss.

Abstract: Vision language action (VLA) policies continue to grow in parameter count, making deployment on resource-constrained robot platforms difficult. The central goal is to reduce the number of LLM-side parameters retained in the deployed policy while preserving downstream task performance. Our approach, AdaDE, adapts selected dense feed forward blocks into mixture of experts (MoE) layers and derives expert retention masks from router statistics during fine tuning. The Dense2MoE conversion preserves the original dense FFN function at initialization, so expert deactivation can start without a separate recovery stage. Instead of using a fixed shutdown rule, expert masks are updated dynamically from router usage statistics, with staged training and expert protection to avoid early collapse. With 40% of the LLM parameters deactivated, AdaDE retains 95.1% average success in LIBERO and 42.0% average success across all 50 RobotWin2.0 tasks. These results suggest that dense to MoE adaptation with dynamic expert deactivation is a practical direction for reducing active VLA model size without severe performance loss.

</details>


### [17] [UniDex-ViTac: Learning Unified Visuo-Tactile Dexterous Manipulation Policy from Human Video Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16504)
*Hyesung Lee,Si-Hwan Heo,Sungwook Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UniDex-ViTac learns a deployable visuo-tactile dexterous manipulation policy from human-video-guided simulation, pairing simulated robot rollouts with fingertip contact observations to train a single ACT-based generalist that transfers to real robots without real demonstrations or fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human videos offer rich dexterous manipulation demonstrations but lack robot-executable actions and tactile sensing, creating a gap for training deployable robot policies with contact feedback.

Method: Object-specific residual RL specialists adapt annotated human-object interaction references to a robotic arm-hand system; their successful rollouts pair final robot action targets with robot-side fingertip contact observations. From 50 human demonstrations over ten objects, 10,000 simulated trajectories train a single ACT-based generalist using point clouds, proprioception, and four binary contact signals encoded via fingertip labels plus a separate token, requiring no human references or privileged object identity/pose at deployment.

Result: Contact-augmented configuration reaches 68.3% macro-average success in simulation vs. 55.5% for a point-cloud-only baseline. Without real-robot demos or fine-tuning, it succeeds in 73/110 physical trials (66.4%) across six seen and five unseen objects, vs. 60/110 (54.5%) for the baseline, an 11.8 percentage-point gain.

Conclusion: Results support the feasibility of learning a unified visuo-tactile dexterous manipulation policy from video-guided simulated interactions.

Abstract: Human videos provide demonstrations of dexterous manipulation but lack robot-executable actions and tactile measurements. We present UniDex-ViTac, a framework that uses human-video-guided simulation to generate robot demonstrations paired with fingertip contact observations for training a deployable visuo-tactile policy. Object-specific residual reinforcement learning specialists adapt annotated human-object interaction references to a robotic arm-hand system. Their successful rollouts pair final robot action targets with robot-side fingertip contact observations. From 50 human demonstrations across ten objects, we collect 10,000 simulated trajectories to train a single Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) based generalist. The policy combines point clouds, proprioception, and four binary contact signals encoded through fingertip labels and a separate token, without requiring human references or privileged object identity and pose at deployment. The contact-augmented configuration achieves 68.3% macro-average success in simulation, compared with 55.5% for the point-cloud-only baseline. Without real-robot demonstrations or policy fine-tuning, it succeeds in 73/110 physical trials (66.4%) across six seen and five unseen objects, compared with 60/110 (54.5%) for the baseline, an increase of 11.8 percentage points. These results support the feasibility of learning a unified visuo-tactile dexterous manipulation policy from video-guided simulated interactions. Project page: https://unidex-vitac.github.io/

</details>


### [18] [ProxiDex: Learning Dynamics-Guided Proximity Policy for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16586)
*Yushan Bai,Boyu Zheng,Zhiyang Mao,Hongzheng Sun,Yuchuang Tong,En Li,Zhengtao Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ProxiDex is a dynamics-guided proximity policy framework that treats hand-object proximity as an interaction state to enable robust multi-finger dexterous manipulation under partial observability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous manipulation depends on stable hand-object interactions, but these are only partially observable: vision is occluded by the hand, tactile sensing adds hardware-specific modalities and calibration burdens, and existing policies rarely model how these cues evolve under actions, causing brittleness under contact uncertainty.

Method: ProxiDex reconstructs interaction point clouds and converts geometric distances into proximity cues, yielding a hardware-agnostic contact representation that also supplies immersive feedback in VR teleoperation. It learns action-conditioned proximity dynamics via a coupled forward-inverse design: future observation latents are predicted from actions, and proximity variations are decoded from latent changes. These dynamics then adaptively reweight proximity tokens across manipulation phases and provide dynamics-consistency supervision for policy inference.

Result: Simulation and real-world experiments show improved success rates and robustness over representative baselines across standard, unseen-object, and perturbation scenarios.

Conclusion: Modeling hand-object proximity as an interaction state with action-conditioned dynamics offers a hardware-agnostic, occlu-sion-robust basis for dexterous manipulation policies, stabilizing action generation under unreliable visual feedback.

Abstract: Multi-finger dexterous manipulation relies on stable hand-object interactions, yet these interactions are partially observable in practice. Visual observations are often occluded by the hand, tactile sensors introduce hardware-specific modalities and calibration burdens, and existing policies rarely model how these cues evolve under actions, making them brittle under contact uncertainty. To address these, we present ProxiDex, a dynamics-guided proximity policy framework that treats hand-object proximity as an interaction state for dexterous manipulation. ProxiDex reconstructs interaction point clouds and converts geometric distances into proximity cues, forming a hardware-agnostic contact representation that provides immersive feedback during VR teleoperation. Built on this representation, ProxiDex learns action-conditioned proximity dynamics with a coupled forward-inverse design: future observation latents are predicted from actions, while proximity variations are decoded from latent changes. Leveraging these dynamics, ProxiDex adaptively reweights proximity tokens across manipulation phases and uses dynamics-consistency supervision to guide policy inference, stabilizing action generation under unreliable visual feedback. Simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate improved success rates and robustness over representative baselines across standard, unseen objects, and perturbation scenarios. Additional visualizations are available at https://proxidex.github.io/.

</details>


### [19] [Learning to Optimize UAV Path Planning for Data Sensing in Wireless Sensor Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16629)
*Sijie Ma,Zeyuan Ma,Weijia Cao,Yue-Jiao Gong,Lingling Ma,Zhiyang Huang,Jun Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes Landscape-Aware Meta Differential Evolution (LAMDE), a learning-assisted bi-level framework for UAV path planning in WSN data collection, combining meta-learned configuration policies, landscape-aware data augmentation, and variable-length Differential Evolution encoding to achieve state-of-the-art performance on complex constrained tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UAV path planning approaches for WSN data sensing suffer from two limitations: (i) they are primarily hand-crafted with design biases that impair adaptation to unseen tasks, and (ii) they assume idealized spatial complexities via simplified simulation, leading to underperformance in real-world deployment. The authors aim to overcome these shortcomings.

Method: The authors re-formulate the UAV path planning problem to incorporate challenging constraints and propose a bi-level learning-to-optimize approach. The meta-level is a trainable algorithm configuration policy that meta-learns an adaptable planning strategy for the low-level planner. A landscape-aware automatic augmentation scheme enriches training data to address data scarcity and distribution shift. At the low level, a Differential Evolution algorithm solves the planning tasks, enhanced by a variable-length encoding strategy that dynamically prunes redundant hover points and optimizes continuous flight parameters within a unified search space.

Result: Comprehensive experiments comparing LAMDE against representative baselines show that it achieves state-of-the-art performance on the tested complex UAV path planning tasks in WSN data collection scenarios.

Conclusion: LAMDE effectively addresses hand-crafted design bias and idealized-simulation limitations through meta-learning, landscape-aware augmentation, and flexible variable-length Differential Evolution encoding, delivering superior performance on complex constrained UAV path planning tasks.

Abstract: UAVs have emerged as highly flexible platforms for data sensing in Wireless Sensor Networks (WSNs). Path planning for UAVs in such tasks plays a key role to assure remote sensing effectiveness and friendly energy consumption. However, existing approaches show two key limitations: i) they are primarily hand-crafted with certain design biases that harm adaptation on unseen tasks. ii) they predominantly assume idealized spatial complexities of actual environments through simplified simulation, causing them to underperform during real-world deployment. In this paper, we propose a novel learning-assisted planning framework, termed Landscape-Aware Meta Differential Evolution (LAMDE), to tackle the mentioned limitations. The major contributions come from the following aspects. We first re-formulate such UAV path planning problem to embrace challenging constraints. To efficiently navigate this highly constrained space, we propose a bi-level learning to optimize approach, where the meta-level is a trainable algorithm configuration policy that meta-learns an adaptable planning strategy for low-level planning algorithm. To address the potential training data scarcity and distribution shift in real-world environments, we introduce a landscape-aware automatic augmentation scheme that enriches training data. At the low-level, a Differential Evolution algorithm is deployed for solving the path planning tasks. To enhance the solving flexibility, we further design a variable-length encoding strategy that dynamically prunes redundant hover points and optimizes continuous flight parameters concurrently within a unified search space. Based on all proposed designs, we meta-train LAMDE and compare it with representative baselines. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that LAMDE achieves state-of-the-art performance on the tested complex UAV path planning tasks in WSN data collection scenarios.

</details>


### [20] [SAVLA: Symmetry-Aware Vision-Language-Action Models for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16641)
*Junle Li,Weixian Waylon Li,Fuxiang Wu,Fusheng Hao,Fengxiang He*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SAVLA is an end-to-end symmetry-aware VLA model that keeps the vision-language backbone frozen and adds an equivariant flow-matching action head plus a learned canonicalizer, improving robustness and data efficiency on LIBERO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action (VLA) models dominate language-conditioned robot manipulation, but they acquire spatial competence purely from demonstrations even though images and language inherently encode geometric information. Consequently, they are reliable only within the range of scene poses covered by demonstrations, limiting robustness.

Method: SAVLA keeps the pretrained vision-language backbone entirely frozen and combines it with (1) an equivariant flow-matching action head that decomposes state, action, and conditioning inputs into invariant and equivariant channels and preserves this typing throughout all layers, and (2) a learned canonicalizer that transforms oblique-view images into a canonical frame and consistently rotates the geometric conditions.

Result: On LIBERO, compared with the GR00T N1.5 baseline, SAVLA improves the success rate averaged over all four LIBERO suites by 5.1 points and raises the mean success rate under rotation on LIBERO-Goal from 41.5% to 90.4%.

Conclusion: Incorporating symmetry-awareness through an equivariant action head and learned canonicalizer into a frozen-backbone VLA yields more robust and data-efficient policy learning, particularly under scene-pose variation such as rotation.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have become the dominant paradigm for language-conditioned robot manipulation. However, although images and language instructions inherently encode geometric information, VLAs acquire their spatial competence purely from demonstrations. As a result, they are reliable only within the range of scene poses that the demonstrations cover. We propose SAVLA, an end-to-end symmetry-aware VLA model for robust and data-efficient policy learning. Our approach keeps the pretrained vision-language backbone entirely frozen while combining it with an equivariant flow-matching action head and a learned canonicalizer. The head decomposes its state, action, and conditioning inputs into invariant and equivariant channels, and preserves this typing throughout all of its layers. The canonicalizer transforms oblique-view images into a canonical frame and rotates the geometric conditions consistently. We evaluate our model on LIBERO. Compared with the GR00T N1.5 baseline, SAVLA improves the success rate averaged over all four LIBERO suites by 5.1 points and increases the mean success rate under rotation on LIBERO-Goal from 41.5% to 90.4%.

</details>


### [21] [WholeBodyWAM: Generalizing Pre-trained World-Action Priors to Humanoid Loco-Manipulation via WBC-Grounded Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16644)
*Zhuo Li,Yiming Yao,Jim Tan,Mengjie Jing,Zhipeng Dong,Fei Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WholeBodyWAM extends world-action models to humanoid loco-manipulation by jointly predicting visual dynamics, manipulation actions, and whole-body control intents, achieving 91.9% simulated success and improved real-world generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most World Action Model (WAM) research targets tabletop or arm-centric manipulation, leaving humanoid loco-manipulation underexplored. A method is needed to leverage pre-trained world-action priors for generalizable whole-body humanoid control.

Method: WholeBodyWAM jointly predicts future visual dynamics, manipulation actions, and whole-body control intents. It preserves pre-trained world-action priors while grounding heterogeneous whole-body controller (WBC) semantics and coordinating whole-body behavior, rather than relearning whole-body behavior from scratch.

Result: Overall simulation task success rate of 91.9%; real-world out-of-distribution task progress improved by 0.23; 70% reduction in success-rate variance across WBCs versus baselines.

Conclusion: Extending pre-trained world-action priors via structured WBC grounding and coordination offers a scalable path toward humanoid whole-body intelligence.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) offer a promising approach to general-purpose robot manipulation by jointly modeling visual dynamics and actions. However, most WAM studies focus on tabletop or arm-centric manipulation, while humanoid loco-manipulation remains less explored. To address this gap, we introduce WholeBodyWAM, which jointly predicts future visual dynamics, manipulation actions, and whole-body control intents for generalizable humanoid loco-manipulation. It preserves pre-trained world-action priors while grounding heterogeneous whole-body controller (WBC) semantics and coordinating whole-body behavior. Extensive experiments show that WholeBodyWAM achieves an overall simulation task success rate of 91.9%, with a 0.23 improvement in real-world out-of-distribution task progress and a 70% reduction in success-rate variance across WBCs relative to the respective baselines. These results suggest a path toward scalable humanoid whole-body intelligence by extending pre-trained world-action priors through structured WBC grounding and coordination, rather than relearning whole-body behavior from scratch. Project page: https://wholebodywam.github.io/.

</details>


### [22] [Weave: Learning Whole-Body Dexterous Loco-Manipulation from Human-Object Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16683)
*Liu Cao,Xingze Wu,Jingzhi Cui,Botian Xu,Mingzhi Pei,Ruoqu Chen,Mengdi Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Weave is a unified framework that learns whole-body dexterous humanoid-object interaction from captured human demonstrations via contact-aware retargeting, approach-motion completion, and a contact- and geometry-aware policy controlling 29 body joints and 12 finger joints, achieving 92.5% success on trained interactions and 65.0% on unseen sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid-object interaction requires coordinating whole-body balance, locomotion, and dexterous hand contact to control both robot and object motion. While human demonstrations provide examples of coordinated interaction, transferring these behaviors to humanoid robots is challenging because effective contacts must be established and maintained under different embodiments and dynamics.

Method: Weave converts captured human-object interactions into executable robot-object references through contact-aware retargeting and approach-motion completion. Its core is a contact- and geometry-aware policy that jointly commands 29 body joints and 12 actuated finger joints across multiple objects and interaction sequences.

Result: Evaluation across nine objects yields a 92.5% success rate on trained interactions and, without additional training, 65.0% on sequences never seen during training. The authors additionally release ~9,000 physically executed rollouts spanning ~23 hours, providing robot-object trajectories with contact annotations.

Conclusion: Weave provides a unified framework for learning whole-body dexterous humanoid-object interaction from human demonstrations, demonstrating strong performance on trained interactions and generalization to unseen sequences. The released dataset supports downstream interaction-policy learning and physically consistent HOI motion generation.

Abstract: Learning humanoid-object interaction requires coordinating whole-body balance, locomotion, and dexterous hand contact to control both robot and object motion. Human demonstrations provide examples of coordinated interaction, but transferring these behaviors to humanoid robots requires learning how to establish and maintain effective contacts under different embodiments and dynamics. We present Weave, a unified framework for learning whole-body dexterous humanoid-object interaction from captured human demonstrations. Weave first converts captured human-object interactions into executable robot-object references through contact-aware retargeting and approach-motion completion. At its core is a contact- and geometry-aware policy that jointly commands 29 body joints and 12 actuated finger joints across multiple objects and interaction sequences. Evaluation across nine objects yields a 92.5% success rate on trained interactions and, without any additional training, 65.0% on sequences never seen during training. We additionally release ~9,000 physically executed rollouts spanning ~23 hours, providing robot-object trajectories with contact annotations for downstream interaction-policy learning and physically consistent HOI motion generation. Project website: https://xiaohu-art.github.io/Weave/

</details>


### [23] [Differentiable Mesh State Estimation via Factor Graph Inference for Deformable Object Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16686)
*Lidia Al-Zogbi,Fangjie Li,Samuel Tobin,James Ferguson,Nithesh Kumar,Alejandro Chara,Kuan-I Chung,Mingxing Rao,Ayberk Acar,Susheela Sharma Stern,Robert Webster,Daniel Moyer,Alan Kuntz,Caleb Rucker,Tucker Hermans,Jie Ying Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces a factor graph-based probabilistic framework for estimating the states of deformable objects by directly updating a tetrahedral mesh, combining physics priors, noisy sensor measurements, and temporal smoothness constraints in a unified nonlinear least-squares optimization solved via Levenberg-Marquardt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating deformable object states remains a fundamental challenge in robotics and simulation. Existing approaches may lack a principled, measurement-driven way to update physically-grounded mesh representations under deformation.

Method: A factor graph-based framework performs probabilistic mesh state estimation. It directly updates a tetrahedral mesh—a rich, physically-grounded environment representation—by fusing physics priors, noisy sensor measurements, and temporal smoothness constraints. The estimation is posed as a nonlinear least-squares problem and solved with Levenberg-Marquardt.

Result: Ex vivo central-airway obstruction experiments and simulations on deforming cube models show reliable and accurate reconstruction under both rigid motion and deformation.

Conclusion: The results highlight the potential of this probabilistic approach for principled, measurement-driven mesh state estimation in deformable object reconstruction.

Abstract: Estimating deformable object states remains a fundamental challenge in robotics and simulation. We propose a novel factor graph-based framework for probabilistic mesh state estimation of deformable objects. The method directly updates a tetrahedral mesh, a rich and physically-grounded representation of an environment, by combining physics priors, noisy sensor measurements, and temporal smoothness constraints within a unified probabilistic formulation. The estimation problem is posed as a nonlinear least-squares optimization and solved using Levenberg-Marquardt. Ex vivo central-airway obstruction experiments and simulations on deforming cube models demonstrate reliable and accurate reconstruction under both rigid motion and deformation, highlighting the potential of this probabilistic approach for principled, measurement-driven mesh state estimation in deformable object reconstruction.

</details>


### [24] [IL-ACT: Imitation Learning with Adaptive Cartesian Tracking Control for a 30-ton Excavator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16696)
*Mehdi Heydari Shahna,Seihun Kim,Soyi Jung,Soohyun Park,Jouni Mattila,Joongheon Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IL-ACT combines an anchored 14-input imitation policy with adaptive Cartesian feedback and a stopping-distance governor to control a 30-ton excavator, outperforming baselines with lower errors and faster goal completion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous excavator control is difficult due to coupled kinematics, actuation lag, and uncertainty, which challenge conventional control and pure imitation approaches.

Method: IL-ACT uses an anchored 14-input imitation policy pretrained on operator demonstrations to generate nominal joint rates. Adaptive Cartesian feedback plus gated gain/bias estimation correct these commands, then a stopping-distance governor constrains joint-reference generation. Evaluation is done in Simscape over 100 sequential goals and spiral, figure-eight, and rounded-raster tracking, with 88 additional runs across three seeds, two initializations, and speeds under varying hydraulic and sensing conditions.

Result: IL-ACT completes all goals faster with lower terminal errors than Teacher+ACT. Telemetry-initialized IL-ACT lowers RMSE in all 24 figure-eight and rounded-raster seed comparisons and cuts additional-load spiral mean RMSE by about 29%. It also beats IL-only and PID on original spiral RMSE. Under shared sensor noise, telemetry-initialized IL-ACT achieves 27.67% lower mean RMSE than Teacher+ACT, and enabling estimation reduces mean RMSE by 22.44% versus a frozen estimator. Pretrained-weight effects are mixed, and the teacher comparison shows a spiral RMSE-maximum-error tradeoff.

Conclusion: The analysis establishes bounded adaptive states and Cartesian feedback, with reference admissibility conditional on governor feasibility.

Abstract: Autonomous excavator control is challenged by coupled kinematics, actuation lag, and uncertainty. We propose imitation learning and adaptive Cartesian tracking (IL-ACT), a novel motion control framework for a 30-ton-class excavator. An anchored, 14-input imitation policy pretrained on operator demonstrations generates nominal joint rates; adaptive Cartesian feedback and gated gain/bias estimation correct these commands before a stopping-distance governor constrains joint-reference generation. Simscape evaluation covers 100 sequential goals and spiral, figure-eight, and rounded-raster tracking, including 88 additional runs across three training seeds, two initializations, and speeds, under hydraulic response and sensing conditions. Compared with Teacher+ACT, IL-ACT completes all goals with shorter duration and lower terminal errors under both response conditions. Telemetry-initialized IL-ACT lowers RMSE in all 24 figure-eight and rounded-raster seed comparisons and lowers additional-load spiral mean RMSE by approximately 29%. Original spiral RMSE also improves over IL-only and PID. Under a shared sensor-noise realization, telemetry-initialized IL-ACT achieves 27.67% lower mean RMSE than Teacher+ACT; enabling estimation reduces mean RMSE by $22.44\%$ relative to the frozen estimator. Pretrained-weight effects remain mixed, and the original teacher comparison exhibits a spiral RMSE--maximum-error tradeoff. Analysis establishes bounded adaptive states and Cartesian feedback, with reference admissibility conditional on governor feasibility.

</details>


### [25] [World Models for Embodied Intelligence: From Plausible to Controllable to Actionable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16697)
*Nanjie Yao,Hao Wang,Chong Cheng,Zhikang Chen,Wenzhe Li,Jiafei Lyu,Li Shen,Peilin Zhao,Zongqing Lu,Gao Huang,Steven Hoi,Dacheng Tao,Deheng Ye*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This survey reframes world-model evaluation from visual fidelity toward behavior-relevant predictive capability, proposing three progressive levels—Plausible, Controllable, and Actionable—and a 3x4 capability-by-improvement-loop matrix to organize progress across embodied tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models in embodied intelligence are typically judged by visual fidelity, yet their real value lies in improving behavior. Anticipation in biological agents (e.g., shaping the hand before grasping a cup) is coarse and rarely pictorial but guides action. Existing surveys organized by architecture, output modality, or application domain leave implicit the central question of which predictive capabilities actually improve behavior.

Method: The authors introduce a capability hierarchy: Plausible models (preserve task-relevant temporal, geometric, or physical structure), Controllable models (additionally predict how interventions alter that structure), and Actionable models (translate predictions into measurable gains in planning, action, learning, evaluation, verification, recovery, or data selection). They complement this with a 3x4 matrix crossing geometry, physics, and action grounding against improvement loops centered on data, rewards, policies, and the model itself. Using this framework, they survey manipulation, navigation, locomotion, autonomous driving, and general embodied learning, tracing technical progressions and examining datasets, benchmarks, and evaluation protocols.

Result: The survey identifies key challenges in long-horizon consistency, uncertainty calibration, causal intervention testing, latency, verification and recovery, and cross-embodiment transfer. It also clarifies capability requirements and technical progressions across the surveyed embodied domains, and analyzes associated datasets, benchmarks, and evaluation protocols.
