Conclusion: The perspective shifts world-model evaluation away from visual plausibility toward whether predictions capture task-relevant state, reflect intervention effects, and improve the closed-loop behavior of embodied agents.

Abstract: World models connect perception and decision-making in embodied intelligence by maintaining hidden state, anticipating consequences, comparing interventions, and adapting when execution departs from expectations. Although progress is often measured by visual fidelity, their value lies in improving behavior. Before reaching for a cup, a person anticipates its weight and resistance to grasping, shaping the hand before contact. Such anticipation is coarse and rarely pictorial, yet it guides action. This raises a central question: which predictive capabilities improve behavior? Existing surveys, organized by architecture, output modality, or application domain, leave this question implicit. We introduce three progressively stronger capability levels: Plausible models preserve task-relevant temporal, geometric, or physical structure; Controllable models additionally predict how interventions alter that structure; and Actionable models translate predictions into measurable gains in planning, action, learning, evaluation, verification, recovery, or data selection. We complement this hierarchy with a 3 x 4 matrix crossing geometry, physics, and action grounding with improvement loops centered on data, rewards, policies, and the model itself. Using this framework, we survey manipulation, navigation, locomotion, autonomous driving, and general embodied learning, tracing technical progressions, clarifying capability requirements, and examining datasets, benchmarks, and evaluation protocols. We identify challenges in long-horizon consistency, uncertainty calibration, causal intervention testing, latency, verification and recovery, and cross-embodiment transfer. This perspective shifts evaluation from visual plausibility toward whether predictions capture task-relevant state, reflect intervention effects, and improve the closed-loop behavior of embodied agents.

</details>


### [26] [The Robot Data Factory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16705)
*Sami Haddadin,Ivan Laptev,Ian Reid,Dezhen Song,Cesare Stefanini,Abdalla Swikir,Xingxing Zuo,Lyes Saad Saoud,Mahmoud Hamandi,Mohamed Heshmat,Oualid Doukhi,Abdeldjallil Naceri,Attique Bashar,Abdelrahim Mohamed,Teodor Tomic,Yue Peng,Samuel Schneider,Cheng-Chung Lee,Janine Guo,Qinghao Zhang,Kim Jeffery*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Robot Data Factory (RDF), an infrastructure and methodology that treats robot *experience*—not raw data—as the defining resource of Physical AI, enabling a continuous Deploy-Measure-Learn-Repeat cycle for scalable, reproducible embodied intelligence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical AI needs more than ever-larger robot datasets; robots acquire knowledge through continuous interaction with the physical world. The authors argue that raw robot data alone is insufficient because it fails to preserve the perception-action-consequence loop. The true scientific resource must therefore be physically grounded robot experience.

Method: RDF organizes heterogeneous robots and environment-specific training grounds via reproducible missions, skill curricula, synchronized multimodal sensing, external ground truth, an agentic robot network, data pipelines, and living benchmarks. The authors formalize robot experience and its quality, introduce a mission-task-skill-episode-dataset-benchmark-capability hierarchy, and derive quantitative scaling laws plus an algorithmic synthesis procedure linking fleet size, sensor rates, storage, learning representations, tokenization, training compute, inference, and latency to Embodied-AI cluster requirements.

Result: The framework is instantiated in three complementary physical training grounds targeting domestic, environmental, and energy applications. RDF implements a closed Deploy-Measure-Learn-Repeat cycle in which validated physical experience supports world models, vision-language-action models, embodied policies, digital twins, and subsequent robot deployment.

Conclusion: RDF reframes robot data generation as a continuous scientific production process, offering a pathway toward reproducible, scalable, and eventually federated infrastructure for Physical AI.

Abstract: Physical AI requires more than increasingly large robot datasets: intelligent robots acquire knowledge through continuous interaction with the physical world. We argue that the defining scientific resource of Physical AI is therefore not raw robot data alone, but robot experience - physically grounded interaction whose observations, actions, embodiment, context, and outcomes preserve the perception-action-consequence loop. We introduce the Robot Data Factory (RDF), a mission-driven infrastructure and methodology for continuously generating, validating, benchmarking, and reusing such experience. RDF organizes heterogeneous robots and environment-specific training grounds through reproducible missions, skill curricula, synchronized multimodal sensing, external ground truth, an agentic robot network, data pipelines, and living benchmarks. Rather than treating datasets as static end products, RDF implements a closed Deploy-Measure-Learn-Repeat cycle in which validated physical experience supports world models, vision-language-action models, embodied policies, digital twins, and subsequent robot deployment. We further formalize robot experience and its quality, introduce a mission-task-skill-episode-dataset-benchmark-capability hierarchy, and derive quantitative scaling laws and an algorithmic synthesis procedure connecting robot fleet size, sensor rates, storage, learning representations, tokenization, training compute, inference, and latency to Embodied-AI cluster requirements. The framework is instantiated in three complementary physical training grounds for domestic, environmental, and energy applications. RDF thus reframes robot data generation as a continuous scientific production process and provides a pathway toward reproducible, scalable, and eventually federated infrastructure for Physical AI.

</details>


### [27] [CorrRisk-WM: Corridor-Conditioned Risk World Modeling for Safety-Critical Trajectory Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16724)
*Tingyu Guo,Reza Langari*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CorrRisk-WM is a planning-oriented partial world model that couples environment evolution with supervised intrusion and near-miss prediction over bounded candidate-trajectory corridors, achieving strong risk-prediction metrics and the lowest collision rate among baselines on the Waymo Open Motion dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safe local planning requires forecasting surrounding-agent motion and evaluating candidate-specific risks, because identical agent motion can pose different risks to different ego trajectories. The work addresses this by conditioning risk prediction on candidate ego trajectories rather than on a single fixed ego path.

Method: A latent environment model recursively predicts agent states and updates agent-agent and agent-map interactions. Each candidate trajectory queries the evolving environment via footprint-aware geometry and learned agent-corridor representations. A lightweight recurrent risk module uses temporal context to estimate per-slice hazards, and survival aggregation yields first-entry and horizon-level event probabilities. Training involves supervised intrusion and near-miss prediction over bounded candidate-trajectory corridors.

Result: On 29,176 scenarios from 100 Waymo validation shards, the model achieves intrusion AP of 0.8567 and 1-m near-miss first-entry AP of 0.8671. It attains the highest near-miss AP at all three distance thresholds and the lowest observed open-loop collision rate (4.88%), with route progress of 15.35 m. Ablations across three seeds show removing dynamic environment modeling or candidate-conditioned geometric interaction reduces mean intrusion AP from 0.8590 to 0.7624 and 0.7252, respectively.

Conclusion: The results support coupling environment evolution with candidate-conditioned geometric reasoning for risk prediction and safety-oriented candidate selection, with ablations confirming the contribution of both dynamic environment modeling and candidate-conditioned geometric interaction.

Abstract: Safe local planning requires forecasting surrounding-agent motion and evaluating candidate-specific risks, since identical agent motion can pose different risks to different ego trajectories. We present CorrRisk-WM, a planning-oriented partial world model coupling environment evolution with supervised intrusion and near-miss prediction over bounded candidate-trajectory corridors. A latent environment model recursively predicts agent states and updates agent-agent and agent-map interactions. Each candidate queries the evolving environment through footprint- aware geometry and learned agent-corridor representations. A lightweight recurrent risk module uses temporal context to estimate per-slice hazards; survival aggregation yields first-entry and horizon-level event probabilities. On 29,176 scenarios from 100 Waymo validation shards, CorrRisk-WM achieves intrusion average precision (AP) of 0.8567 and 1-m near-miss first-entry AP of 0.8671. In baseline comparisons, it attains the highest near-miss AP at all three distance thresholds and the lowest observed open-loop collision rate (4.88%), with route progress of 15.35 m. Across three seeds, removing dynamic environment modeling or candidate-conditioned geometric interaction reduces mean intrusion AP from 0.8590 to 0.7624 and 0.7252, respectively. These results support coupling environment evolution with candidate-conditioned geometric reasoning for risk prediction and safety-oriented candidate selection.

</details>


### [28] [Seeing What Matters: Visual Cue Guided Video Planning for Generalizable Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16737)
*Hojin Lee,Sizhe Lester Li,Maximilian Hilger,Susie Lu,Achim J. Lilienthal,Vincent Sitzmann,Daniel A. Duecker*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CueNav combines visual cue-guided video planning with an embodiment-specific Inverse-Dynamics Model (IDM) for robot navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative-video navigation methods rely on short-horizon guidance and recover waypoints via scene reconstruction, leaving longer-horizon planning and precise video-to-action translation underexplored.

Method: Use a BEV map as a visual cue encoding global task context and retain part of the robot body in the egocentric view to expose embodiment context; these cues guide the video planner, while an embodiment-specific IDM translates dense flow fields from the video plan into robot actions.

Result: Nearly 2x higher success in maze navigation vs. planning without the cue; 70% success in a narrow passage where baselines largely fail; zero-shot semantic-conditioned navigation and deployment of the same video planner across different robot platforms.

Conclusion: Visual cue-guided video planning with embodiment-specific action grounding enables generalizable navigation for longer-horizon planning and embodiment-aware control.

Abstract: Generative video models can serve as a promising backbone for robot navigation by predicting future observations as video plans. Recent approaches often condition video planning on short-horizon guidance and recover geometric waypoints through scene reconstruction, leaving longer-horizon planning and precise video-to-action translation less explored. We present CueNav, a video model-based navigation framework combining visual cue guided video planning with an embodiment-specific Inverse-Dynamics Model (IDM). As visual cues, we use a Bird's-Eye View (BEV) map to convey global task context and retain part of the robot body in the egocentric observation to expose embodiment context. These cues guide the video planner, while the IDM translates dense flow fields extracted from the video plan into robot actions. With the visual cue encoding global task context, CueNav achieves nearly 2x higher success in maze navigation than planning without the cue. The body-aware view with the IDM enables precise navigation with 70% success in a narrow passage where comparison methods largely fail to complete the task. We further demonstrate zero-shot semantic-conditioned navigation and deployment of the same video planner across different robot platforms. Our results show that visual cue-guided video planning with embodiment-specific action grounding paves the way toward a generalizable navigation framework for longer-horizon planning and embodiment-aware control. Additional results and code are available on our project website: https://cuenav.github.io.

</details>


### [29] [The Latent That Never Was: A Forensic Re-run of the CVAE Ablation in Action Chunking Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16745)
*Bo Kang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The claimed success-rate drop from removing the ACT encoder does not reproduce in re-runs of the original code; training length and checkpoint selection can flip which policy wins, and the sampled latent provides little reconstruction benefit on the tested benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original ACT paper reported that removing the CVAE encoder dropped mean success rate from 35% to 2% on two simulated tasks, a widely cited result. This paper questions whether that ablation finding depends on implementation details or the training data, and whether success rates alone are sufficient to justify the encoder's role.

Method: Re-run the encoder-removal ablation in the original ACT code and a second implementation; vary training length and checkpoint-selection criteria for evaluation; measure reconstruction benefit of the sampled latent across nonzero weights of the latent-information penalty; note that ACT sets the latent to zero at inference; time training throughput with and without the encoder.

Result: The published 35% to 2% drop does not reappear; smaller gains or losses remain uncertain. Training length and checkpoint selection can each reverse which policy scores higher, but the cause of the published drop is unidentified. On the tested benchmark, the sampled latent offers little reconstruction benefit at every tested nonzero penalty weight. Skipping the encoder increases training throughput in both implementations timed.

Conclusion: The original ACT encoder ablation is not reproducible in the authors' tests, and success rate is too coarse a metric to establish the encoder's utility. Since the latent is unused (set to zero) at inference and yields minimal reconstruction gain, skipping the encoder appears reasonable and also improves training throughput; code, evaluation tools and results are released for further replication.

Abstract: Action Chunking Transformers (ACT) are widely used to learn robot manipulation from demonstrations. Their conditional variational autoencoder includes an encoder meant to capture differences between demonstrations during training. The original ACT paper reported that encoder removal dropped the mean success rate from 35% to 2% on two simulated tasks with human demonstrations. We re-ran this ablation in the original code and checked whether the findings depend on the implementation or training data. The published drop does not reappear in our tests, although smaller gains or losses in success rate remain uncertain. To investigate the discrepancy, we varied training length and how checkpoints are selected for evaluation. Both can reverse which policy scores higher, but the published drop's cause remains unknown. Success rates alone leave open whether the encoder provides information that helps the policy reconstruct demonstrated actions. On the tested ACT benchmark, the sampled latent provides little reconstruction benefit at every tested nonzero weight of the penalty on latent information. At inference, ACT leaves this latent unused and sets it to zero. Skipping the encoder increases training throughput in both implementations we timed. We release code, evaluation tools and results so others can repeat the comparisons and test the encoder on other tasks.

</details>


### [30] [Optimal Excitation Trajectories for System Identification of Underwater Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16786)
*Fotis Panetsos,Kostas J. Kyriakopoulos*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A structured method designs optimal Bezier-based excitation trajectories for underwater vehicle system identification, maximizing data quality under safety constraints and validating parameter estimates via forward velocity prediction on unseen trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable estimation of an underwater vehicle's dynamic parameters is difficult because identification quality depends strongly on the excitation trajectory, and arbitrary motions may be unsafe or dynamically infeasible. A principled way is needed to generate trajectories that are both informative and physically safe.

Method: Trajectories are parameterized with Bezier curves to guarantee smooth, differentiable motion profiles and to enforce constraints by manipulating control points. An optimization problem is formulated to find a dynamically feasible excitation trajectory that respects safety limits while maximizing the quality of collected data, so vehicle dynamic parameters can be estimated reliably via least squares.

Result: The approach is experimentally validated in a laboratory water tank. Parameters identified from the optimized trajectory are assessed by predicting the vehicle's velocity through forward simulation on previously unseen trajectories.

Conclusion: The methodology yields optimal, safety-compliant excitation trajectories and demonstrates practical validity for underwater vehicle dynamic parameter identification.

Abstract: In this work, we propose a structured methodology for the system identification of underwater vehicles through the design of optimal excitation trajectories. To this end, the trajectories are parameterized using Bezier curves, which ensure smooth and differentiable motion profiles while facilitating the enforcement of constraints through appropriate manipulation of the control points. An optimization problem is formulated to determine a dynamically feasible excitation trajectory that respects safety limits and maximizes the quality of the collected data, thereby enabling reliable estimation of the vehicle's dynamic parameters using least squares. The proposed methodology is experimentally validated in a laboratory water tank, where the dynamic parameters, identified from the optimized trajectory, are evaluated by predicting the vehicle's velocity through forward simulation on previously unseen trajectories.

</details>


### [31] [Motion planning in high dimensional spaces hybridizing RRT and HAR via position-direction decoupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16810)
*Frederic Cazals,Nelson Feyeux*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes sampling-based path-planning methods that combine RRT and Hit-and-Run (HAR) random walks by decoupling the point to extend from its extension direction, and introduces a sparse-move strategy that moves only a fraction p_r of robots per step, scaling to 384 DOF and 64 robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exploring high-dimensional spaces is challenging, especially with narrow passages and small clearances. Classical RRT moves all degrees of freedom at each step and struggles in cluttered, high-dimensional multi-robot configurations.

Method: The authors decouple the point being extended from the direction of extension, coupling RRT with Hit-and-Run random walks. They show RRT and HAR are special cases of a generic algorithm combining separate biases for point and direction extension. They further introduce a sparse-move strategy moving only a fraction p_r of robots per step. Tests cover 3D piano mover problems and complex molecular systems of tens of rigid domains modeled as independent robots exploring SE(3)^N.

Result: Algorithms solve instances with up to 64 robots and 384 degrees of freedom within seconds on a standard laptop. The HARF method is up to two orders of magnitude faster than classical all-robot RRT when the latter succeeds, and still up to 2.4-fold faster on most instances when both use their best p_r.

Conclusion: HARF is recommended as the method of choice for complex multi-robot planning problems, offering substantial speedups and better scalability in high-dimensional, cluttered spaces.

Abstract: The exploration of high-dimensional spaces remains a challenging problem, in particular in the presence of narrow passages and small clearances. We propose novel sampling-based path-planning methods for high-dimensional spaces combining Rapidly-exploring Random Trees (RRT) and Hit-and-Run (HAR) random walks by decoupling the point being extended from the direction of extension. We also show that RRT and HAR appear as special cases of a generic algorithm coupling the biases used for the point and direction extension, respectively. We further study a sparse-move strategy in which only a fraction p_r of the robots is moved at each step, helping both RRT and the proposed HAR algorithms handle cluttered instances. Tests are presented for two families of models: classical piano mover problems in 3D, and complex molecular systems involving tens of rigid domains moving relatively to one another -- the latter viewed as independent robots exploring the motion space SE(3)N . Within seconds on a standard laptop, our algorithms solve instances with up to 64 robots and 384 degrees of freedom. We conclude by suggesting one of our methods, HARF, as the method of choice for complex multi-robot planning problems, being up to two orders of magnitude faster than the classical RRT moving all robots at each step--when it succeeds at all, and still up to 2.4 fold faster on most instances when both use their best p_r.

</details>


### [32] [Rethinking Visual Embodiment Dependence in Visuomotor Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16815)
*Hongjie Fang,Yuxuan Lu,Chenxi Wang,Haoxiang Qin,Shirun Tang,Zihao He,Shangning Xia,Jingjing Chen,Wanxi Liu,Shiquan Wang,Cewu Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Visuomotor policies use visible robot configuration as a shortcut to task progress (visual embodiment dependence, VED). Instead of removing VED, the authors structure it via embodiment canonicalization in 3D point clouds, replacing the robot with a canonical end-effector representation (CER), plus configuration-decorrelation augmentation, improving human-to-robot transfer without robot demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visuomotor policies observe both the task scene and the acting embodiment, so embodiment-specific visual cues can bias action prediction. The authors identify this as visual embodiment dependence (VED) and show via cue-conflict interventions that visible robot configuration can degenerate into a shortcut correlated with task progress, harming generalization. Rather than eliminating VED, they argue it should be structured around embodiment information that supports control and generalization.

Method: Embodiment canonicalization in 3D point clouds: the original embodiment is replaced by a canonical end-effector representation (CER) that preserves control-relevant geometry while abstracting embodiment-specific morphology. The editable CER form enables configuration-decorrelation augmentation for unfamiliar robot configurations.

Result: Embodiment canonicalization substantially improves human-to-robot policy transfer without robot demonstrations, whereas simply removing the embodiment is insufficient because control-relevant geometry is not preserved. CER itself can become a configuration shortcut when robot configuration is decoupled from task progress; configuration-decorrelation augmentation mitigates this failure mode and restores robust recovery without sacrificing performance on seen configurations.

Conclusion: Robust visuomotor learning benefits from structuring, rather than removing, visual embodiment information.

Abstract: Visuomotor policies observe both the task scene and the acting embodiment, allowing embodiment-specific visual cues to influence action prediction. We study this phenomenon as visual embodiment dependence (VED) and show, through cue-conflict interventions across representative policies, that visible robot configuration can become a shortcut to task progress. Rather than eliminating VED, we argue that it should be structured around embodiment information that supports control and generalization. We realize this through embodiment canonicalization in 3D point clouds, replacing the original embodiment with a canonical end-effector representation (CER) that preserves control-relevant geometry while abstracting embodiment-specific morphology. Its editable form further enables configuration-decorrelation augmentation for unfamiliar robot configurations. Experiments show that embodiment canonicalization substantially improves human-to-robot policy transfer without robot demonstrations, while simply removing the embodiment is insufficient without preserving control-relevant geometry. We further find that CER itself can become a configuration shortcut when robot configuration becomes decoupled from task progress; configuration-decorrelation augmentation mitigates this failure mode and restores robust recovery without sacrificing performance on seen configurations. Together, these results show that robust visuomotor learning benefits from structuring, rather than removing, visual embodiment information. Project website: https://tonyfang.net/ved

</details>


### [33] [TEMPO: Learning Temporal Context for Dynamic Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16864)
*Zhenyang Feng,Jimin Heo,Erik B. Sudderth,Unnat Jain*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TEMPO augments a pretrained VLA model with two temporal inputs—a motion summary from a frozen video foundation model and a compact proprioceptive history—to resolve motion ambiguity and state aliasing in dynamic manipulation, improving Bottle Handover success from 44% to 74% without backbone modification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models excel at quasi-static manipulation but fail at dynamic tasks because they act on a single observation at inference. The authors identify two representational failures: motion ambiguity (a single observation lacks scene dynamics, preventing anticipation of moving objects) and state aliasing (visually similar observations at different task stages require different actions). They argue these failures stem from missing temporal context, not limited model capacity or latency.

Method: TEMPO augments a pretrained VLA with two temporal inputs: (1) a motion summary extracted from a frozen video foundation model to resolve motion ambiguity, and (2) a compact proprioceptive history to resolve state aliasing. It requires no modification to the VLA backbone and adds minimal compute overhead at training or deployment.

Result: Across four dynamic manipulation tasks, TEMPO improves Bottle Handover success from 44% to 74% and is the only method that solves state aliasing. Probing and ablation studies confirm each temporal signal independently addresses its corresponding failure. The authors also release TEMPO-Bench, a benchmark of over 50k annotated frames for motion-aware robot perception evaluation in regression and multiple-choice formats.

Conclusion: The bottleneck for dynamic manipulation in VLA models is missing temporal context rather than model capacity. Providing complementary temporal signals—motion summary and proprioceptive history—efficiently resolves motion ambiguity and state aliasing, enabling strong dynamic manipulation performance without backbone changes.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have achieved impressive performance in quasi-static manipulation, but struggle in dynamic manipulation tasks because they operate on a single observation at inference time. We identify two representational failures that underlie this limitation. The first is motion ambiguity, where a single observation does not include scene dynamics and therefore cannot anticipate the future state of moving objects. The second is state aliasing, where visually similar observations from different points in a task require different actions. We argue that these failures persist regardless of model scale and inference latency, showing that the bottleneck is missing temporal context rather than model capacity. Based on this insight, we propose TEMPO, which augments a pretrained VLA with two temporal inputs: a motion summary extracted from a frozen video foundation model to resolve motion ambiguity and a compact proprioceptive history to resolve state aliasing. TEMPO requires no modification to the backbone and adds minimal compute overhead at training or deployment. Across four dynamic manipulation tasks, it improves Bottle Handover success from 44% to 74% and is the only method that solves state aliasing. Probing and ablation studies confirm that each temporal signal independently addresses its corresponding failure. We further release TEMPO-Bench, a benchmark of over 50k annotated frames for evaluating motion-aware robot perception in both regression and multiple-choice formats.
  Project Website: https://tempo-robot.github.io/

</details>


### [34] [Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Space Robot Operations: Technologies, Challenges and Prospects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16880)
*Zeyuan Huang,Gang Chen,Zixuan Hao,Guoqin Tang,Junyi Zong,Guoyou Ban,Jiale Wang,Haoyang Lv,Chaoqian Ren,Sitong Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This review surveys AI-enabled space robot operations (AI-SRO) through a capability-building lens, organizing the field into a three-layer framework—capability foundations, capability formation, and capability deployment/evolution—and outlining key future research directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Space robots are increasingly required to perform long-duration, contact-rich, multi-stage operations with minimal human intervention. While AI, robot learning, and embodied foundation models offer new potential for autonomy and adaptability, their transfer to space is hindered by scarce mission data, space-specific dynamics and sensing conditions, limited onboard resources, and stringent safety requirements—motivating a structured review of the field.

Method: The authors adopt a capability-building perspective. They first summarize representative operational scenarios, autonomy trends, and space-specific constraints. They then construct a three-layer technical framework: (1) capability foundations; (2) capability formation; and (3) capability deployment/evolution. Within this framework they review simulation environments, datasets and benchmarks; task/environment understanding, state perception, decision-making and planning, and action execution; and onboard deployment, ground-to-space adaptation, continual learning, and capability transfer.

Result: The review consolidates the AI-SRO landscape into a coherent three-layer taxonomy, mapping existing work in simulation, data/benchmarks, perception, decision-making, execution, and deployment/adaptation onto the proposed framework, thereby clarifying current capabilities and gaps.

Conclusion: The paper proposes key research directions toward trustworthy simulation and data, open-world multimodal cognition, long-horizon safe decision-making, physically constrained policy learning, and space computing infrastructures, aiming to guide future development of autonomous space robotic operations.

Abstract: Space robots are increasingly expected to perform long-duration, contact-rich, and multi-stage operations with limited human intervention. Recent advances in artificial intelligence (AI), robot learning, and embodied foundation models provide new opportunities to improve the autonomy and adaptability of such systems, but their transfer to space is constrained by scarce mission data, space-specific dynamics and sensing conditions, limited onboard resources, and stringent safety requirements. This article reviews artificial intelligence-enabled space robot operations (AI-SRO) from a capability-building perspective. We first summarize representative operational scenarios, autonomy trends, and space-specific constraints. We then establish a three-layer technical framework comprising capability foundations, capability formation, and capability deployment/evolution. Within this framework, we review simulation environments, datasets and benchmarks; task and environment understanding, state perception, decision-making and planning, and action execution; and onboard deployment, ground-to-space adaptation, continual learning, and capability transfer. Finally, we propose key research directions toward trustworthy simulation and data, open-world multimodal cognition, long-horizon safe decision-making, physically constrained policy learning, and space computing infrastructures.

</details>


### [35] [Waggle Dance Inspired Motion Communication for Multiple UAVs in MuJoCo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16958)
*Zhang Nengbo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A MuJoCo multi-observer extension of MoCom where one performer broadcasts a six-bit navigation message via dance-like flight primitives; 44 of 53 receiver exposures decoded correctly, supporting feasibility under ideal conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The honeybee waggle dance inspires a communication mechanism in which one agent's movement conveys spatial information guiding others' actions. Prior point-to-point motion communication (MoCom) does not address one-to-many broadcast with independent observers, so this work extends that setting to one performer and multiple observers.

Method: A MuJoCo system with one performer broadcasting a six-bit navigation payload using four flight primitives and explicit null signals. Each of one to five observers processes onboard RGB images, extracts optical-flow trajectories, recognizes symbols, parses the message, and begins navigation only after confirming its own complete frame. Reception states and execution triggers are independent per observer, while simulation control and safety checks rely on shared ground truth. Tests used stationary observers, 25 Hz image input, and ideal state-feedback control.

Result: On a fixed standard suite, 44 correct complete messages were obtained from 53 receiver exposures across 17 nominal broadcasts; 13 broadcasts passed all group-level decoding and execution checks. Three no-message or input-fault controls met expectations. A supplemental suite with frozen code at default geometry achieved 14 successful receiver exposures across three broadcasts. Near-range and wide-angle configurations exposed tracking and recognition failures, with unsuccessful receivers remaining stationary.

Conclusion: These finite simulation results support the feasibility of a waggle-dance-inspired broadcast-to-action mechanism under the tested conditions while identifying current perceptual and protocol limits.

Abstract: The honeybee waggle dance motivates a communication mechanism in which one agent's movement conveys spatial information that guides other agents' actions. This paper presents a MuJoCo system that extends the point-to-point motion communication setting of MoCom to one performer and multiple observers. A performer broadcasts a six-bit navigation payload using four flight primitives and explicit null signals. Each of one to five observers processes its own onboard RGB images, extracts optical-flow trajectories, recognizes symbols, parses the message, and starts navigation only after confirming its own complete frame. Reception states and execution triggers are separate across observers, while simulation control and safety checks use shared ground truth. With stationary observers, 25 Hz image input, and ideal state-feedback control, a fixed standard suite yielded 44 correct complete messages from 53 receiver exposures across 17 nominal broadcasts; 13 broadcasts passed all group-level decoding and execution checks. Three additional no-message or input-fault controls met their expected outcomes. A separately reported supplemental suite, using the same frozen code at the default geometry, achieved 14 successful receiver exposures across three broadcasts. Near-range and wide-angle configurations exposed tracking and recognition failures, while unsuccessful receivers remained stationary. These finite simulation results support the feasibility of a waggle-dance-inspired broadcast-to-action mechanism under the tested conditions and identify the present perceptual and protocol limits.

</details>


### [36] [Overcoming technical adoption barriers for mobile service robots in rehabilitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16996)
*Christian Sternitzke,Sebastian Blumenthal,Lukas Kleedoerfer,Verena Deserno,Anke Mayfarth*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper shows how a medical-device-approved mobile service robot advances from low to higher technology readiness levels through incremental peripheral functions and minor improvements derived from real-world clinical testing, rather than from ex-ante user questioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Much of the literature on healthcare robotic systems reports early-stage work at low technology readiness levels. However, achieving user acceptance and widespread adoption requires identifying downstream improvements that users cannot articulate before testing systems in clinical or operational environments. The paper is motivated by the need to understand how such peripheral functions and smaller refinements drive the transition to higher TRLs and acceptance.

Method: The paper presents a case-based account of a mobile service robot that has already been approved as a medical device. It examines how the robot reaches higher technology readiness levels by adding peripheral functions and making smaller improvements, drawing on insights obtained from testing in clinical settings and operational environments.

Result: Peripheral functions and smaller improvements, informed by real-world testing, prove pivotal for user acceptance in clinical environments. Certain aspects only emerge when systems are tested in clinical or operational settings and cannot be elicited by questioning users ex-ante. Such downstream developments can require significant implementation time.

Conclusion: Developers of service robots in healthcare are advised to plan for downstream developments—peripheral functions and minor refinements surfaced through clinical testing—because these are essential for user acceptance and widespread adoption, and they can take considerable implementation time.

Abstract: Many publications on robotic systems in healthcare describe early-stage work on low technology readiness levels. This paper describes how a mobile service robot approved as a medical device reaches higher technology readiness levels by adding peripheral functions and smaller improvements, which are pivotal for user acceptance in clinical environments and which often cannot be elicited by questioning users ex-ante as certain aspects only come in mind from testing the systems in clinical settings or operational environments. Especially developers of service robots in healthcare are advised to plan with such downstream developments, which can take significant implementation time, to obtain user acceptance and achieve widespread adoption of their robotic systems.

</details>


### [37] [SWIM: Vision-Language-Grounded Soft Whole-Body Interactive Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17035)
*Tingcong Liu,Aye Phyu Phyu Aung,Junjie Xiong,Siyi Ma,Bo An,Ke Wu,Senthilnath Jayavelu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SWIM is a vision-language-action framework that maps an RGB observation and a language instruction to a full actuation-command sequence for tendon-driven soft robots. Its policy, SWIM-VLA, couples a diffusion action head with Visual Soft Proprioception (VSP) over shared RGB/language/tendon-state representations, and executes commands via iterative virtual rollout plus intrinsic mechanical compliance. It reaches 100%/96%/88% success in simulation and 100%/80%/75% on hardware for packing/reaching/grasping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft and continuum robots achieve manipulation through distributed body deformation and contact, but translating language and visual context into executable whole-body actuation remains a fundamental challenge. Existing VLA approaches struggle to retain body geometry under limited demonstrations and typically require online policy queries to adapt to contact.

Method: SWIM maps an initial RGB observation and a language instruction to a complete actuation-command sequence. SWIM-VLA combines a diffusion action head, modeling conditional distributions of expert command chunks, with Visual Soft Proprioception (VSP), which supervises ordered body-anchor predictions using simulation ground truth through a shared representation of RGB observations, language instructions, and tendon states. Embodied mechanical intelligence supports physical execution of sequences generated by iterative virtual rollout from evolving simulated observations, with intrinsic compliance providing local contact adaptation without online policy queries.

Result: Evaluated on packing, reaching, and grasping with a planar tendon-driven soft robot (grasping targets anchored). In simulation, SWIM-VLA achieves 100%, 96%, and 88% success, outperforming an adapted OpenVLA-OFT baseline and controlled ablations. On hardware, SWIM achieves 100%, 80%, and 75%, versus 75%, 40%, and 25% for direct online deployment of the same policy checkpoint.

Conclusion: Combining diffusion-based action chunking with visual soft proprioception and embodied mechanical intelligence yields effective whole-body language-conditioned control for soft robots, substantially outperforming both a VLA baseline and direct online deployment of the same checkpoint.

Abstract: Soft and continuum robots enable manipulation through distributed body deformation and contact, yet translating language and visual context into executable whole-body actuation remains a fundamental challenge. We present SWIM, a framework that maps an initial RGB observation and a language instruction to a complete actuation-command sequence. Its vision-language-action (VLA) policy, SWIM-VLA, combines a diffusion action head with Visual Soft Proprioception (VSP) through a shared representation of RGB observations, language instructions, and tendon states. The diffusion head models conditional distributions of expert command chunks, while VSP supervises ordered body-anchor predictions using simulation ground truth, encouraging the representation to retain body geometry when learning from limited demonstrations. Embodied mechanical intelligence supports physical execution of command sequences generated through iterative virtual rollout from evolving simulated observations, with intrinsic compliance providing local contact adaptation without online policy queries. We evaluate SWIM on packing, reaching, and grasping on a planar tendon-driven soft robot, with grasping targets anchored. In simulation, SWIM-VLA achieves success rates of 100\%, 96\%, and 88\%, respectively, outperforming an adapted OpenVLA-OFT baseline and controlled ablations. On hardware, SWIM achieves success rates of 100\%, 80\%, and 75\%, compared with 75\%, 40\%, and 25\% for direct online deployment of the same policy checkpoint.

</details>


### [38] [BRAVE-6D: Benchmark for Robotic Active Vision in 6DOF Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17106)
*Philipp Ausserlechner,Bernhard Neuberger,Alessandro Scherl,Michael Schebek,Stefan Thalhammer,Markus Vincze*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BRAVE-6D is a benchmark for evaluating robotic active vision systems on 6D object pose estimation, using Gaussian Splatting-based view synthesis to enable reproducible comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detecting and grasping small objects is challenging in robotics. Active vision (moving the robot closer) is an intuitive solution, but fair comparison of approaches is difficult because identical physical scene setups are required. There is a need for a common ground to benchmark active vision systems.

Method: The authors introduce BRAVE-6D, a benchmark for robotic active vision systems targeting object pose estimation as a first step toward grasping. It leverages view synthesis based on 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) to supply scenes and tools for benchmarking. Baseline solutions are provided that perform visual servoing within the scene and estimate poses of small objects.

Result: Baseline solutions successfully perform visual servoing within the scene and accurately estimate poses of small objects.

Conclusion: BRAVE-6D provides a reproducible benchmark for active vision in small-object 6D pose estimation, enabled by 3DGS view synthesis.

Abstract: Detecting and grasping small objects remains a significant challenge in robotics. Active vision, where the robot moves closer to the object, is an intuitive solution, yet comparing approaches on common ground is difficult since identical physical scene setups are required. Hence, we introduce BRAVE-6D, a benchmark designed to evaluate robotic active vision systems for object pose estimation, a crucial first step in grasping objects. BRAVE-6D leverages view synthesis based on Gaussian Splats (3DGS) to provide scenes and tools for benchmarking active vision systems. We show baseline solutions performing visual servoing within the scene and accurately estimating the poses of small objects.

</details>


### [39] [Intrinsic Robot Rewarding: Reusing VLA Representations for Autonomous Evaluation and Policy Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17115)
*Tobias Schaffer,Mohab Elkhayat,Daniela Nicklas,Mustafa Almohamad,Elham Al-Fuqara*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IRR proposes reusing a VLA system's existing visual encoder and demonstration endpoints as a reference bank to score the robot's own outcomes, avoiding a separate evaluator or perception backbone; a COMAU Racer 3 demonstrator at TRL 4 validates the laboratory foundation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA systems already provide rich visual representations and successful task demonstrations, but these resources are typically used only for policy learning. IRR argues they can serve a second purpose: evaluating the robot's own outcomes and generating feedback for policy improvement, thereby lowering integration effort, reducing recurring human outcome scoring, and enabling improvement from data already available in industrial robot systems.

Method: IRR adds a reference bank and a scoring operation to the existing VLA pipeline. Successful demonstration endpoints define task-specific references, and the policy's frozen visual encoder supplies the feature space in which new outcomes are assessed—no separate learned evaluator or additional perception backbone is required. The paper specifies the reward formulation, central research questions, and an evaluation methodology that links reward reliability to task success and supervision effort.

Result: An operational COMAU Racer 3 demonstrator is available at technology readiness level 4 (TRL 4), establishing a laboratory foundation for connecting internal outcome evaluation to physical policy improvement. The work positions itself relative to established research on visual rewards and learning from experience.

Conclusion: IRR is presented as a position and a reusable approach: reusing the perception and demonstration resources already present in VLA-based industrial robots to evaluate outcomes and drive policy improvement. The next research step is to link internal outcome evaluation to physical policy improvement.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) systems already bring together two valuable resources for robot learning: rich visual representations and demonstrations of successful task execution. Intrinsic Robot Rewarding (IRR) proposes to use these resources for a second, complementary purpose: evaluating the robot's own outcomes and providing feedback for policy improvement. Successful demonstration endpoints define task-specific references, and the policy's frozen visual encoder provides the feature space in which new outcomes are assessed. The core reward mechanism adds a reference bank and a scoring operation to the existing pipeline, without requiring a separate learned evaluator or an additional perception backbone. Our position is that this reuse offers a promising route to lower integration effort, efficient reward computation, and reduced recurring human outcome scoring. Building on established research in visual rewards and learning from experience, IRR brings these ideas into the robot's existing perception and demonstration pipeline. An operational COMAU Racer 3 demonstrator is available at technology readiness level 4 (TRL 4). This laboratory foundation supports the next research step: connecting internal outcome evaluation to physical policy improvement. We present the reward formulation, central research questions, and an evaluation methodology linking reward reliability to task success and supervision effort. The intended contribution is a reusable approach to learn and improve from the data and experience already available in industrial robot systems.

</details>


### [40] [LOTUSim-Energy: A Maritime Simulator for Human-Drone Interaction in Autonomous Offshore Operation \&amp; Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17124)
*Juliette Grosset,Marie Dubromel,Hélène Lechêne,Quentin Arzel,Cédric Buche*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LOTUSim-Energy is a real-time maritime simulator that unifies UAVs, USVs, AUVs, and ROVs with coupled metocean forcing and immersive (desktop and VR) human supervision, offering an offshore task library and energy-aware, fault-detection modules for repeatable validation of multi-domain human–drone offshore maintenance workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offshore operation and maintenance demand coordinated activity across air, surface, and subsea domains with human supervision, but realistic, repeatable evaluation environments that integrate heterogeneous vehicles, environmental disturbances, and human-in-the-loop oversight are lacking, making sea deployment risky and hard to de-risk.

Method: A distributed, real-time simulation architecture couples heterogeneous unmanned vehicles (UAVs, USVs, AUVs, ROVs) with environmental forcing (wind, waves, currents); immersive supervision interfaces (desktop and VR); a structured offshore task library for repeatable autonomy-stack evaluation; and modular tools for realistic physics, energy-aware battery modeling, and fault detection.

Result: System-level performance is demonstrated on a multi-domain inspection scenario of monopile and transition piece structures, evaluating the reliability of an integrated waypoint-follower plugin and AIS-referenced trajectory tracking under real-time energy monitoring.

Conclusion: By combining unified environmental physics, heterogeneous vehicle simulation, and immersive supervision, LOTUSim-Energy serves as an integration testbed for prototyping and rehearsing offshore human–robot collaboration workflows, contributing to de-risking sea deployment.

Abstract: Offshore maintenance requires operations in the air, the surface, and the subsea domain and include human supervision. This paper presents LOTUSim-Energy, a real-time maritime simulator designed for multi-domain human--drone interaction for offshore operation and maintenance. The plat- form unifies heterogeneous unmanned vehicles (Unmanned Aerial Vehicles: UAVs, Unmanned Surface Vehicles: USVs, Autonomous Underwater Vehicles: AUVs, Remotely Operated Vehicles: ROVs) within a distributed architecture coupling environment forcing (wind, waves, currents) and provides immersive user interfaces for supervision (desktop and virtual reality). A structured offshore task library enables repeatable evaluation of autonomy stacks under realistic metocean disturbances. The simulator supports realistic physics, energy-aware battery modeling, and fault-detection pipelines as modular validation tools. System-level performance is demonstrated on a multi-domain inspection scenario for monopile and transition piece structure, where we evaluate the reliability of integrated waypoint-follower plugin and Automatic Identification System (AIS)-referenced trajectory tracking under real-time energy monitoring. By combining unified environmental physics, heterogeneous vehicle simulation, and immersive supervision, LOTUSim-Energy provides an integration testbed for prototyping and rehearsing offshore human--robot collaboration workflows, as a step toward de-risking sea deployment.

</details>


### [41] [Continual Learning for Traversability Prediction with Uncertainty-Aware Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17141)
*Hojin Lee,Yunho Lee,Daniel A Duecker,Cheolhyeon Kwon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a continual learning framework for traversability prediction that incrementally adapts to new terrains using a generative experience recall model, avoiding storage of past data while enabling uncertainty-aware adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traversability prediction in unstructured environments is challenging due to complex robot-terrain interactions causing traction loss and dynamic instability. Existing learning-based methods fail to adapt to novel terrains, and when adaptation occurs, they suffer from catastrophic forgetting of previously learned environments.

Method: A continual learning framework that incrementally adapts to new terrains via a generative experience recall model. It retains prior experience without storing past data and incorporates the uncertainty of generated samples from the recall model, enabling uncertainty-aware adaptation.

Result: Real-world experiments with a skid-steering robot demonstrate the framework's effectiveness, showing its ability to adapt across a series of diverse environments while mitigating catastrophic forgetting.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables effective continual adaptation for traversability prediction, preserving prior experience without storing past data and supporting uncertainty-aware adaptation, validated through real-world experiments.

Abstract: Traversability prediction is a critical component of autonomous navigation in unstructured environments, where complex and uncertain robot-terrain interactions pose significant challenges such as traction loss and dynamic instability. Despite recent progress in learning-based traversability prediction, these methods often fail to adapt to novel terrains. Even when adaptation is achieved, retaining experience from previously trained environments remains a challenge, a problem known as catastrophic forgetting. To address this challenge, we propose a continual learning framework for traversability prediction that incrementally adapts to new terrains using a generative experience recall model. A key virtue of the proposed framework is two folds: i) retain prior experience without storing past data; and ii) incorporate the uncertainty of the generated samples from the recall model, enabling uncertainty-aware adaptation. Real-world experiments with a skid-steering robot validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework, demonstrating its ability to adapt across a series of diverse environments while mitigating catastrophic forgetting.

</details>


### [42] [LiLi: Lie Theory Based 3D LiDAR Scan Alignment Degeneracy Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17145)
*Vsevolod Hulchuk,Jan Bayer,Jan Faigl*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes LiLi, a Lie-theory-based degeneracy detection method for 3D LiDAR scan alignment that identifies the full set of degenerate transformations in SE(3) via perturbations and Lie algebra generators, outperforming Hessian-based detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In degenerate environments such as straight corridors or flat fields, the scan alignment solution is non-unique, degrading localization and mapping accuracy. Existing degeneracy detection methods neglect the potential for reassociating data points and are thus sensitive to noise and complex degeneracies.

Method: LiLi leverages Lie theory to identify the full set of degenerate transformations within the SE(3) rigid transformation Lie group. It applies perturbations to the optimized solution and compares the resulting optimized poses for robust degeneracy detection, using generators from the Lie algebra se(3) to systematically describe the set of degenerate transformations.

Result: On synthetic data, LiLi reduces alignment error by 50% over the state-of-the-art Hessian-based method, with larger gains on noisy datasets. In real-world degenerate datasets integrated into LiDAR odometry, it achieves superior localization on a 260 m trajectory and succeeds on a 430 m round-trip tunnel trajectory where the Hessian-based reference fails.

Conclusion: Leveraging Lie theory for degeneracy detection yields more robust and complete identification of degenerate transformations, improving LiDAR odometry localization in challenging degenerate scenarios beyond Hessian-based approaches.

Abstract: In this paper, we study 3D LiDAR scan alignment in challenging scenarios with degeneracies, such as straight corridors or flat fields, where the alignment solution is not unique and compromises localization and mapping accuracy. Existing degeneracy detection methods that neglect the potential for reassociating data points are prone to being sensitive to noise and complex degeneracies. Therefore, we propose LiLi - a novel method that leverages Lie theory to identify the full set of degenerate transformations within the SE(3) Lie group of rigid transformations. The method employs perturbations of the optimized solution and compares the resulting optimized poses to ensure robust detection of degeneracies. By leveraging generators from the Lie algebra se(3), the method provides a systematic approach to describing the set of degenerate transformations. Quantitative evaluations on synthetic data show significant improvement over the state-of-the-art Hessian-based method, reducing alignment error by 50%, with more significant improvements for datasets featuring noise. In the real-world degenerate datasets, the proposed method integrated into LiDAR-based odometry yields superior localization performance compared to the reference solution based on the Hessian-based degeneracy detector on a 260 m long trajectory, and succeeds on a 430 m long round-trip tunnel trajectory where the reference fails.

</details>


### [43] [Kernel-Based Metrics Learning for Uncertain Opponent Vehicle Trajectory Prediction in Autonomous Racing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17147)
*Hojin Lee,Youngim Nam,Sanghun Lee,Cheolhyeon Kwon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes heterogeneous kernel metrics for Deep Kernel Learning (DKL) to predict uncertain opponent vehicle trajectories in autonomous racing, enabling safe overtaking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous racing faces significant challenges in safely overtaking Opponent Vehicles (OVs) with uncertain trajectories arising from unknown driving policies.

Method: The authors propose heterogeneous kernel metrics for Deep Kernel Learning (DKL) that robustly capture diverse OV driving policies and carry out precise trajectory predictions with associated uncertainties. The kernel metrics align similar driving policies and disjoin dissimilar ones in an unsupervised manner based on observed EV-OV interactions.

Result: Experiments on a 1/10th scale racecar platform demonstrate improved prediction accuracy and safe overtaking against OVs, while remaining computationally efficient for onboard computing units.

Conclusion: The proposed method is viable for fast-paced racing environments, offering accurate uncertainty-aware predictions for safe overtaking.

Abstract: Autonomous racing confronts significant challenges in safely overtaking Opponent Vehicles (OVs) that exhibit uncertain trajectories, stemming from unknown driving policies. To address these challenges, this study proposes heterogeneous kernel metrics for Deep Kernel Learning (DKL), designed to robustly capture the diverse driving policies of OVs, and carry out precise trajectory predictions along with the associated uncertainties. A key virtue of the proposed kernel metrics lies in their ability to align similar driving policies and disjoin dissimilar ones in an unsupervised manner, given the observed interactions between the Ego Vehicle (EV) and OVs. The efficacy of the proposed method is substantiated through experimental studies on a 1/10th scale racecar platform, demonstrating improved prediction accuracy and thereby safely overtaking against OVs. Furthermore, our method is computationally efficient for onboard computing units, affirming its viability in fast-paced racing environments. The video and source code can be found at https://github.com/HMCL-UNIST/OpponentPredictionWithKMDKL.git.

</details>


### [44] [HuMemSLAM: Efficient Human-Inspired Semantic Place Recognition for Robust Visual SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17168)
*Mayowa Adebambo,Sebastian Donnelly,Armand Amaritei,Andrew Bradley,Alexander Rast*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes HuMem-VPR, a brain-inspired visual place recognition method coupling bottom-up perceptual evidence with top-down contextual reasoning, and HuMemSLAM, its integration with ORB-SLAM3, achieving higher retrieval accuracy and lower latency than state-of-the-art VPR methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous systems need reliable place recognition for efficient SLAM, but traditional geometric visual SLAM relies on low-level features and geometric consistency, remaining vulnerable to perceptual aliasing (similar-looking different places) and perceptual variation (differently-appearing same place). While semantic SLAM and learned VPR improve robustness, real-time deployment demands both high retrieval accuracy and low latency.

Method: Inspired by human memory and perception, the authors propose HuMem-VPR, which exploits the bidirectional relationship between bottom-up perceptual evidence and top-down contextual reasoning to achieve high-level place understanding. They further introduce HuMemSLAM, integrating HuMem-VPR with ORB-SLAM3.

Result: HuMem-VPR achieved the highest aggregate retrieval accuracy on the real-image benchmark, competitive accuracy on the CARLA benchmark, and approximately two to three times lower latency than evaluated state-of-the-art VPR methods. Across evaluated dataset families and online experiments, HuMemSLAM substantially improved integrated Recall@1 over ORB-SLAM3's native retrieval while reducing proposals submitted to its geometric backend.

Conclusion: HuMem-VPR provides an accurate and low-latency place recognition solution, and its integration into ORB-SLAM3 (HuMemSLAM) improves retrieval performance and reduces backend load, demonstrating the value of the human-memory-inspired bidirectional perception-reasoning design for real-time SLAM.

Abstract: Autonomous systems require reliable place recognition for efficient and effective simultaneous localisation and mapping (SLAM). Traditional geometric visual SLAM approaches rely on low-level features and geometric consistency, but remain vulnerable to perceptual aliasing, where different places appear similar, and perceptual variation, where the same place appears different. Although semantic SLAM and modern learned visual place recognition (VPR) methods improve robustness under challenging perceptual conditions, real-time deployment requires both high retrieval accuracy and low latency. Inspired by human memory and perception, we propose HuMem-VPR, which exploits the bidirectional relationship between bottom-up perceptual evidence and top-down contextual reasoning to achieve high-level place understanding. We further introduce HuMemSLAM, the integration of HuMem-VPR with ORB-SLAM3. HuMem VPR achieved the highest aggregate retrieval accuracy on the real-image benchmark, competitive accuracy on the CARLA benchmark, and approximately two to three times lower latency than the evaluated state-of-the-art VPR methods. Across the evaluated dataset families and online experiments, HuMemSLAM substantially improved integrated Recall @1 over ORB-SLAM3's native retrieval while reducing the proposals submitted to its geometric backend.

</details>


### [45] [Fingers as Legs: Learning Self-Supported Locomotion and Manipulation with an Anthropomorphic Hand](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17172)
*Amirhossein Kazemipour,Hehui Zheng,Robert Katzschmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that an anthropomorphic robotic hand can reuse its own fingers for locomotion, weight support, and manipulation, learning these skills via reinforcement learning with a finger-aware design and hardware-calibrated simulation, achieving untethered crawling, steering, and fall recovery on hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A walking robotic hand must use the same fingers to move its body, support its weight, and interact with the environment. The goal is to enable an anthropomorphic hand to learn these skills while retaining its finger design and position controller, achieving a compact mobile manipulator that reuses its fingers for locomotion and interaction without a separate locomotion mechanism.

Method: The approach uses reinforcement learning that accounts for the hand's unequal fingers. Training is performed in a simulator calibrated from hardware measurements. The platform is self-contained with onboard power and computation. A reward formulation tailored to the hand is compared against tuned rewards originally designed for quadrupeds.

Result: In simulation, the hand moves faster with the proposed reward formulation than with tuned rewards originally designed for quadrupeds. On hardware, task-specific policies enable untethered crawling, steering, and fall recovery. While supporting its own weight, the hand executes successive keyboard commands without vision and pushes an object to targets using overhead visual feedback.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate a compact mobile manipulator that reuses its fingers for locomotion and interaction, without a separate locomotion mechanism. An anthropomorphic hand can learn locomotion and manipulation skills while retaining its finger design and position controller.

Abstract: A walking robotic hand must use the same fingers to move its body, support its weight, and interact with the environment. We show how an anthropomorphic hand can learn these skills while retaining its finger design and position controller. Onboard power and computation make the platform self-contained. Our reinforcement learning approach accounts for the hand's unequal fingers, with training in a simulator calibrated from hardware measurements. In simulation, the hand moves faster with our reward formulation than with tuned rewards originally designed for quadrupeds. On hardware, task-specific policies enable untethered crawling, steering, and fall recovery. While supporting its own weight, the hand also executes successive keyboard commands without vision and pushes an object to targets using overhead visual feedback. These results demonstrate a compact mobile manipulator that reuses its fingers for locomotion and interaction, without a separate locomotion mechanism.

</details>


### [46] [Fleet-To-Lab: A Transfer Learning Framework For Lunar Rover Slippage Estimation Via Model Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17187)
*Riccardo Viviano,Saki Omi,Andrej Orsula,Miguel Olivares-Mendez*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces Fleet-to-Lab, a transfer learning framework that fuses heterogeneous expert models collected from previously deployed lunar rovers to improve wheel slip estimation for a future rover, using AcoMerge, a swarm-intelligence-based model fusion algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate wheel slip estimation is critical for autonomous lunar rover mobility and navigation. However, ML models trained on terrestrial data generalize poorly to lunar terrain, and real lunar datasets remain scarce due to few missions and costly data acquisition, creating an Earth-Moon domain gap.

Method: Fleet-to-Lab leverages proprioceptive data from previously deployed heterogeneous lunar rovers as expert models and fuses them into one architecture using a modest post-deployment dataset. AcoMerge, a hybrid swarm-intelligence algorithm, searches for an optimal combination of expert parameters to perform model fusion.

Result: High-fidelity physics simulation experiments show balanced accuracy and macro-F1 improvements over deep model fusion baselines. AcoMerge is competitive with joint training on deep architectures while achieving higher macro-F1 and balanced accuracy on a smaller model.

Conclusion: Model fusion, as realized by Fleet-to-Lab and AcoMerge, represents a viable transfer learning alternative for slip estimation in space robotic missions constrained by limited data.

Abstract: Accurate wheel slip estimation is essential for autonomous lunar rover mobility and navigation. Machine Learning models trained on terrestrial data generalize poorly to lunar terrain, and real lunar datasets are scarce due to the limited number of missions and costly data acquisition. We present Fleet-to-Lab, a transfer learning framework that leverages proprioceptive data collected by previously deployed heterogeneous lunar rovers to mitigate the Earth-Moon domain gap in slip estimation for a future deployable unit. We fuse several heterogeneous expert models into a single architecture, using a modest dataset collected after the rover deployment. We propose AcoMerge, a new hybrid swarm-intelligence algorithm that performs model fusion by searching for an optimal combi- nation of expert parameters. Experiments conducted in a high- fidelity physics simulation show balanced accuracy and macro- F1 improvements compared to deep model fusion baselines. AcoMerge exhibits competitive performance with joint training on deep architectures, while achieving higher macro-F1 and balanced accuracy on a smaller model. Overall, our framework shows model fusion as a possible transfer learning alternative for slippage estimation in space robotic missions with limited data.

</details>


### [47] [TIO-Former: Ultra-Lightweight 6-Directional ToF-Inertial Odometry for Nano-UAVs via a Streaming Causal Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17198)
*Yang Liu,Yifan He,Wenhao Zhao,Xiangyu Mo,Yang Xu,Hao Wei,Mingze Ma,Huan Li,Yifan Wu,Zipeng Dai,Xin Zhou,Fei Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TIO-FORMER is a camera-free, optical-flow-free, mapless range-inertial odometry framework for autonomous nano-UAV navigation, fusing an IMU with six orthogonal 8x8 multi-zone ToF arrays (15 g payload) via a Streaming Causal Transformer with bounded inference cost and memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nano-UAV ego-motion estimation is constrained by strict SWaP-C limits: visual sensors and LiDARs exceed payload budgets, optical flow degrades in low-texture scenes, and inertial-only estimation accumulates drift. Multi-zone ToF arrays offer a lightweight metric complement, but 6-DoF estimation from only 384 ranges per frame is hard due to invalid returns, anisotropic observability, and temporal computational scaling.

Method: A frontend pairs consecutive range grids with a bilateral gated difference; IMU-guided cross-attention dynamically routes directional features conditioned on platform kinematics. A Streaming Causal Transformer couples an uncompressed Local KV cache with a compressed Chunk-FIFO memory to keep inference cost and memory footprint bounded regardless of flight duration.

Result: In real-flight tests, TIO-FORMER cuts open-loop position error by 54.4% versus nano-UAV optical flow and by 66.4%–89.1% versus learned inertial baselines. Robustness is also evaluated across multiple environments and under severe sensing degradation. On an edge RISC-V companion computer it reaches P95 latency of 10.466 ms and 6.324 MiB peak resident memory (<5% system RAM).

Conclusion: Sparse range sensing provides practical geometric anchoring for resource-constrained micro-aerial robots, validating the framework's accuracy and efficiency for nano-UAV navigation.

Abstract: Autonomous nano-UAV navigation requires accurate ego-motion estimation under stringent size, weight, power, and computing (SWaP-C) constraints, where visual sensors and LiDARs exceed payload limits, optical flow degrades in low-texture scenes, and inertial-only state estimation is susceptible to accumulated drift. While multi-zone time-of-flight (ToF) arrays provide a lightweight metric complement, 6-DoF estimation from merely 384 ranges per frame is challenged by invalid returns, anisotropic observability, and temporal computational scaling. We propose TIO-FORMER, a camera-free, optical-flow-free, and mapless range-inertial odometry framework driven by an IMU and an ultra-lightweight (15 g) payload of six orthogonal 8 x 8 ToF arrays. Our frontend pairs consecutive range grids with a bilateral gated difference, while IMU-guided cross-attention dynamically routes directional features conditioned on platform kinematics. A Streaming Causal Transformer couples an uncompressed Local KV cache with compressed Chunk-FIFO memory, maintaining bounded inference cost and memory footprint independent of flight duration. In real-flight evaluations, TIO-FORMER reduces open-loop position error by 54.4% compared to nano-UAV optical flow and by 66.4%-89.1% over learned inertial baselines. We also evaluate performance across multiple environments and robustness under severe sensing degradation. Deployed on an edge RISC-V companion computer, TIO-FORMER achieves a P95 latency of 10.466 ms and peak resident memory of 6.324 MiB (less than 5 percent system RAM), demonstrating that sparse range sensing provides practical geometric anchoring for resource-constrained micro-aerial robots. Code is available at https://github.com/Ly041021/TIO-Former.

</details>


### [48] [FluxVLA Engine: A One-Stop VLA Engineering Platform for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17210)
*Yinhao Li,Weixin Mao,Zihan Lan,Jikun Rong,Qirui Hu,Yiming Zhang,Weipeng Deng,Bowen Shen,Minzhao Zhu,Yiming Mao,Yan Yang,Chenguang Cui,Hongyuan Chen,Xu Huang,Zheyi Zhao,Pinxi Shen,Bozhen He,Zhen Fu,Yifan Wang,Zexin Zhang,Ang Gao,Haoyu Chen,Chengqi Shi,Hua Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FluxVLA Engine is an open, configuration-driven platform that standardizes interfaces across the embodied-policy stack to enable a reproducible data-to-deployment workflow for VLA models, WAMs, and offline RL, targeting the engineering bottlenecks between algorithms and reliable robot deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models, world-action models (WAMs), and offline RL are rapidly expanding the design space of embodied policies, but translating these algorithms into reliable robot systems is constrained by fragmented data formats, training stacks, evaluation protocols, inference runtimes, and embodiment-specific interfaces. The paper targets these engineering bottlenecks separating promising embodied-learning algorithms from reproducible evaluation and dependable deployment.

Method: Rather than introducing a new policy model, FluxVLA standardizes interfaces for datasets, vision-language and world models, action heads, reward- or advantage-weighted learning, distributed training, simulation evaluation, optimized inference, and robot operators. It integrates compositional dual-arm simulation, scalable automatic data generation, and model-decoupled human-in-the-loop rollout, takeover, correction collection, and reward annotation. For responsive physical execution, it combines Real-Time Chunking (RTC) with accelerated inference backends, lightweight remote GPU serving, and configurable trajectory post-processing, connecting offline learning, simulation validation, online correction, and real-robot execution via shared, auditable contracts.

Result: The platform delivers a unified, reproducible data-to-deployment workflow that bridges offline learning, simulation validation, online correction, and real-robot execution through shared and auditable contracts. Code is available at https://github.com/FluxVLA/FluxVLA.

Conclusion: FluxVLA provides an open, configuration-driven infrastructure solution—rather than a new policy algorithm—aimed at resolving the engineering fragmentation that hinders reproducible evaluation and dependable deployment of embodied-learning algorithms.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models, world-action models (WAMs), and offline reinforcement learning methods are rapidly expanding the design space of embodied policies, yet turning these algorithms into reliable robot systems remains constrained by fragmented data formats, training stacks, evaluation protocols, inference runtimes, and embodiment-specific interfaces. We present $\mathrm{FluxVLA}$ Engine, an open, configuration-driven platform that turns heterogeneous embodied-policy components into a reproducible data-to-deployment workflow. Rather than introducing another policy model, $\mathrm{FluxVLA}$ standardizes interfaces for datasets, visual-language and world models, action heads, reward- or advantage-weighted learning, distributed training, simulation evaluation, optimized inference, and robot operators. The engine further integrates compositional dual-arm simulation, scalable automatic data generation, and model-decoupled human-in-the-loop rollout, takeover, correction collection, and reward annotation. For responsive physical execution, it combines Real-Time Chunking (RTC) with accelerated inference backends, lightweight remote GPU serving, and configurable trajectory post-processing. Together, these capabilities connect offline learning, simulation validation, online correction, and real-robot execution through shared and auditable contracts. $\mathrm{FluxVLA}$ therefore targets the engineering bottlenecks separating promising embodied-learning algorithms from reproducible evaluation and dependable deployment. Code is available at https://github.com/FluxVLA/FluxVLA

</details>


### [49] [Swim-and-Breach at Palm Scale: A Rudder-Steered Two-Propeller Underwater Robot Platform with Differential-Thrust Pitch Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17240)
*Daehyun Choi,Ian Bergerson,Hengjia Zhu,Tianjun Lan,Saad Bhamla*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A palm-scale (65 mm, 34 g) swim-and-breach robot uses stacked propellers with PID pitch control and a rudder for yaw, achieving 13.9 BL/s swimming, 209 deg/s turning, and a seamless cruise-leap-cruise jump of 1.6 BL high and 3.7 BL long.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
