Motivation: Small underwater robots are limited to swimming and cannot cross dry gaps or barriers between pools, restricting inspection in streams, flooded structures, and industrial systems. A compact platform that both swims and leaps is needed.

Method: The robot has two vertically stacked propellers providing propulsion and differential-thrust pitch control under a PID loop, plus a tail rudder for yaw. The hull was evaluated by flow simulation to cut drag five-fold versus an equivalent cuboid, and propellers were optimized via B-series modeling validated by dynamometer measurements.

Result: Swimming speed reaches 13.9 body lengths per second and turning 209 deg per second, near reported upper limits for underwater robots. The pitch loop commands any nose-up angle, and with rudder-stabilized exit the robot leaps 1.6 BL high and 3.7 BL long in a seamless cruise-leap-cruise sequence.

Conclusion: The platform enables small-scale robots that cross barriers and dry gaps between pools for inspection in streams, flooded structures, and industrial systems.

Abstract: We present a palm-scale (65 mm, 34 g) swim-and-breach robot platform. Two vertically stacked propellers provide both propulsion and differential-thrust pitch control under a proportional-integral-derivative (PID) loop, and a tail rudder adds yaw control. The hull, evaluated by flow simulation, reduces the drag five-fold relative to an equivalent cuboid, and the propellers are optimized using B-series modeling validated by dynamometer measurements. The current robot swims at 13.9 body lengths per second and turns at 209 deg per second, corresponding to the upper limits reported for underwater robots. In free swimming, the pitch loop turns the body to any commanded nose-up pitch angle, and, with the rudder stabilizing the exit, the current robot leaps 1.6 body lengths high and 3.7 long in a seamless cruise-leap-cruise sequence. The platform can be used to build small-scale robots that cross barriers and dry gaps between pools for inspection in streams, flooded structures, and industrial systems.

</details>


### [50] [DriveMCP: An Agentic AI framework for Advanced Driver Assistance System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17247)
*Farzad Nadiri,Mehdi Cina,Ahmad B. Rad*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DriveMCP is a modular, auditable agentic driver-assistance framework that fuses vision-language scene understanding, retrieval-augmented compliance reasoning, weather/traction estimation, and CAN/OBD telemetry through MCP servers, then arbitrates "speak vs. act" via an RSS-inspired guardrail; in CARLA it cuts traffic infractions and overspeed while improving hazard response and keeping sub-second latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLM-based driving assistants often rely on injected simulator ground truth for compliance cues and lack auditable, modular arbitration between advice and action. The work aims to build a pipeline where critical compliance elements (speed limits, jurisdiction cues) are derived from perception outputs rather than injected, and where multiple expert signals are coordinated and safety-filtered before any recommended action.

Method: DriveMCP integrates a sensor-like perception stack with DriveLM as the vision-language front end to produce graph-structured scene understanding (Graph VQA) and language-grounded driving information. Compliance elements in world_state (posted speed limits, jurisdiction cues) are extracted via a structured parsing layer from DriveLM outputs, not simulator ground truth. A stateful orchestration layer coordinates specialized experts exposed as MCP servers: (i) a Rules server doing retrieval-augmented compliance reasoning over jurisdiction-specific traffic codes and sign conventions, (ii) a Weather server estimating traction risk and contextual speed advisories, and (iii) an MCP-CAN server surfacing CAN/OBD telemetry and diagnostics for health-aware risk shaping. Outputs are fused into a structured decision recommending a course of action, then filtered by an RSS-inspired guardrail that arbitrates speak vs. act under bounded online adaptation.

Result: In CARLA simulation across multilingual, cross-border, and dynamic speed-limit scenarios, DriveMCP reduces traffic infractions and overspeed compared with VLM-Direct, VLM-Direct+RAG, and VLM-Tools-NoArbiter baselines, while improving hazard response time and maintaining sub-second advisory latency.

Conclusion: A modular agentic architecture that combines perception-grounded compliance inference, MCP-exposed domain experts, and an RSS-inspired arbiter yields safer, more compliant driver assistance than direct VLM or RAG-only/tool-only baselines, with real-time feasibility and improved auditability.

Abstract: An agentic AI driver-assistance framework that integrates perception, compliance reasoning, vehicle-state interpretation, and safety arbitration into a modular and auditable pipeline. The architecture, referred to as DriveMCP, incorporates a sensor-like perception stack alongside DriveLM as the vision-language front end to generate a graph-structured scene understanding (Graph Visual Question Answering) and language-grounded driving information. Key compliance elements in world_state, including posted speed limits and jurisdiction cues, are derived from DriveLM outputs through a structured parsing layer rather than being injected as simulator ground truth. A stateful orchestration layer coordinates specialized experts exposed as Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers: (i) a Rules server that performs retrieval-augmented compliance reasoning over jurisdiction-specific traffic codes and sign conventions, (ii) a Weather server that estimates traction risk and contextual speed advisories, and (iii) an MCP-CAN server that surfaces Controller Area Network (CAN)/On-Board Diagnostics (OBD) telemetry and diagnostic context for health-aware risk shaping. These outputs are fused to generate a structured decision that prompts a recommended course of action. The outcome is then further filtered by a Responsibility-Sensitive Safety (RSS)-inspired guardrail that arbitrates speak versus act decisions under bounded online adaptation. In CARLA simulation across multilingual, cross-border, and dynamic speed-limit scenarios, DriveMCP reduces traffic infractions and overspeed relative to the VLM-Direct, VLM-Direct+RAG, and VLM-Tools-NoArbiter baselines, while improving hazard response time and maintaining sub-second advisory latency.

</details>


### [51] [Port-Hamiltonian Koopman Operator Synthesis for Mechanical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17249)
*Rajpal Singh,Aditya Singh,Jishnu Keshavan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This work proposes a structure-preserving Koopman framework for Euler-Lagrange systems using generalized-momentum coordinates, learning passive port-Hamiltonian liftings via a structure-constrained neural architecture and Cayley-midpoint discretization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-dimensional Koopman models enable efficient linear prediction and control of nonlinear robotic systems, but models learned purely from trajectory data may violate the energetic structure of the underlying mechanics, producing predictions that exhibit artificial energy growth and diverge under recursive propagation.

Method: The framework is built on generalized-momentum coordinates, where the momentum transformation exposes the mechanical actuation as a known, state-independent port preserved explicitly in the lifted dynamics. A structure-constrained neural architecture jointly learns the lifting functions and a port-Hamiltonian Koopman generator, rendering the learned dynamics passive by construction rather than through penalty terms or post-hoc projection. A Cayley-midpoint discretization preserves the storage-dissipation balance exactly in discrete time.

Result: Simulation and experimental studies demonstrate improved prediction accuracy, data efficiency, and closed-loop tracking over Koopman baselines, with increasing gains for higher-dimensional systems.

Conclusion: The properties of the proposed framework are established analytically by deriving the discrete storage balance and associated stability guarantees of the learned predictor, yielding passive-by-construction learned dynamics.

Abstract: Finite-dimensional Koopman models enable efficient linear prediction and control of nonlinear robotic systems. However, models learned purely from trajectory data may violate the energetic structure of the underlying mechanics, producing predictions that exhibit artificial energy growth and diverge under recursive propagation. This work presents a structure-preserving Koopman framework for Euler-Lagrange systems built on generalized-momentum coordinates. The momentum transformation exposes the mechanical actuation as a known, state-independent port, which is preserved explicitly in the lifted dynamics. A structure-constrained neural architecture is developed to jointly learn the lifting functions and a port-Hamiltonian Koopman generator, rendering the learned dynamics passive by construction rather than through penalty terms or post-hoc projection. A Cayley-midpoint discretization further preserves the corresponding storage-dissipation balance exactly in discrete time. These properties are established analytically by deriving the discrete storage balance and associated stability guarantees of the learned predictor. Simulation and experimental studies demonstrate improved prediction accuracy, data efficiency, and closed-loop tracking over Koopman baselines, with increasing gains for higher-dimensional systems.

</details>


### [52] [CAD-Based Relation Learning and Geometric-Symbolic Planning for Robotic Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17263)
*Fabian Harlacher,Christian Friedrich*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a hybrid Assembly Sequence Planning (ASP) framework that combines learning-based relation extraction with geometric-symbolic reasoning to generate feasible robotic disassembly sequences from imperfect CAD data, achieving 85.83% planning success on ASAP while cutting median planning time by over an order of magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ASP is combinatorially hard, making exhaustive planning impractical for complex industrial assemblies. Additionally, many CAD models lack reliable semantic contact information or require extensive manual preprocessing, which limits the applicability of existing methods.

Method: A neural network predicts semantic geometric relations from point clouds, with human-in-the-loop verification to correct uncertain predictions and planning failures. Extracted relations are converted into a symbolic assembly graph, on which a geometric-symbolic planner computes locally valid sets of robotic manipulation primitives. A visibility-based ray-casting strategy guides the search for feasible disassembly directions without exhaustive combinatorial search, and the local solution space supports efficient sequence optimization.

Result: Evaluated on an introduced assembly dataset and the ASAP test dataset. On ASAP, the planner achieves an 85.83% planning success rate, reducing median planning time by more than one order of magnitude across all assembly sizes and by more than a factor of 50 for assemblies with more than 30 components versus the baseline.

Conclusion: The hybrid framework enables efficient robotic assembly sequence planning from imperfect CAD data while substantially reducing planning time. By integrating learning-based feature segmentation, human-in-the-loop verification, and geometric-symbolic reasoning, it offers a practical foundation for scalable and adaptable robotic assembly and disassembly planning.

Abstract: Assembly Sequence Planning (ASP) remains a challenging problem due to its combinatorial nature, making exhaustive planning approaches impractical for complex industrial assemblies. Furthermore, many CAD models lack reliable semantic contact information or require extensive manual preprocessing, limiting the applicability of existing methods. This paper presents a hybrid ASP framework combining learning-based relation extraction with geometric-symbolic reasoning to generate feasible robotic disassembly sequences from imperfect CAD data. A neural network predicts semantic geometric relations from point clouds, while human-in-the-loop verification enables correction of uncertain predictions and planning failures. Extracted relations are transformed into a symbolic assembly graph, enabling a geometric-symbolic planner to efficiently compute locally valid sets of robotic manipulation primitives. A visibility-based ray-casting strategy guides the search for feasible disassembly directions without requiring an exhaustive combinatorial search, while the local solution space enables efficient sequence optimization. The framework is evaluated on an introduced assembly dataset and on the ASAP test dataset. On the ASAP test dataset, the proposed planner achieves an 85.83% planning success rate while reducing the median planning time by more than one order of magnitude across all assembly sizes and by more than a factor of 50 for assemblies with more than 30 components compared to the baseline. The results demonstrate that the proposed hybrid framework enables efficient robotic assembly sequence planning from imperfect CAD data while substantially reducing planning time. By combining learning-based feature segmentation, human-in-the-loop verification, and geometric-symbolic reasoning, the framework provides a practical foundation for scalable and adaptable robotic assembly and disassembly planning.

</details>


### [53] [Escape-Aware Control Barrier Functions for Quadrotor Safety under Body-Rate Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17292)
*Lei Shi,Haosong Wen,Qichao Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: State-only control barrier functions (CBFs) mis-certify input-constrained quadrotors: thrust reorientation lag makes some certified states inescapable. The authors quantify this certification gap in closed form and propose an "escape barrier" on the augmented (state, previous input) pair, with closed-form inverse for maximum certifiable closing speed, embedded in a predictive controller. It completes all 550 episodes, outperforming a stopping-distance CBF (15%/25% failures) and a backup-CBF baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Input-constrained CBFs embed the admissible input set in the safe set, yet the barrier is typically a function of state alone. On a quadrotor this is not cosmetic: the thrust vector must be reoriented before decelerating an approach, and reorientation is bounded by attainable body rate. A state-only barrier therefore certifies states from which no escape is reachable in time—a genuine certification gap.

Method: The authors characterize the certification gap in closed form, showing its width is proportional to closing speed and inversely proportional to body-rate limit. They define an escape barrier on the augmented pair of state and previously applied input, measuring escape authority over the one-step reachable thrust cap. This admits a closed form and an analytic inverse for the maximum certifiable closing speed, and embeds in a predictive controller at no additional state cost.

Result: Across 550 paired closed-loop episodes on a 13-state quadrotor, the proposed controller completes every tested scenario, whereas the stopping-distance barrier over the same horizon fails 15% and 25% of episodes in exactly the two scenarios entering the predicted gap. Against an online backup-CBF baseline enforcing the same escape condition at the reached state, it holds a 29–74 degree larger directional margin and 3–18 times the clearance, and an independent conservative rollout referee finds no certified state from which escape fails.

Conclusion: State-only barriers leave a quantifiable certification gap for input-constrained quadrotors due to thrust reorientation lag. Augmenting the barrier with the previously applied input yields a closed-form escape barrier with analytic inverse, which when embedded predictively eliminates the gap, achieves full success and larger margins, and is validated by an independent conservative rollout referee.

Abstract: Control barrier functions for input-constrained systems place the admissible input set inside the definition of the safe set, yet the resulting barrier is almost always a function of the state alone; On a quadrotor this is not cosmetic: because the thrust vector must be reoriented before it can decelerate an approach, and reorientation is limited by the attainable body rate, a state-only barrier certifies states from which no escape is reachable in time; We characterize the certification gap in closed form and show its width is proportional to closing speed and inversely proportional to the body-rate limit; We then define an escape barrier on the augmented pair of state and previously applied input, with escape authority measured over the one-step reachable thrust cap; It admits a closed form and an analytic inverse for the maximum certifiable closing speed, and embeds in a predictive controller at no additional state cost; Across 550 paired closed-loop episodes on a 13-state quadrotor, the proposed controller completes every tested scenario, whereas the stopping-distance barrier enforced over the same horizon fails 15% and 25% of episodes in exactly the two scenarios that enter the predicted gap; Against an online backup-CBF baseline enforcing the same escape condition at the reached state, it holds a 29-74 degree larger directional margin and 3-18 times the clearance, and an independent conservative rollout referee finds no certified state from which escape fails.

</details>


### [54] [Online Geometric Change Detection via Scene Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17302)
*David Thorne,Samuel Jia Cong Chua,Nakul Joshi,Aiden Wong,Christa S. Robison,Philip Osteen,Brett T. Lopez*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes CDSD, a submap-based framework for accurate online geometric change detection using LiDAR or RGB-D sensors, decomposing mapped environments into scenes and comparing local dense submaps to efficiently detect changes for real-time map reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous robots on long-duration single- and multi-session missions in dynamic environments need to identify environmental changes (e.g., fallen trees, opened doors) as contextual information for online planning. Existing dense SLAM maps allow comparison but global map comparison across sessions is computationally expensive and cannot support single-session online change detection.

Method: CDSD spatially decomposes mapped environments into unique scenes so that changes are found efficiently by comparing dense, local subsets of the global map (submaps). As the first submap-based geometric change detection approach, it addresses four challenges: identifying appropriate minimal-redundancy scenes, generating dense representative submaps per scene, detecting changes between submaps with differing fields of view, and processing detected changes for real-time map reconstruction.

Result: The algorithm is demonstrated on custom datasets collected at the Army Research Laboratory facility in Graces Quarters, Maryland, and on open-source multi-session change detection datasets.

Conclusion: CDSD enables efficient online geometric change detection via scene decomposition and submap comparison, supporting real-time map reconstruction and outperforming global-map comparison approaches in computational feasibility for single- and multi-session missions.

Abstract: Autonomous robots are increasingly deployed on long duration single- and multi-session missions in dynamic environments, where the ability to identify environmental changes such as fallen trees or opened doors provides important contextual information for online planning. We propose a framework called Change Detection via Scene Decomposition (CDSD) for accurate online geometric change detection using LiDAR or RGB-D sensors. Recent advances in geometric SLAM have made it possible to generate dense, tightly aligned maps without post processing, but comparing global maps across entire sessions is computationally expensive and does not allow for single-session online change detection. CDSD instead spatially decomposes mapped environments into unique scenes where changes can be found efficiently by comparing dense, local subsets of the global map called submaps. As the first submap-based approach for geometric change detection, we identify and address the following core challenges: 1) identifying appropriate scenes for change detection that require minimal redundant information; 2) generating dense and representative submaps for each scene; 3) detecting changes between submaps with differing fields of view; and 4) processing detected changes for real-time map reconstruction. Results demonstrate our algorithm on custom datasets collected at the Army Research Laboratory facility in Graces Quarters, Maryland, and on open-source multi-session change detection datasets.

</details>


### [55] [XPACE: Joint World and Action Modeling from Heterogeneous Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17372)
*Jiacheng Wei,Jerry Bai,Xiaoyu Yue,Zidong Wang,Xiaoyang Guo,Cheng Chen,Fanqi Pu,Fan Wu,Zhixu Yue,Yizhuo Li,Feng Qiu,Bo Liu,Yuying Ge,Hui Zhou,Chenyi Chen,Yixiao Ge*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: XPACE is a unified embodied world model that jointly predicts robot actions and future video (world action model) and predicts visual outcomes of prescribed actions (world simulator), using a shared video backbone to connect heterogeneous experience and generate new experience for policy improvement, validated on XPENG's IRON humanoid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose robots must draw on diverse experience, select actions, and anticipate the world changes caused by those actions. Existing approaches typically separate action learning from dynamics modeling, wasting action-unlabeled video and limited robot demonstrations. The paper argues video prediction can both connect heterogeneous experience to action learning and generate new experience for policy improvement.

Method: XPACE uses a single world action model with a shared video backbone serving dual roles: a policy (joint action + future video prediction) and a simulator (predicting visual consequences of given actions). Training combines action-unlabeled video for visual dynamics with action-labeled human and robot demonstrations for joint video-action prediction. A coarse-to-fine curriculum progressively emphasizes robot control while retaining human experience. The simulator is then adapted to its own generated context to synthesize deviation-recovery trajectories around expert demonstrations, and the policy is fine-tuned on filtered recovery examples.

Result: On XPENG's IRON humanoid robot, heterogeneous training improved robustness and enabled transfer of human-observed skills to tasks absent from robot demonstrations. Recovery data generated by the model's own simulator further improved real-world task completion.

Conclusion: Joint world and action modeling effectively connects learning from heterogeneous experience with simulation-driven policy self-improvement, yielding improved robustness and real-world task completion on a humanoid robot.

Abstract: A general-purpose robot needs to draw on diverse experience, choose actions, and anticipate how those actions will change the world. We introduce XPACE, a unified embodied world model that serves as both a world action model, jointly predicting executable robot actions and future video, and a world simulator, predicting the visual consequences of prescribed actions. Our key insight is that video prediction can both connect heterogeneous experience to action learning and generate new experience for policy improvement. With a shared video backbone between the policy and simulator, we use action-unlabeled video to learn visual dynamics and action-labeled human and robot demonstrations to jointly learn video and action prediction. Building on this architecture, a coarse-to-fine training curriculum progressively emphasizes robot control while retaining human experience, allowing the policy to learn behaviors beyond those covered by robot demonstrations. Beyond learning from recorded experience, XPACE uses its simulator to create additional recovery supervision for the policy. Specifically, we adapt the simulator to its own generated context, synthesize deviation-recovery trajectories around expert demonstrations, and fine-tune the policy on filtered recovery examples. Experiments on XPENG's IRON humanoid robot show that heterogeneous training improves robustness and enables transfer of human-observed skills to tasks absent from robot demonstrations, while recovery data generated by the model's own simulator further improves real-world task completion. Together, these results demonstrate how joint world and action modeling connects learning from heterogeneous experience with simulation-driven policy self-improvement.

</details>


### [56] [Exact Fusion and Coordinated Exploration in Multi-Robot Active Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17384)
*Peng Wu,Mohsen Imani,Amidu Kamara,Md Tamzeed Islam,Seyede Fatemeh Ghoreishi,Mahdi Imani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Robot teams sharing a common environment model double-count the shared belief—once at fusion (the common prior is multiplied n times) and once at planning (all robots score plans under the same belief). Adding evidence increments to the shared natural parameter, realized at fusion and expected at planning, removes both errors and yields corrected information gains that sum to the joint gain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When robot teams that learn a common environment model exchange belief summaries and plan by expected information gain, conjugate exponential-family beliefs cause a systematic double-counting of shared information. At fusion, multiplying local posteriors counts the common prior n times; at planning, every robot scores its plan under the same shared belief, so the team converges on the same unknown. These errors inflate redundancy and misvalue exploration, motivating a correction that tracks only new evidence.

Method: Introduce evidence increments to the shared natural parameter: realized increments at fusion and expected increments at planning. The expected increment of a committed teammate supplies the next robot's conditional gain. Analyze exactness for Gaussian beliefs with fixed sampling paths and for Dirichlet beliefs under the novelty approximation of discrete active inference; for finite hypothesis classes, replace the approximation with short exact enumeration. Use sequential commitment for planning.

Result: Corrected gains sum to the joint gain; the removed redundancy equals the total correlation of the planned observation streams; sequential commitment retains the 1/2 greedy guarantee. The expected increment is exact for Gaussian beliefs with fixed sampling paths and for Dirichlet beliefs under the novelty approximation, whose team objective has a closed concave form within an explicit bound of exact mutual information; it fails for finite hypothesis classes. Experiments on cooperative RockSample, foraging, and field monitoring show fusion correction leaves exploration redundancy unchanged, anticipated evidence removes it, and sequential commitment recovers most of the value of centralized joint planning at cost linear in team size.

Conclusion: Correcting belief fusion and planning by evidence increments eliminates shared-belief double-counting, aligns per-robot gains with the joint information gain, and preserves greedy performance guarantees. The approach is exact for Gaussian and (approximately) Dirichlet settings, requires exact enumeration for finite hypothesis classes, and empirically achieves near-centralized planning value at linear cost in team size, with anticipated evidence—not fusion correction—responsible for removing exploration redundancy.

Abstract: Robot teams that learn a common environment model exchange belief summaries and plan by the expected information gain of their actions. Under conjugate exponential-family beliefs the shared belief is counted once per robot at two points: at fusion, the product of local posteriors counts the common prior $n$ times, and at planning, every robot scores its plan under the same belief and the team converges on the same unknown. Both errors are removed by adding evidence increments to the shared natural parameter, realized increments at fusion and expected increments at planning. The expected increment of a committed teammate gives the next robot its conditional gain; corrected gains sum to the joint gain, the redundancy removed equals the total correlation of the planned observation streams, and sequential commitment keeps the $1/2$ greedy guarantee. The expected increment is exact for Gaussian beliefs with fixed sampling paths and for Dirichlet beliefs under the novelty approximation of discrete active inference, whose team objective has a closed concave form within an explicit bound of the exact mutual information, and fails for finite hypothesis classes, where a short exact enumeration replaces it. Experiments on cooperative RockSample, foraging, and field monitoring show that fusion correction leaves exploration redundancy unchanged, anticipated evidence removes it, and sequential commitment recovers most of the value of centralized joint planning at cost linear in the team size.

</details>


### [57] [Residual Fault Adaptation for Dexterous In-Hand Manipulation Under Runtime Joint Faults](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17404)
*Linan Deng,Xing Liu,Lin Hong,Feng Hua,Guijun Ma,Zuogong Yue,Fumin Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes Residual Fault Adaptation (RFA), a teacher-anchored framework that compensates for hidden command-channel joint faults in dexterous in-hand manipulation using a recurrent residual policy trained with fault-injection domain randomization and adaptive sampling, without requiring fault labels or switching signals at deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous in-hand manipulation depends on coordinated control of multiple actuated joints. A runtime joint fault can abruptly disrupt the contact configuration needed for successful manipulation, and hidden command-channel faults are especially challenging because the controller receives no explicit fault indication.

Method: RFA retains a frozen healthy teacher to provide nominal behavior and trains a recurrent residual policy to infer corrective actions from proprioceptive and command-response history. Fault-injection domain randomization (FIDR) varies fault mode, affected joint, severity, and onset time during training, while adaptive sampling increases the frequency of fault modes with lower recent performance. A frozen Direct FIDR policy provides a distributional reference only on fault-active training samples and is absent from deployment.

Result: Simulation experiments on a dexterous hand show that RFA improves manipulation performance relative to the healthy policy under a fixed mixed-fault protocol. Real-robot experiments with software-injected faults demonstrate zero-shot deployment of the learned adaptation policy.

Conclusion: RFA provides an effective teacher-anchored residual learning approach for compensating hidden command-channel faults in dexterous manipulation, achieving improved performance in simulation and zero-shot real-robot deployment without fault labels or controller-switching signals.

Abstract: Dexterous in-hand manipulation requires coordinated control of multiple actuated joints, and a runtime joint fault can abruptly disrupt the contact configuration required for successful manipulation. In this work, we propose residual fault adaptation (RFA), a teacher-anchored framework for compensating for hidden command-channel faults. RFA retains a frozen healthy teacher to provide nominal behavior and trains a recurrent residual policy to infer corrective actions from proprioceptive and command-response history. During training, fault-injection domain randomization (FIDR) varies the fault mode, affected joint, severity, and onset time, while adaptive sampling increases the frequency of fault modes associated with lower recent performance. A frozen Direct FIDR policy provides a distributional reference only on fault-active training samples and is absent from deployment. The deployed controller receives neither fault labels nor controller-switching signals. Simulation experiments on the dexterous hand indicate that RFA can improve manipulation performance relative to the healthy policy under a fixed mixed-fault protocol. Real-robot experiments with software-injected faults further demonstrate zero-shot deployment of the learned adaptation policy.

</details>


### [58] [Optimized Wrench Polytope Analysis for Real-Time Stability Control of Legged Robots in Complex Multi-Contact Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17405)
*Friedrich Graaf,Elias Birkefeld,Christian Eichmann,Elias Hofele,Tristan Schnell,Georg Heppner,Arne Roennau,Rüdiger Dillmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes an optimized algorithm to compute the full actuatable wrench polytope for arbitrary contact scenarios, enabling joint-torque computation at 49 Hz and allowing real-time deployment of a stability controller on legged robot hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legged robots are useful across many real-world automation tasks, but traversal remains very challenging in difficult terrains such as slopes, caves, and scaffolding. Existing controllers cannot guarantee stability in these complex scenarios, motivating a faster and more general method for contact-aware stability control.

Method: The authors develop an optimized algorithm for evaluating the full actuatable wrench polytope for arbitrary contact scenarios. This improved analysis enables per-joint torque computation within a control frequency of 49 Hz, making it feasible to deploy the method inside a regular control loop for actuating robot poses under different contact configurations.

Result: The stability controller was evaluated extensively in simulation and validated on actual walking robot hardware. It achieved stability in very complex scenarios that are currently not achievable by any other controller.

Conclusion: An optimized actuatable wrench polytope computation allows real-time, contact-aware stability control for legged robots and extends their feasible traversal capabilities to highly challenging terrains, both in simulation and on hardware.

Abstract: Legged robots offer a variety of automation applications in real-world scenarios. But areas that are difficult to traverse, like slopes, caves, or scaffolding, still pose a great challenge for traversal. To tackle this problem, we propose an optimized algorithm for evaluating the full actuatable wrench polytope for arbitrary contact scenarios. With our improved analysis algorithm, the torques for each joint of the robot can be calculated within a control frequency of 49 Hz. The achieved speedup allows for deployment within a regular control loop for actuating robot poses for different contact scenarios. We evaluated our stability controller extensively in simulation scenarios and validated its applicability by deploying it on actual walking robot hardware. The proposed controller achieved stability in very complex scenarios that are currently not achievable by any other controller.

</details>


### [59] [Hamilton-Jacobi Reachability for Hybrid Systems: Unified Goal-Driven Control with Safety Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17430)
*Javier Borquez,Shuang Peng,Somil Bansal*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper extends Hamilton-Jacobi reachability analysis to hybrid dynamical systems, characterizing safe sets via a generalized value function over discrete and continuous states, and introduces a least-restrictive safety filter and hybrid backward reach-avoid tubes, validated on a quadrupedal robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid dynamical systems are a powerful framework for robotic systems in contact-rich environments, but ensuring safety and performance is difficult due to the coupling between continuous dynamics and discrete mode transitions. Existing formal verification methods like classical HJ reachability target continuous-time nonlinear systems and do not directly handle hybrid systems.

Method: The authors extend classical Hamilton-Jacobi reachability analysis to hybrid dynamical systems. They define a generalized value function over both discrete and continuous states, accounting for control constraints and model uncertainty, and provide a numerical algorithm to compute it. Building on the resulting safe sets, they propose (1) a hybrid least-restrictive safety filter that intervenes on both discrete and continuous components of a nominal controller only when necessary, and (2) hybrid backward reach-avoid tubes that enforce safety and goal-reaching simultaneously, enabling synthesis of continuous and discrete control policies.

Result: The proposed framework is validated through simulation studies and real-world experiments on a quadrupedal robot, demonstrating effectiveness in hybrid mode planning and safety-critical applications.

Conclusion: The work provides a formal, computationally tractable extension of HJ reachability to hybrid systems, offering safety filtering and reach-avoid synthesis capabilities, and shows practical viability on real robotic hardware.

Abstract: Hybrid dynamical systems provide a powerful modeling framework for robotic systems, particularly in contact-rich environments. However, ensuring safety and performance in such systems remains challenging due to the intricate coupling between continuous dynamics and discrete mode transitions. In this work, we extend classical Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) reachability analysis, a formal verification method for continuous-time nonlinear systems, to hybrid dynamical systems. Our framework characterizes safe sets for hybrid systems through a generalized value function defined over both discrete and continuous states while accounting for control constraints and model uncertainty. We additionally provide a numerical algorithm to compute this value function.
  Building on these safe sets, we propose two different mechanisms to integrate performance objectives. First, we introduce a hybrid least-restrictive safety filter that intervenes on both the discrete and continuous components of a nominal controller only when necessary to avoid unsafe states, thereby preserving nominal behavior whenever possible. Second, we formulate and compute hybrid backward reach-avoid tubes, enabling the simultaneous enforcement of safety and goal-reaching behavior, an extension not previously addressed within hybrid HJ reachability. This enables the synthesis of continuous and discrete control policies that guarantee both safety and task completion. We validate our framework through simulation studies and real-world experiments on a quadrupedal robot, demonstrating its effectiveness in hybrid mode planning and safety-critical applications.

</details>


### [60] [Gaussian Processes for Modelling Spatial Fields with Robot Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17463)
*Guillermo Legarda Herranz,Gianpiero Francesca,Mauro Birattari*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces location-unaware Gaussian process regression (LU-GPR), enabling robot swarms without external positioning systems to model spatial fields using only local sensing and communication, while agreeing on a common frame of reference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robot swarm methods for spatial field modelling rely on external positioning systems so each robot knows its own position. This dependency limits applicability in GPS-denied or infrastructure-free environments. The paper aims to remove this dependency.

Method: LU-GPR lets each robot infer the posterior mean and variance of the field in space while simultaneously converging to a shared frame of reference with peers, using only local sensing and communication. An online algorithm is proposed so each robot consistently updates local estimates as its local frame converges to the common one. A product of experts model combines each robot's estimates with those of its peers to build a global model.

Result: LU-GPR scales well with the number of robots and is robust to limited communication ranges. It is demonstrated in real-world monitoring scenarios, specifically estimating the flow of an evacuating crowd.

Conclusion: Location-unaware Gaussian process regression provides a decentralised, scalable, and robust approach to spatial field modelling for robot swarms operating without external positioning systems.

Abstract: Robot swarms, by virtue of their decentralised architecture, are a natural tool for scalable, robust modelling of spatial fields, such as water temperature, wind velocity, or terrain elevation. However, existing methods rely on external positioning systems that allow each robot to determine its own position in space. Here, we introduce location-unaware Gaussian process regression (LU-GPR) as a solution to the modelling of spatial fields in the absence of such positioning systems. LU-GPR allows each robot to infer the posterior mean and variance of the field in space, while simultaneously agreeing on a common frame of reference with its peers, using only local sensing and communication. We propose an online algorithm that allows each robot to consistently infer local estimates as its local frame of reference converges to the common one. By means of a product of experts model, each robot also combines the estimates of its peers with its own to obtain a global model. Our results show that LU-GPR scales well with the number of robots and is robust to limited communication ranges. We also demonstrate how it can be used in real-world monitoring scenarios to estimate the flow of an evacuating crowd.

</details>


### [61] [Dissecting Motion-Prior Regularization for Data-Scarce Robotic Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17484)
*Ning Hu,Shuai Li,Jindong Tan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Training-time motion-prior regularization (minimum jerk, speed-curvature, joint, or generic smoothness) was compared for a diffusion policy learned from 15 demonstrations; joint and minimum-jerk-only both reached 87.5% insertion success across 80 real-robot trials, but joint offered no gain over minimum jerk alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study investigates whether training-time motion-prior regularization can improve insertion success when a diffusion policy is trained from only 15 demonstrations, addressing the challenge of learning effective policies under scarce demonstration data.

Method: Minimum jerk (discouraging abrupt changes in predicted translational acceleration) and speed-curvature (coupling movement speed to path geometry) priors were compared individually and jointly, alongside neither prior and generic smoothness. Evaluation used 80 real-robot trials per setting, pooled over four recorded condition classes, with success rates and Wilson 95% confidence intervals visualized.

Result: Joint and minimum-jerk-only settings each achieved 70/80 successes (87.5%); speed-curvature only achieved 69/80 (86.3%); neither prior 66/80 (82.5%); generic smoothness 67/80 (83.8%). Joint regularization exceeded neither by 5.0 percentage points but showed no observed gain over minimum jerk alone.

Conclusion: Minimum jerk is motivated as the simpler candidate for replication. The results do not establish synergy, biomechanical specificity, improved safety, or distribution-shift robustness.

Abstract: This study asks whether training-time motion-prior regularization can improve insertion success when a diffusion policy is learned from only 15 demonstrations. Minimum jerk discourages abrupt changes in predicted translational acceleration; speed-curvature regularization instead couples movement speed to path geometry. These are candidate mechanisms for task completion, not safety guarantees. We compare the priors individually and jointly, neither prior, and generic smoothness, with 80 real-robot trials per setting pooled over four recorded condition classes. Joint and minimum-jerk-only settings each achieved 70/80 successes (87.5%), versus 69/80 (86.3%) for speed-curvature only, 66/80 (82.5%) for neither prior, and 67/80 (83.8%) for generic smoothness. Success rates and Wilson 95% confidence intervals are visualized for direct comparison. Joint regularization exceeded neither by 5.0 percentage points but provided no observed gain over minimum jerk alone. The results motivate minimum jerk as the simpler candidate for replication, without establishing synergy, biomechanical specificity, improved safety, or distribution-shift robustness.

</details>


### [62] [Modality-Autoregressive World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17524)
*Adam Hung,Bardienus P. Duisterhof,Deva Ramanan,Jeffrey Ichnowski*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ModAR is the first world-action model that autoregressively denoises multiple future visual modalities before predicting actions, systematically studying modality combinations and showing sequential generation outperforms prior WAM formulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World-action models (WAMs) typically predict the future as RGB images, but other visual modalities like depth, pretrained visual features, and point tracks can capture geometric, semantic, and motion features more efficiently. How best to combine these modalities within WAMs remains an open question that this work aims to address.

Method: ModAR is introduced as the first WAM to autoregressively denoise multiple future modalities before predicting actions, allowing each prediction to condition on previously generated modalities. Models are trained from scratch to systematically study how training-data mixtures, predicted modalities, and WAM formulations affect performance. Comparisons are made against the video-model-initialized WAM Flex-π fine-tuned on the same data.

Result: WAMs benefit from predicting point tracks, DINO features, and depth maps, while additionally predicting future RGB does not provide consistent benefit. ModAR's sequential generation outperforms existing WAM formulations, achieving the highest average success rate at all evaluated data scales. ModAR reaches a slightly higher observed average success rate than Flex-π (75% vs. 72%) with ~20× fewer training FLOPs and no pretraining. On three real-world bimanual tasks, ModAR outperforms baselines and improves with human videos.

Conclusion: Autoregressive multi-modality denoising in WAMs is an effective formulation: point tracks, DINO features, and depth are valuable predictive modalities, sequential modality generation surpasses prior WAM designs, and ModAR achieves strong real-world bimanual performance with far lower compute and no pretraining.

Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) jointly model future observations and actions, typically predicting the future as RGB images. Other visual modalities such as depth, pretrained visual features, and point tracks can more efficiently capture geometric, semantic, and motion features. However, how best to combine these modalities within WAMs remains an open question. We introduce ModAR, the first WAM to autoregressively denoise multiple future modalities before predicting actions. This allows each prediction to condition on previously generated modalities. We train from scratch to systematically study how training-data mixtures, predicted modalities, and WAM formulations affect performance. In our evaluations, WAMs benefit from predicting point tracks, DINO features, and depth maps, while additionally predicting future RGB does not provide a consistent benefit. We also find that ModAR's sequential generation outperforms existing WAM formulations, with the highest average success rate at all evaluated data scales. We also fine-tune the video-model-initialized WAM Flex-$π$ on the same data; ModAR achieves a slightly higher observed average success rate (75% vs. 72%) while using approximately $20\times$ fewer training FLOPs and no pretraining. On three real-world bimanual tasks, ModAR outperforms baselines and improves with human videos.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [63] [Optimal Pruning for Neural Architectures using Fisher Information Distances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16129)
*David S. Berman,Yen-Yu Fu,Edward Hirst,Thelma Chiwete Obirai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A differential-geometric pruning scheme is derived from the geodesic distance under the Fisher information metric. It yields a hierarchy of pruning methods: magnitude pruning is the crudest approximation, while more faithful geodesic approximations give better pruning. It outperforms magnitude and local-Fisher pruning across architectures, datasets, and pruning ratios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameter pruning is widely used, but it often lacks a principled, mathematically motivated criterion for choosing which parameters to remove. The paper seeks a theoretically grounded measure of a parameter's true importance based on the actual change in model behavior caused by pruning.

Method: Pruning a parameter to zero is cast as displacing the model to the hypersurface where that parameter vanishes. The true change is measured by the minimal geodesic distance to that hypersurface in model space, using the Fisher information metric. Progressively more faithful approximations of this geodesic distance produce a hierarchy of pruning schemes, ranging from traditional magnitude pruning to new, more sophisticated criteria. The approach is evaluated on fully-connected networks and vision transformers, on MNIST and CIFAR-10, over the full 0-100% pruning range and five random seeds.

Result: The new method outperforms pruning by parameter magnitude and by local Fisher information alone in every architecture-dataset combination, improving both accuracy and the Matthews correlation coefficient. Intermediate geodesic approximations yield computationally efficient pruning schemes that retain near-optimal performance.

Conclusion: The geometric framework provides a state-of-the-art pruning methodology for AI models and a verified, mathematically motivated justification for pruning criteria, unifying existing methods as special cases.

Abstract: A new scheme for parameter pruning is introduced, derived from the differential-geometric distance in model space. Pruning a parameter sets its value to zero, representing a displacement of the model to the hypersurface on which that parameter vanishes. The minimal distance from the unpruned model to this hypersurface is naturally computed via the geodesic distance in the model space as determined by the Fisher information metric. This distance determines the true change in the model, and its performance, under pruning. By analysing progressively more faithful approximations of this geodesic distance a natural hierarchy of optimality for pruning methods is determined. This starts with the traditional magnitude pruning, then develops into new more sophisticated and effective pruning schemes. The method is demonstrated for both fully-connected networks and vision transformers, on MNIST and CIFAR-10, over the complete $0$-$100\%$ pruning range and across five random seeds. It outperforms pruning by parameter magnitude and by the local Fisher information alone in every architecture and dataset combination considered, on both accuracy and the Matthews correlation coefficient. Additionally, analysis of different levels of geodesic approximation produces intermediate pruning schemes that are computationally efficient and maintain near-optimal performance. This geometric picture supplies not only a state-of-the-art pruning methodology for AI models, but also a verified and mathematically-motivated justification for pruning schemes.

</details>


### [64] [Safe Error Correction for Language Models: Frozen-Base Adjustment with Capability Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16145)
*Gautam Kishore*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CRN v2, a ~34M-parameter logit-level correction module trained atop a fully frozen Gemma 4 E2B, corrects 53.3% of base-model errors on a 60-question domain exam with no measurable degradation on tested capability benchmarks, whereas a matched-budget LoRA baseline corrects more (83.3%) but loses 30-75% of capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper asks whether a small correction module can fix errors in a frozen language model's outputs without degrading its base capabilities, framing the work as a study of the correction-capability tradeoff rather than a claim of architectural novelty.

Method: A lightweight logit-level correction module (CRN v2, ~34M trainable parameters, 0.73% of the 4.65B text module) is attached to a fully frozen Gemma 4 E2B; only the correction module is trained via supervised fine-tuning followed by reference-free DPO on 83,400 error-correction pairs, with a KL preservation term (lambda = 0.1). Ablations cover KL weight, a hidden-state injection variant at earlier layers, shallower injection (layer 4), multi-depth logit correction, and longer training (5,000 SFT + 2,000 DPO); a LoRA baseline is matched to the CRN v1 budget (6.6M params, rank 19).

Result: CRN v2 corrects 53.3% of base-model errors (43.3% on a reworded variant) with no degradation on MMLU/BoolQ (N=200) and car-wash (N=8). The matched-budget LoRA baseline achieves 83.3% correction but suffers 30-75% capability loss. The KL term is critical: lambda = 0.01 lowers correction to 35.0%. The hidden-state injection variant (1.6M params, SFT-only) reaches 50.0%/55.8%, shallow injection (layer 4) drops to 30.0%/28.3%, multi-depth logit correction (~35M) reaches only 40%, and longer training stays at 53.3%; no tested alternative exceeded the rank-128 logit result, indicating a best-achieved result of ~53% rather than a floor.

Conclusion: The paper presents a design principle -- frozen base + logit correction + KL anchoring -- that yields non-degrading error correction at a modest ~53% correction rate, and releases all code, main-result weights, and evaluation scripts (deep variant as code only, without trained deep checkpoints).

Abstract: We study a practical question: can a small correction module fix errors in a frozen language model's outputs without degrading its base capabilities? We propose CRN v2, a lightweight logit-level correction module (~34M trainable parameters, 0.73% of the 4.65B text module) that sits atop a fully frozen Gemma 4 E2B model. The base model is never updated; only the correction module learns, via supervised fine-tuning followed by reference-free DPO on 83,400 error-correction pairs. On a 60-question domain exam (CEHRI: Certified Human-Robot Intelligence, covering facts, arithmetic, and implicit-goal reasoning), CRN v2 corrects 53.3% of base-model errors (reworded variant: 43.3%) while showing no degradation on tested capability benchmarks (MMLU/BoolQ N=200; car-wash N=8). A LoRA baseline at the matched CRN v1 budget (6.6M params, rank 19) achieves 83.3% correction but suffers 30-75% capability loss on the same benchmarks -- the correction-capability tradeoff. An ablation shows that the KL preservation term (lambda=0.1) is critical: lowering it to 0.01 degrades correction to 35.0%. A hidden-state injection variant at earlier layers (1.6M params, SFT-only) reaches 50.0%/55.8% but does not exceed logit correction; shallower injection (layer 4) drops to 30.0%/28.3%; multi-depth logit correction (~35M) reaches only 40%; and longer training (5,000 SFT + 2,000 DPO) stays at 53.3% -- none of the alternative configurations we tested exceeded the rank-128 logit result, consistent with a best-achieved result of ~53% rather than a floor. This is a study of a design principle (frozen base + logit correction + KL anchoring), not a claim of architectural novelty. All code, main-result weights, and evaluation scripts are released (deep variant as code only -- no trained deep checkpoints).

</details>


### [65] [GPEvac: GNN-Based PPO for Adaptive Evacuation Routing During Shooting Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16163)
*Daniel Perkins,Subhadeep Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GPEvac, a GNN-based PPO framework, computes adaptive evacuation routes during shooting events, generalizing across building layouts and running in 14.73 ms on local CPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mass shootings are rising, creating an urgent need for real-time systems that guide victims to safety. Effective evacuation must minimize threat exposure while handling adversarial uncertainty and crowding. Existing methods are limited: literature approaches use layout-specific policies and are computationally intractable at large scale, while practical guidelines only give coarse "run, hide, fight" advice.

Method: GPEvac combines a GNN with PPO for adaptive evacuation routing. It uses an edge-first sequential message-passing scheme plus a learnable virtual global node to capture local and long-distance dependencies. The resulting graph embeddings feed a permutation-invariant scoring mechanism, so one learned policy transfers across building topologies and sizes.

Result: In extensive simulation, GPEvac outperforms intelligent baselines across distinct architectural layouts and significantly reduces total threat exposure. It computes global evacuation routes in 14.73 ms on local CPU hardware, supporting integration with live surveillance systems.

Conclusion: GPEvac enables real-time, layout-agnostic evacuation guidance, potentially saving lives during shooting events. Its methods transfer to other graph-structured decision-making domains such as critical infrastructure, intelligent transportation, and adaptive sensor networks.

Abstract: The sharp increase in mass shootings underscores an urgent need for systems that guide victims to safety in real time. An effective evacuation system must minimize threat exposure while also accounting for adversarial uncertainty and crowding dynamics. Current methods in the literature are rigidly constrained to layout-specific policies and computationally intractable in large-scale layouts, while practical guidelines simply advise victims to "run", "hide", or "fight". We propose GPEvac: a GNN-based PPO framework that computes adaptive evacuation routes during shooting events. To capture both local and long-distance dependencies, we introduce an edge-first sequential message-passing scheme with a learnable virtual global node. The resulting graph embeddings are integrated into a permutation-invariant scoring mechanism that allows a single learned policy to operate across building layouts of diverse topologies and sizes. Through extensive simulation, we show that GPEvac outperforms intelligent baselines across distinct architectural layouts, significantly reducing total threat exposure. Crucially, the system computes global evacuation routes in just 14.73 ms on local CPU hardware, enabling seamless integration with live surveillance systems. In addition to saving lives during shooting events, the methodologies developed are transferable to other graph-structured decision-making domains, including critical infrastructure, intelligent transportation systems, and adaptive sensor networks.

</details>


### [66] [Position: AI Is Not Ready for Strategic Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16189)
*Mark Riedl,Glenn Matlin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper argues that no LM-enabled wargame should influence planning, doctrine, policy, or crisis response without an auditable safety case, and that open-ended wargames' proper current use is to stress-test decision-influencing LM agents, not to serve as safety cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LM-based open-ended strategic wargames are high-stakes social simulations covering adversaries, institutions, escalation, and crisis response. LMs are attractive for playing agents, generating branches, adjudicating ambiguous actions, and summarizing lessons, but the same property—model language determining both what an actor attempts and what becomes simulated reality—makes open-ended roles dangerous. Yet there is no clear standard for when such simulations may legitimately inform consequential decisions.

Method: Conceptual/position analysis. The authors identify and characterize five failure modes and assess why ordinary benchmarks cannot establish safety in these settings, framing wargames instead as stress tests.

Result: Five failure modes are identified: decision laundering, adjudication opacity, role collapse, escalation-through-adjudication, and failure of strategic imagination. Ordinary benchmarks are shown to be incapable of establishing safety for these settings.

Conclusion: Wargames can expose failures as stress tests, but they are not themselves safety cases for consequential use; consequential use requires an auditable safety case.

Abstract: Open-ended strategic wargames are high-stakes LM-based social simulations: they model adversaries, institutions, escalation, plan brittleness, doctrine, and crisis response. Language models (LMs) are attractive because they can play agents, generate scenario branches, adjudicate ambiguous actions, and summarize lessons, but the same affordances make open-ended roles dangerous: model language determines both what an actor attempts and what becomes simulated reality. This position paper argues that no LM-enabled wargame should inform planning, doctrine, policy, or crisis response without an auditable safety case, and that the proper use of open-ended wargames today is to stress-test decision-influencing LM agents. We identify five failure modes: decision laundering, adjudication opacity, role collapse, escalation-through-adjudication, and failure of strategic imagination. Ordinary benchmarks cannot establish safety for these settings. Wargames can expose failures as stress tests; they are not themselves safety cases for consequential use.

</details>


### [67] [Calibrate, Then Route: A Measured Study of Learned Request Routing for Disaggregated LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16206)
*Srikanta Datta Tumkur,Jay Iyer,Mehar Simhadri,Sai Pavan Kumar,Sai Kapil Kumar,Ramesh Nampelly*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A routing policy for disaggregated LLM serving that scores each instance by estimated added completion time—using exact prompt length, predicted output length, post-admission KV cache pressure, and SLO class—achieves the highest mean goodput (0.864) across bursty traces on eight A40 GPUs, outperforming round robin, least loaded, and a length heuristic, with gains dependent on hardware calibration, decode pool size, and traffic heterogeneity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disaggregated LLM serving separates compute-heavy prefill from memory-heavy decode across distinct GPU pools, and systems like DistServe, Splitwise, and Mooncake make this separation fast. However, the routing decision—which instance handles each request—remains the key determinant of performance, motivating a study of a cost-estimating router.

Method: The authors develop a routing policy in a discrete event simulator that estimates each instance's additional completion time using exact prompt length, predicted output length, post-admission KV cache pressure, and SLO class. They validate it on eight NVIDIA A40 GPUs, each running a vLLM engine, with NIXL transferring KV caches between pools, running all workloads at measured saturation across three mixed, bursty arrival traces.

Result: The calibrated router achieves the highest mean goodput at 0.864 versus 0.835–0.847 for round robin, least loaded, and a length heuristic, with the lowest variance across traces. It beats round robin and the length heuristic on all three traces and least loaded on two (on the third it trails by 0.003, within noise). Simulator-derived constants cost 4.5 goodput points and about 40% of the tail latency advantage. Benefits grow with decode pool size and traffic heterogeneity but vanish at three instances. Under extreme scarcity, greedy cost minimization concentrates requests on the cheapest instance, and blind spreading performs better. With calibrated costs, the router matches round robin's goodput using six GPUs instead of seven.

Conclusion: A cost-estimating router informed by hardware-calibrated constants improves goodput and tail latency in disaggregated LLM serving, but its advantages depend on decode pool scale and traffic heterogeneity, disappear in small pools, and degrade under extreme scarcity without calibrated costs.

Abstract: Disaggregated LLM serving places compute heavy prefill and memory heavy decode on separate GPU pools. Systems such as DistServe, Splitwise, and Mooncake make this separation fast, but routing still determines which instances handle each request. We study a router that estimates the additional completion time on each instance using exact prompt length, predicted output length, post admission KV cache pressure, and SLO class. We develop the policy in a discrete event simulator and validate it on eight NVIDIA A40 GPUs, each running a vLLM engine, with NIXL transferring KV caches between pools. All workloads run at measured saturation. Across three mixed, bursty arrival traces, the calibrated router achieves the highest mean goodput at 0.864, compared with 0.835 to 0.847 for round robin, least loaded, and a length heuristic. It also shows the lowest variance across traces. It beats round robin and the length heuristic on all three traces and least loaded on two. On the third, it trails by 0.003, within run to run noise. Hardware calibration matters: simulator derived constants cost 4.5 goodput points and roughly 40 percent of the tail latency advantage, reducing the scorer to little more than queue counting. Benefits grow with decode pool size and traffic heterogeneity but disappear in pools with three instances, where queue counts are often enough. Under extreme scarcity, greedy cost minimization concentrates requests on the cheapest scored instance, and blind spreading performs better. With calibrated costs, the learned router matches the goodput of round robin using six GPUs instead of seven.

</details>


### [68] [Artificial intelligence and biosecurity: capabilities, threat pathways, and defense-in-depth governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16213)
*Candace S. Y. Chan,Aris Karatzikos,Ilias Georgakopoulos-Soares*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This review maps the biosecurity risks across the AI-enabled biological research pipeline, argues that current AI uplift primarily affects digital rather than physical tasks, and proposes defense-in-depth governance linking capability thresholds to proportionate responsibilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is pervading the connected digital-to-physical biological research workflow, offering major benefits to medicine, public health, and biotechnology, but also raising biosecurity concerns. The authors argue risk cannot be judged from AI capability alone; it depends on users, expertise, intent, access to laboratory tools and materials, and existing safeguards, motivating a systematic review of threats and governance.

Method: A review approach: characterizing biological threats from AI tool use across the full workflow, including information gathering, biological design, procurement, synthesis, testing, scale-up, and potential release. Additionally, the analysis examines why alignment techniques developed for general-purpose models transfer poorly to biological foundation models, and assesses the emerging role of interpretability for auditing whether hazardous capabilities are genuinely removed. Existing evidence is synthesized by comparing frontier-model performance on in-silico and screening-evasion benchmarks against controlled wet-laboratory studies.

Result: AI uplift exists but is primarily digital rather than physical. Frontier systems exceed expert baselines on in-silico and screening-evasion benchmarks, whereas controlled wet-lab studies show tacit knowledge and physical execution remain substantial barriers.

Conclusion: The authors advocate defense-in-depth governance that links capability thresholds to proportionate responsibilities across the biological AI ecosystem, aiming to reduce high-consequence risk while preserving beneficial use.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is reshaping biological research across an increasingly connected digital-to-physical workflow. General-purpose large language models can retrieve and integrate scientific information, support experimental planning, and computational analysis; biological foundation models can predict, optimize, and generate proteins, genes, and genome-scale sequences; agentic systems can coordinate multistep research tasks; automated laboratories can partially close the design-build-test-learn cycle. These technologies could greatly benefit medicine, public health, and biotechnology. However, their biosecurity risk depends not only on what the AI can do, but also on who uses it, their expertise and intent, their access to laboratory tools and materials, and the safeguards in place. Current evidence shows that AI uplift exists but primarily affects digital rather than physical tasks. Frontier systems have exceeded expert baselines on in-silico, and screening-evasion benchmarks, whereas controlled wet-laboratory studies find that tacit knowledge and physical execution remain substantial barriers. This review describes the different biological threats from AI tool use, from information gathering and biological design to procurement, synthesis, testing, scale-up, and potential release. We further examine why alignment techniques for general-purpose models transfer poorly to biological ones, and the emerging role of interpretability in auditing whether hazardous capabilities are genuinely removed. We argue for defense-in-depth governance that links capability thresholds to proportionate responsibilities across the biological AI ecosystem, reducing high-consequence risk while preserving beneficial use.

</details>


### [69] [Where Should the KV Cache Live? Placement Policies Across GPU, CPU, and SSD for Long-Lived Sessions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16215)
*Srikanta Datta Tumkur,Jay Iyer,Mehar Simhadri,Sai Pavan Kumar,Sai Kapil Kumar,Ramesh Nampelly*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper uses a calibrated discrete event simulator to study KV cache placement across GPU HBM, CPU DRAM, and SSD, finding that tiering itself (not placement policy) drives capacity and cost gains, that recency beats reuse frequency on chat traffic, and that prefetching is never worth its bandwidth cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GPU HBM is scarce and expensive, and accumulating KV cache state in chat, agent loops, and document QA consumes much of it. Prior systems (Mooncake, LMCache, FlexGen, InfiniGen, AttentionStore) extend GPU memory with DRAM and SSD, but the harder open questions remain unresolved: which blocks belong in each tier, when to move or evict them, and whether prefetching is worthwhile. The paper targets these placement, migration, and prefetching decisions.

Method: A discrete event simulator spanning GPU HBM, CPU DRAM, and SSD, calibrated against a random forest execution time predictor. The authors compare placement policies—recency, reuse frequency, predicted reuse, and an EWMA predictor with prefetch lookahead—swept across the policy and cache size grid, over chat, agent, and document QA workloads. Tiering configurations (1 + 8 + 64) are contrasted with policy effects, and impacts on throughput, PCIe migration traffic, and time to first token are measured, including an oracle with knowledge of future requests.

Result: Tiering supports 73.02x more concurrent sessions per GPU and reduces cost per session by 62.04x, gains attributed to tier capacities (1+8+64) rather than placement policy. Decode is compute bound at batch size one, so placement barely affects throughput and mainly changes PCIe migration traffic and time to first token. Recency yields 2.30x less migration traffic than reuse frequency for chat, while reuse frequency performs best for agents and document QA. The existing predicted reuse policy is byte identical to recency, so its agent recommendation is effectively recency. A genuine EWMA predictor changes behavior but still ranks behind reuse frequency on the workloads prediction was meant to help. Prefetching never justifies its bandwidth cost; even an oracle with future request knowledge never beats no prefetch on migration traffic.

Conclusion: Workload-specific placement can reduce data movement, but the predicted-reuse and prefetch recommendations are not supported as implemented. The paper's contribution is a rigorous, calibrated simulation study that isolates tiering capacity from policy, shows placement mainly affects migration traffic and TTFT rather than throughput, and cautions against adopting predicted-reuse or prefetching policies without stronger evidence.

Abstract: GPU high bandwidth memory is scarce and expensive, and KV caches consume much of it as chats, agent loops, and document question answering accumulate state. Systems such as Mooncake, LMCache, FlexGen, InfiniGen, and AttentionStore extend GPU memory with CPU DRAM and SSD. The harder question is which blocks belong in each tier, when to move or evict them, and whether prefetching helps. We study these choices in a discrete event simulator spanning GPU HBM, CPU DRAM, and SSD, calibrated against a random forest execution time predictor. We compare recency, reuse frequency, predicted reuse, and an EWMA predictor with prefetch lookahead across chat, agent, and document question answering workloads. Tiering supports 73.02 times more concurrent sessions per GPU and lowers cost per session by 62.04 times. These gains come from tier capacities of 1 plus 8 plus 64, not placement policy. Decode is compute bound at batch size one in our setup, so placement barely affects throughput. It mainly changes PCIe migration traffic and time to first token. Recency produces 2.30 times less migration traffic than reuse frequency for chat. Reuse frequency performs best for agents and document question answering. The existing predicted reuse policy is byte identical to recency, making its agent recommendation effectively recency. A genuine EWMA predictor changes behavior but still ranks behind reuse frequency on the workloads prediction was expected to help. Prefetching does not justify its bandwidth cost. Across the policy and cache size grid, even an oracle with knowledge of future requests never beats no prefetch on migration traffic. Workload specific placement can reduce data movement, but the predicted reuse and prefetch recommendations are not supported as implemented.

</details>


### [70] [Toward Governance-Aware Autonomous GIS: A Narrative Review of Ethical and Privacy Risks in LLM-Enabled GeoAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16232)
*Maya Subramanian,Devika Jain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This narrative review identifies eight recurring governance issues in LLM-enabled geospatial AI (GeoAI), characterizes and illustrates each, proposes a governance-aware architecture mapping issues to enforceable controls and auditable artifacts, and highlights an evidence gap for field-tested governance evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-powered GeoAI expands natural-language querying, generation, and interpretation of spatial information, creating governance challenges that general AI ethics discussions do not fully capture: passive location inference, spatially structured bias amplification, hallucinated spatial facts, and cross-modal uncertainty compounding.

Method: Narrative review synthesizing eight recurring issues; for each, the underlying mechanism is characterized, grounded in a literature example, and assessed for current technical/institutional responses. A governance-aware architecture for LLM-enabled autonomous GIS is then proposed, mapping issues to enforceable controls and auditable artifacts across the geospatial data lifecycle, illustrated via a flood-response routing scenario.

Result: The eight issues are data provenance and consent; spatial privacy and inference risk; algorithmic bias and spatial inequity; spatial mechanisms as structural risk (spatial autocorrelation, MAUP, scale effects); LLM-specific technical risks; explainability; policy and regulatory gaps; and public enablement and workforce development. Responses range from largely unaddressed to actively debated or subject to emerging policy, with a persistent evidence gap: proposed responses remain largely conceptual.

Conclusion: Field-tested evaluations of governance controls for LLM-enabled GeoAI remain limited. The paper outlines a research agenda emphasizing empirical validation, spatially specific interpretability tools, and workforce training aligned with these emerging risks.

Abstract: Geospatial artificial intelligence (GeoAI) powered by large language models (LLMs) is expanding the capacity to query, generate, and interpret spatial information through natural-language interfaces and agentic autonomous GIS workflows. This capability creates governance challenges that general AI ethics discussions do not fully capture, including passive location inference from mobility traces, spatially structured bias amplification driven by spatial autocorrelation and scale effects, hallucinated spatial facts, and uncertainty compounding across multimodal geospatial inputs. This narrative review identifies eight recurring issues in LLM-enabled GeoAI: data provenance and consent, spatial privacy and inference risk, algorithmic bias and spatial inequity, spatial mechanisms as structural risk (spatial autocorrelation, the modifiable areal unit problem, and scale effects), LLM-specific technical risks, explainability, policy and regulatory gaps, and public enablement and workforce development. For each issue, we characterize the underlying mechanism, ground it in an illustrative example from the literature, and assess the current state of technical or institutional responses, ranging from largely unaddressed to actively debated or subject to emerging policy. Building on this synthesis, we propose a governance-aware architecture for LLM-enabled autonomous GIS that maps each issue to enforceable controls and auditable artifacts across the geospatial data lifecycle, illustrated through a worked flood-response routing scenario. The review highlights a persistent evidence gap: proposed responses remain largely conceptual, and field-tested evaluations of governance controls for LLM-enabled GeoAI remain limited. We close by outlining a research agenda emphasizing empirical validation, spatially specific interpretability tools, and workforce training aligned with these emerging risks.

</details>
