Existing token-reduction solutions face a dilemma: lightweight encoder-driven methods miss critical semantics, while heavyweight MLLM-driven reduction erases efficiency gains. The authors identify a deeper inefficiency: fine-grained details, though essential for detailed understanding, are largely redundant for the preliminary task of selecting semantically relevant regions.

Method: The framework decouples selection from reasoning. Semantic filtering is performed on a cost-effective Macro Proxy derived from downscaled frames; the selected regions are then projected onto high-fidelity Micro Tokens for detailed understanding only when necessary.

Result: On LongVideoBench, VideoMM achieves a 6.13x speedup and a 7.4% accuracy gain over full-context baselines, and further accelerates inference by 2.73x over current leading methods.

Conclusion: VideoMM marks a paradigm shift from model-centric downsizing to adaptive perceptual granularity, establishing a highly scalable paradigm for long-video understanding.

Abstract: Scaling Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to long-form video understanding is bottlenecked by the explosion of visual tokens, which saturates context windows and incurs prohibitive costs. Current solutions predominantly rely on auxiliary models for token reduction but face a fundamental dilemma: lightweight encoder-driven approaches often overlook critical semantic information, whereas heavyweight MLLM-driven reduction negates the efficiency gains. {In this work, we identify a more fundamental inefficiency underlying this dilemma: while fine-grained visual details are essential for detailed understanding, they are largely redundant for the preliminary task of selecting semantically relevant regions. } Motivated by this, we introduce \textbf{VideoMM}, which marks a paradigm shift from model-centric downsizing to adaptive perceptual granularity. Specifically, our framework {decouples selection from reasoning} by executing semantic filtering on a cost-effective \textit{Macro Proxy} (derived from downscaled frames), and projecting the selected regions onto high-fidelity \textit{Micro Tokens} for detailed understanding only when necessary. Extensive evaluations show that VideoMM significantly outperforms existing solutions. It achieves a 6.13$\times$ speedup and a 7.4\% accuracy gain over full-context baselines on LongVideoBench, and further accelerates inference by 2.73$\times$ over current leading methods, establishing a highly scalable paradigm for long-video understanding. Our code is available at: https://github.com/adfh917k/VideoMM.

</details>


### [94] [LSREP: A Longitudinal State-Replay Protocol for Evaluating Conversational Memory, with ICE v2 as an Audited Local-First Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16730)
*Deepesh Sonar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces LSREP, a Longitudinal State-Replay Evaluation Protocol for conversational memory systems, and applies it to ICE v2, exposing failure modes that endpoint QA alone cannot reveal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational memory changes during use, so endpoint question answering cannot establish how a persistent state accumulates, ages, or incorporates revisions. Architectural descriptions and aggregate scores alone fail to identify distinct failure modes.

Method: LSREP combines ordered replay, explicit lifecycle schedules, repeated probes, evolving reference answers, and mechanism-fidelity checks. The architectural case study is ICE v2, a local-first memory middleware with typed stores, retrieval fusion, and dynamic context budgets. Instantiations include a private single-user dataset (1,985 turns, 219 probes, 1,211 probe-checkpoint observations across 52 checkpoints) and a matched public diagnostic on LongMemEval.

Result: On three ordinary-density datasets, ICE v2 shows near-zero mean quality difference from vector-RAG while selecting 32% fewer fragments but using 6.6% more estimated prompt tokens. A fourth dense dataset exposes catastrophic failures of the unbudgeted baseline. The fidelity audit shows procedural retrieval is defective, several mechanisms are unexercised, and graph utility is unestablished. On LongMemEval, ICE v2 loses decisively to pure vector-RAG: 50.8% vs 72.8% (evidence-only oracle) and 43.0% vs 69.5% (full-S), with paired differences of -22.0 points (95% CI [-26.6, -17.4]) and -26.5 ([-31.3, -21.8]). Conservative abstention accompanies severe multi-session and temporal failures.

Conclusion: ICE uses less context in the public diagnostic, establishing a quality-cost trade-off rather than superior efficiency. Replay, fidelity auditing, and public endpoint testing together expose distinct failure modes that neither architectural descriptions nor aggregate scores identify alone.

Abstract: Conversational memory changes during use, so endpoint question answering alone cannot establish how a persistent state accumulates, ages, or incorporates revisions. We introduce LSREP, a Longitudinal State-Replay Evaluation Protocol combining ordered replay, explicit lifecycle schedules, repeated probes, evolving reference answers, and mechanism-fidelity checks. Its architectural case study is ICE v2, a local-first memory middleware with typed stores, retrieval fusion, and dynamic context budgets. The private, single-user instantiation contains 1,985 turns, 219 distinct probes, and 1,211 probe-checkpoint observations across 52 checkpoints. On three ordinary-density datasets, ICE v2 has a near-zero mean quality difference from vector-RAG while selecting 32% fewer fragments but using 6.6% more estimated prompt tokens. A fourth, dense dataset exposes catastrophic failures of the unbudgeted baseline. The fidelity audit limits attribution: procedural retrieval is defective, several mechanisms are unexercised, and graph utility is not established. In a complementary matched public diagnostic, ICE v2 loses decisively to pure vector-RAG on LongMemEval: 50.8% versus 72.8% in the evidence-only oracle and 43.0% versus 69.5% in full-S. Paired differences are -22.0 points (95% CI [-26.6, -17.4]) and -26.5 ([-31.3, -21.8]). Conservative abstention accompanies severe multi-session and temporal failures. ICE uses less context in this diagnostic, establishing a quality-cost trade-off rather than superior efficiency. Together, replay, fidelity auditing, and public endpoint testing expose distinct failure modes that neither architectural descriptions nor aggregate scores identify alone.

</details>


### [95] [Beyond Episodic AI: Cognitive Field Networks for Biologically Inspired Persistent Cognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16752)
*Byung Gyu Chae*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Cognitive Field Network (CFN), a recurrent Transformer in which a history-dependent hidden state (cognitive field) re-enters subsequent inference, and shows that learning organizes persistent content-dependent dynamics whose persistence is renewed by relevant input.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cognitive Field Theory posits cognition as memory-dressed collective dynamics producing a persistent macroscopic field, but there is a need for a controlled computational testbed to study such persistent, history-dependent cognitive dynamics without prescribing an explicit memory module.

Method: The authors develop a Cognitive Field Network, a recurrent Transformer where the organized hidden field re-enters inference via Φ_{n+1}=F_θ(X_{n+1},Φ_n). New information acts on an already history-dependent collective state instead of a prescribed memory operation. They train the model across varying recurrent horizons and probe recurrence with semantic continuation, passive relaxation, periodic re-exposure, unrelated-input and recurrence-off controls, and near-paraphrased re-exposure.

Result: Learning organizes persistent, content-dependent recurrent dynamics whose timescale grows with the trained recurrent horizon. Semantic continuation propagates the recurrent state well beyond that horizon without replaying the target answer. Without content-specific support, the field shows finite passive relaxation; periodic re-exposure to relevant input renews the surviving state and drives it toward an approximately stationary nonzero regime. Unrelated-input and recurrence-off controls fail to reproduce this, and near-paraphrased re-exposure yields weaker renewal, indicating representation-sensitive persistence.

Conclusion: The CFN distinguishes three dynamical processes—collective memory dressing forming and sustaining a history-dependent field, structured input reorganizing it, and cross-cycle re-entry making the resulting state causally available to later inference—thereby offering a controlled platform for studying persistent, history-dependent cognitive dynamics without a separately prescribed memory system.

Abstract: Cognitive Field Theory (CFT) proposes that cognition arises from memory-dressed collective dynamics that generate a persistent macroscopic cognitive field. Here we develop a Cognitive Field Network (CFN), a recurrent Transformer in which the organized hidden field re-enters subsequent inference through \[ Φ_{n+1}=F_θ(X_{n+1},Φ_n). \] Rather than prescribing an explicit memory operation, the CFN allows new information to act on an already history-dependent collective state. We find that learning organizes persistent, content-dependent recurrent dynamics whose timescale increases systematically with the trained recurrent horizon. Semantic continuation propagates the recurrent state far beyond this horizon without replay of the target answer. Without content-specific support, the field exhibits finite passive relaxation, whereas periodic re-exposure to relevant input repeatedly renews the surviving state and drives it toward an approximately stationary nonzero regime. Unrelated-input and recurrence-off controls do not reproduce this behavior, while near-paraphrased re-exposure produces weaker renewal, demonstrating representation-sensitive persistence. These results distinguish three dynamical processes: collective memory dressing forms and sustains a history-dependent cognitive field, structured input reorganizes this field, and cross-cycle re-entry makes the resulting state causally available to subsequent inference. The CFN therefore provides a controlled computational platform for studying persistent, history-dependent cognitive dynamics without a separately prescribed memory system.

</details>


### [96] [Turn-level Multiscale Density Ratio Estimation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16760)
*Zishuo Zhao,Kai Chen,Ao Li,Yuan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Turn-level Multiscale Density Ratio Estimation (tlm-DRE), an alignment method for post-training LLMs in multi-turn agent scenarios, assigning turn-specific weights and asymmetric token-level training based on positive-negative space gaps. It performs competitively against traditional alignment methods on a wide range of agent benchmarks, with robust in-domain and out-of-domain multi-turn reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-enhanced agent systems show promise for complex tasks involving multi-step thinking or tool interaction, and post-training in multiple agent scenarios is necessary for better performance. Alignment methods like PPO, DPO, DIL, and GRPO are popular and effective, but most address simple single-turn tasks, leaving room for improvement in complex multi-turn tasks.

Method: Turn-level Multiscale Density Ratio Estimation (tlm-DRE) assigns different weights to corresponding turns and proposes asymmetric token-level training based on the positive-negative space gaps across multiple turns of tasks.

Result: Experiments on a wide range of agent benchmarks show that the proposed method performs competitively compared to traditional alignment methods, enabling LLMs to perform robustly in multi-turn reasoning tasks under both in-domain and out-of-domain conditions.

Conclusion: The proposed training method (tlm-DRE) enables robust multi-turn reasoning performance for LLMs, with both in-domain and out-of-domain generalization, making it a competitive alternative to traditional alignment approaches for agent post-training.

Abstract: With the rapid development of Large language model (LLM), agent systems enhanced by LLMs show huge potential in being able to deal with complex tasks, especially involving multi-step thinking or interaction with tools. For applying LLM techniques with a well-designed agent paradigm, post-training of LLM in multiple agent scenarios is necessary to achieve better performance. Among the variable post-training techniques, alignment methods such as PPO, DPO, DIL, and GRPO become popular because many papers show a significant positive impact on the model's performance by punishing negative samples while keeping acceptable training complexity. However, most alignment methods address simple single-turn tasks, and there remains room for improvement for complex multi-turn tasks. We propose Turn-level Multiscale Density Ratio Estimation (tlm-DRE), which assigns different weights on corresponding turns and proposes asymmetric token-level training based on the positive-negative space gaps across multiple turns of tasks. The results of the experiment on a wide range of agent benchmarks show that the proposed method performs competitively compared to traditional alignment methods. The proposed training method enables LLMs to perform robustly in multi-turn reasoning tasks with both in-domain and out-of-domain conditions.

</details>


### [97] [Coverage-Aware Virtual IMU Augmentation for Low-Resource Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16768)
*Jiayuan Gao,Yingwei Zhang,Ziyao Tang,Yuejia Ma,Yuanzhe Chen,Shuchao Song,Boshi Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a coverage-aware virtual IMU augmentation framework for human activity recognition (HAR). It identifies where real data is lacking via diversity and scarcity anchors in a learned embedding space, generates prompt-driven virtual IMU candidates, selects them by a cost combining anchor proximity and label consistency, and trains with reliability-based weights, consistently outperforming baselines on public HAR benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable IMU-based HAR models that generalize across users and real-world conditions require large amounts of labeled IMU data, which is costly and hard to collect. Existing augmentation and synthesis methods expand data indiscriminately, offering little new coverage and introducing unreliable supervision.

Method: A coverage-aware virtual IMU augmentation framework that decides (1) where to supplement real data, (2) how to generate and select virtual candidates, and (3) how strongly to weight them. Diversity and scarcity anchors are selected in a learned sensor embedding space, anchor dynamics are converted into prompts to generate virtual IMU candidates per anchor, candidates are ranked by a selection cost combining anchor proximity and label consistency, and the selected candidates are integrated into HAR training with reliability-based weights.

Result: Experiments on public HAR benchmarks show consistent recognition performance improvements over competitive baselines, and ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of the proposed framework design.

Conclusion: Coverage-aware, selective virtual IMU augmentation with reliability weighting effectively addresses limited labeled data and unreliable synthetic supervision, improving HAR generalization.

Abstract: IMU-based human activity recognition (HAR) enables continuous, privacy-friendly monitoring of daily activities using wearable sensors. However, building reliable HAR models that generalize across diverse users and real-world conditions requires large amounts of labeled IMU data, which are expensive and difficult to collect. Existing approaches mainly rely on augmentation or synthesis to expand available data, but indiscriminately adding virtual samples may provide little new coverage and introduce unreliable supervision. To overcome these challenges, we propose a novel coverage-aware virtual IMU augmentation framework that decides where to supplement real data, how to generate and select virtual candidates, and how strongly to weight them during training. Specifically, we select diversity and scarcity anchors in a learned sensor embedding space, convert anchor dynamics into prompts, and generate virtual IMU candidates for each anchor. We then rank candidates by a selection cost combining anchor proximity and label consistency, and incorporate the selected candidates into HAR training with reliability-based weights. Experiments on public HAR benchmarks show that our method consistently improves recognition performance over competitive baselines, and ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of the proposed framework design.

</details>


### [98] [Integrating the Analytic Hierarchy Process with Large Language Models for Transparent Multi-Criteria Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16779)
*Han Zhiguang,Farah Benamara,Pascale Zaraté*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies LLM decision-making through the Analytic Hierarchy Process (AHP), introduces the first annotated AHP benchmark and an end-to-end method enabling LLMs to execute the full AHP workflow, and shows improved alignment with expert judgments in legal and higher-education ranking cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for decision-making, but their opaque internal reasoning hinders validation and interpretation, which is especially problematic in high-stakes settings. Interpretability is therefore needed, and AHP offers a classical, structured multicriteria framework suited to this purpose.

Method: The authors adopt the Analytic Hierarchy Process as an interpretable multicriteria decision-making framework. They build a new annotated benchmark based on AHP and propose the first end-to-end approach that lets LLMs perform the complete AHP workflow.

Result: Experiments on real-world decision problems in the legal domain and the higher-education ranking domain show that the proposed method significantly improves alignment with expert judgments.

Conclusion: LLMs can be effectively integrated into the full AHP workflow via the proposed benchmark and end-to-end method, yielding decisions that better match expert judgments and enhancing interpretability in high-stakes decision-making.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly employed in a wide range of decision-making tasks. However, the opacity of their internal reasoning makes it difficult to validate or interpret their outputs, and the need for interpretability becomes especially critical in high-stakes settings. This study examines the decision-making capabilities of LLMs through the Analytic Hierarchy Process (AHP), a classical and widely used multicriteria decision-making framework. We construct a new annotated benchmark based on AHP and propose the first end-to-end approach that enables LLMs to perform the complete AHP workflow. Experiments in real-world decision problems in the legal and higher-education ranking domains show that our method significantly improves alignment with expert judgments.

</details>


### [99] [Layers, Sinks, and Scaling: Adaptive Evidence Selection for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16795)
*Zhenbin Wang,Lei Zhang,Lituan Wang,Wei Huang,Yan Wang,Zhenwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AREA is a training-free, inference-time method that adaptively allocates evidence highlighting in multimodal LLMs, deciding whether, how much, and when to highlight retrieved text and visual regions, outperforming other training-free highlighting methods across KB-VQA and standard multimodal benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs answering knowledge-intensive visual questions may overlook relevant evidence in either modality, attending weakly to needed textual sentences or visual regions. Prior highlighting approaches use a fixed, one-shot policy that fails to adapt to three variation sources: whether highlighting is necessary, how much evidence different examples require, and when different textual evidence becomes relevant as the answer unfolds.

Method: AREA formulates evidence highlighting as adaptive allocation. It generates a single probe token to read visual and textual relevance from fixed backbone layers, then makes three decisions: (i) whether to intervene, controlled by natural attention coverage and visual sink contamination; (ii) how much evidence to expose, determined by relevance entropy; and (iii) when to refresh text during generation, triggered by causal context-attention peaks.

Result: Across four KB-VQA and seven standard multimodal benchmarks with nine frozen MLLM checkpoints, AREA establishes the best performance among training-free highlighting methods.

Conclusion: AREA provides an adaptive, training-free inference-time solution for evidence highlighting in MLLMs, improving over fixed highlighting policies by dynamically deciding whether, how much, and when to highlight evidence.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can answer knowledge-intensive visual questions by combining visual evidence from images with facts retrieved from external sources. However, MLLMs may overlook relevant evidence in both modalities, attending weakly to the textual sentences or visual regions needed for the correct answer. Recent efforts address this by highlighting retrieved text and marking visual regions before generation, but apply a fixed, one-shot policy that cannot adapt to three sources of variation: whether highlighting is necessary, how much evidence different examples require, and when different textual evidence becomes relevant as the answer unfolds. We introduce Adaptive Relevance-guided Evidence Allocation (AREA), a training-free inference-time method that formulates evidence highlighting as adaptive allocation. AREA generates a single probe token to read visual and textual relevance from fixed backbone layers, then makes three decisions: i) whether to intervene (controlled by natural attention coverage and visual sink contamination), ii) how much evidence to expose (determined by relevance entropy), and iii) when to refresh text during generation (triggered by causal context-attention peaks). Across four KB-VQA and seven standard multimodal benchmarks with nine frozen MLLM checkpoints, establishes the best performance among training-free highlighting methods.

</details>


### [100] [Can We Do Interpretable NLI with Graphs Based on Atomic Propositions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16814)
*Younes Boufouss,Luc Pommeret,Thomas Gerald,Patrick Paroubek,Sophie Rosset*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A fully graph-based NLI pipeline using ConceptNet triples and a 0.8B-parameter LLM reaches 89.7% on SNLI (1.9 points below a text-based model), and hybrid graph+text reaches 92.1%, defining the "price of interpretability."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based NLI systems are accurate but their decision-making lacks auditable structures. The authors ask whether NLI can be performed using only interpretable, graph-based representations of evidence, so the classifier never directly processes the input text.

Method: The classifier never sees the raw text. Sentences are decomposed into atomic propositions and converted into ConceptNet triples via constrained decoding. Each premise-hypothesis pair is represented as three graphs: premise, hypothesis, and a retrieved ConceptNet subgraph, which are fed into a fine-tuned 0.8-billion-parameter language model.

Result: On SNLI, the pipeline achieves 89.7% accuracy, 1.9 points below an identically trained text-based model. On ANLI, it matches published RoBERTa-large performance on R2 and R3 (50%) but trails by 16 points on R1, an overall gap of 9-14 points versus its text counterpart. Ablations show graphs and text are complementary: combining both modalities reaches 92.1% on SNLI.

Conclusion: The performance gap, termed the "price of interpretability," stems from representational limitations rather than data constraints. Graph and text modalities are complementary, suggesting hybrid approaches are promising for interpretable and accurate NLI.

Abstract: While Large Language Model (LLM)-based Natural Language Inference (NLI) systems achieve high accuracy, their decision-making processes lack auditable structures. This paper explores whether NLI can be performed using only interpretable, graph-based representations of evidence. We introduce a fully graph-based pipeline where the classifier never directly processes the input text. Instead, sentences are decomposed into atomic propositions, converted into ConceptNet triples via constrained decoding, and represented as three graphs per pair: premise, hypothesis, and a retrieved ConceptNet subgraph. These graphs are then fed into a fine-tuned 0.8-billion-parameter language model. On the SNLI dataset, our pipeline achieves 89.7% accuracy, just 1.9 points below an identically trained text-based model. On ANLI, it matches the published performance of RoBERTa-large on rounds R2 and R3 (50% accuracy) but trails by 16 points on R1, resulting in an overall gap of 9 to 14 points compared to its text counterpart. We term this gap the price of interpretability and demonstrate that it stems from representational limitations rather than data constraints. Ablation studies further reveal that graphs and text are complementary: combining both modalities achieves 92.1% accuracy on SNLI.

</details>


### [101] [Execution Flexibility in Automated Planning: A Comparative Evaluation of Deordering and Reordering Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16822)
*Md. Monjurul Islam,Sabah Binte Noor,Fazlul Hasan Siddiqui,Gahangir Hossain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper surveys and empirically compares partial-order planning deordering/reordering strategies, showing that block deordering-based methods generate substantially more flexible plans than MaxSAT-based methods because they restructure causal dependencies rather than merely optimizing within the existing causal structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enhancing plan-execution flexibility is practically valuable, and removing unnecessary ordering constraints from sequential plans yields partial-order plans with more concurrency. Multiple deordering methods exist, but their capabilities have not been systematically analyzed and compared on a common benchmark, leaving unclear which approach is superior and why.

Method: The study reviews foundational concepts: partial-order planning, the producer-consumer-threat formalism, and deordering/reordering strategies. It characterizes existing methods across six dimensions—ordering, action handling, parameter handling, plan structure, concurrency, and complexity—and evaluates them against each other on a shared benchmark, contrasting block deordering/substitution with MaxSAT-based encodings.

Result: Block deordering-based approaches substantially outperform MaxSAT-based approaches despite the latter's theoretical minimum-reordering guarantees. Block methods are anytime algorithms that always return a valid result, whereas MaxSAT methods fail entirely on a substantial portion of plans and yield no partial solutions. Block substitution extends parallelism via non-concurrency constraints but helps only in resource-interaction domains. Block deordering achieves the highest flex gain per unit computation time, while MaxSAT encodings incur large computational overhead.

Conclusion: Restructuring causal dependencies via block-level grouping and subplan substitution is more effective than optimizing within a fixed causal structure. Minimum reordering is structurally limited because it preserves the plan's existing causal structure, whereas block deordering changes that structure to expose orderings that otherwise appear necessary. Practically, anytime block-based methods dominate MaxSAT-based ones in robustness and computational efficiency.

Abstract: This study covers foundational concepts for enhancing plan-execution flexibility, including partial-order planning, the producer-consumer-threat formalism, and a range of deordering and reordering strategies. Creating a partial-order plan from a sequential one by removing unnecessary ordering constraints is a practical way to improve execution flexibility, and several methods have been proposed for this task. This study analyzes their capabilities across ordering, action handling, parameter handling, plan structure, concurrency, and complexity, and evaluates them against each other on a shared benchmark. The central finding is that block deordering-based approaches, which restructure causal dependencies through block-level grouping and subplan substitution, substantially outperform MaxSAT-based approaches despite the latter's theoretical guarantees of minimum reordering. The reason is structural: minimum reordering optimizes within the causal structure already present in the plan, whereas block deordering-based methods change that structure, exposing orderings that would otherwise appear necessary. A further distinction is practical: block deordering-based methods are anytime algorithms that always return a valid result, while MaxSAT-based methods fail entirely on a substantial portion of plans and offer no partial solution when they do. Block substitution further extends the parallel execution by formalizing non-concurrency constraints, though its impact is limited to domains with resource-based interactions. On efficiency, block deordering-based approaches achieve the highest flex gain per unit of computation time, while MaxSAT-based encodings incur large computational overhead.

</details>


### [102] [CoAdapt: An LLM-based Framework for Adaptive Collaborative Perception in IIoT Robotic Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16852)
*Houssam Hajj Hassan,Antonia Maria Masucci,Lynda Zitoune,Salah-Eddine Elayoubi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoAdapt is an adaptive collaborative perception framework for industrial IoT robotic swarms that uses an LLM as a runtime fusion controller to jointly select participating robots and the fusion algorithm based on current spatial configuration and network state, achieving 38% communication reduction on OPV2V while preserving detection precision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial IoT deployments use autonomous mobile robots whose collaborative perception (e.g., shared LiDAR) yields richer environmental models than single agents. However, IIoT environments are dynamic: robot positions shift, bandwidth fluctuates, and each robot's marginal contribution to perception quality varies at runtime. Existing collaborative perception methods assume static participation and cannot adapt without sacrificing detection precision or communication efficiency.

Method: CoAdapt employs a Large Language Model as a runtime fusion controller. The LLM reasons over structured natural language descriptions of the scene derived from raw LiDAR point clouds, and jointly decides (1) which robots participate in fusion and (2) which fusion algorithm to apply, conditioned on the current spatial configuration and network state. The approach requires no task-specific training and generalizes to unseen swarm topologies.

Result: Evaluated on the OPV2V benchmark across 25 scenarios, CoAdapt reduces communication cost by 38% while maintaining detection precision comparable to static baseline approaches.

Conclusion: LLM-driven runtime control enables adaptive, training-free collaborative perception for dynamic IIoT robot swarms, balancing communication efficiency and detection precision while generalizing to unseen topologies.

Abstract: Industrial IoT environments increasingly deploy autonomous mobile robots for tasks such as material handling, product assembly, or infrastructure inspection. In such deployments, collaborative perception enables robots to share LiDAR observations and collectively construct a richer model of their environment than an individual agent could produce alone. However, industrial environments are dynamic spaces where robot positions shift continuously, network bandwidth fluctuates, and the marginal contribution of robots to perception quality varies at runtime. Existing collaborative perception approaches are designed for static participation assumptions and cannot adapt to these dynamics without sacrificing either detection precision or communication efficiency. This paper presents CoAdapt, an adaptive collaborative perception framework for IIoT robotic swarms in which a Large Language Model (LLM) serves as a runtime fusion controller, jointly deciding which robots participate in the fusion process and which fusion algorithm to apply based on the current spatial configuration and network state. The LLM reasons over structured natural language descriptions of the scene derived from raw LiDAR point clouds, requiring no taskspecific training and generalizing to unseen swarm topologies. Evaluated on the OPV2V benchmark across 25 scenarios, our approach achieves a 38% reduction in communication cost while maintaining detection precision comparable to static baseline approaches.

</details>


### [103] [Bridging Learned Visual Perception and Symbolic Belief-Space Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16884)
*Guy Azran,Michael Navat,Sarah Keren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a third paradigm, VLM-as-probabilistic-grounder, which models VLM predicate grounding uncertainty as a probability distribution over symbolic states, enabling belief-space planning for robust symbolic planning under partial observability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In partially observable settings, agents must act without full knowledge of the world state and rely on uncertain state estimation. Existing VLM-integrated approaches—VLM-as-planner and VLM-as-grounder—ignore uncertainty in the planning process, compromising robustness. Obtaining grounded and verifiable symbolic plans under such uncertainty remains a key challenge.

Method: The authors introduce VLM-as-probabilistic-grounder, which captures the uncertainty of VLM predicate groundings as a probability distribution over symbolic states. This representation enables planning in belief space, allowing the generation of robust plans under uncertainty.

Result: Experiments in simulated household robot settings demonstrate improved robustness and task success compared to deterministic grounding, showing that the approach leverages foundation models for reliable planning under uncertainty.

Conclusion: Modeling VLM grounding uncertainty as a probabilistic distribution over symbolic states and planning in belief space yields more robust and successful symbolic plans than deterministic grounding approaches.

Abstract: In partially observable settings, agents must act without full knowledge of the world state and rely on uncertain state-estimation pipelines. Obtaining grounded and verifiable symbolic plans under such uncertainty remains a key challenge. Recent work has integrated Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to bridge perception and symbolic reasoning, following two main paradigms. The first, VLM-as-planner, maps images directly to action sequences, and the second, VLM-as-grounder, grounds observations into symbolic predicates used as the initial state by off-the-shelf planners. Both approaches ignore uncertainty in the planning process, compromising robustness. We introduce a third paradigm, VLM-as-probabilistic-grounder, a novel approach that captures the uncertainty of VLM predicate groundings as a probability distribution over symbolic states. This enables planning in belief space and producing robust plans under uncertainty. Experiments in simulated household robot settings show improved robustness and task success over deterministic grounding, underscoring how our approach leverages foundation models for reliable planning under uncertainty.

</details>


### [104] [QART: A Quantum-Classical Hybrid Architecture for Long-Horizon Reasoning -- Exploring a Conditional Path toward Quantum Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16887)
*Lehao Lin,Yuheng Cheng,Guolong Liu,Yao Li,Xuning Tan,Xiyuan Zhou,Ruixi Zou,Shi Wang,Huan Zhao,Wenxuan Liu,Haifeng Wu,Junhua Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QART is a quantum-classical hybrid reasoning architecture that shows conditional asymptotic reliability separation from autoregressive LLMs and wins 14 of 15 backbone-benchmark comparisons, though the empirical results do not validate the asymptotic theory and quantum advantage remains conditional.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon reasoning is vulnerable to early errors that compound and compromise later decisions. Single-trajectory autoregressive LLMs lack mechanisms to recover from such irreversible mistakes, motivating an architecture designed to preserve optimal-path recovery under cumulative error risk.

Method: QART couples a backbone language model with quantum encoding, CIM-based QUBO optimization, and quantum decoding; semantics derive from hidden representations or generated text. The authors prove a conditional asymptotic reliability separation from autoregressive LLMs under explicit assumptions (uniformly positive optimal-path coverage, semantic fidelity, spectral certification, dynamical reachability, faithful readout), and run paired measurements on six long-horizon benchmarks with three backbones in a Codex agent environment.

Result: Autoregressive acceptance probability tends to zero when cumulative conditional risk diverges, while QART's optimal-path recovery stays bounded away from zero under the stated assumptions. Empirically QART wins 14 of 15 backbone-benchmark pairs, with gains up to 84.0% (SciCode), 47.6% (τ³-Bench), and 44.4% (Terminal-Bench 4.0); a regression of 7.8% occurs on DeepSWE.

Conclusion: The architecture alone does not imply the reliability bounds, and the empirical results do not directly validate the asymptotic separation. Quantum scaling laws and any quantum-advantage interpretation remain conditional, requiring a demonstrated CIM advantage over strong classical solvers that transfers end-to-end after all overheads.

Abstract: Long-horizon reasoning is vulnerable to early errors that compromise later decisions. We present QART, the Quantum-Augmented Reasoning Transformer, a quantum--classical hybrid architecture combining a backbone language model with quantum encoding, CIM-based QUBO optimization, and quantum decoding. Semantic information can come from hidden representations or model-generated text; detailed encoding and optimization procedures remain proprietary. Under explicit assumptions, we establish a conditional asymptotic reliability separation from single-trajectory autoregressive LLMs. For a common task family with aligned optimality and acceptance criteria, autoregressive acceptance probability tends to zero when cumulative conditional risk of irreversible errors diverges. QART's task-optimal-path recovery probability remains bounded away from zero if conditional probabilities for optimal-path coverage and semantic fidelity, spectral certification, dynamical reachability, and faithful readout remain uniformly positive under a specified resource schedule. The architecture alone does not imply these bounds. Paired measurements on six long-horizon benchmarks using DeepSeek V4 Flash, GLM-5.3, and GPT-5.5 xhigh in a Codex agent environment favor QART in 14 of 15 backbone--benchmark pairs. Relative gains reach 84.0% on SciCode, 47.6% on $τ^3$-Bench, and 44.4% on Terminal-Bench 4.0; the DeepSeek V4 Flash configuration regresses by 7.8% on DeepSWE. These results do not directly validate the asymptotic separation. Potential quantum scaling laws are formulated as conditional hypotheses. A quantum-advantage interpretation requires a demonstrated CIM quantum advantage over strong classical solvers and its transfer to end-to-end reasoning after all system overheads.

</details>


### [105] [AntennaFlow: A Generative Flow Model for Offset Correction in Phaseless Antenna Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16948)
*Yongzhi Li,Chongting Shen,Menglin Chen,Xun Jiang,Zhengpeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AntennaFlow is a three-stage framework that jointly solves phaseless and offset-vector-free near-field to far-field (NF–FF) transformation from sparse amplitude-only measurements, exploiting the fact that amplitude fields under different offsets are coordinate-transformed views of the same near field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NF–FF transformation for large-aperture antenna testing faces two coupled problems: expensive phase acquisition at millimeter-wave bands and violations of the centering assumption caused by offset mounting. Prior methods treat these issues separately, requiring either dense full-field data or known offset vectors, so a joint, data-efficient solution is needed.

Method: A three-stage framework: (1) a contrastively learned encoder mapping offset amplitude views to an offset-invariant embedding; (2) a deterministic flow-matching transport recovering the center-aligned field from offset amplitudes, without phase or offset vector; (3) the Simplified Extrapolation Technique, whose Green-function Taylor expansion is valid only for centered fields.

Result: Experiments demonstrate fast, phaseless, offset-vector-free NF–FF reconstruction from sparse amplitude-only measurements, consistently outperforming existing baselines while preserving physical consistency.

Conclusion: AntennaFlow jointly resolves the phase-acquisition and offset-mounting challenges, enabling practical, low-cost NF–FF transformation and providing a physically consistent alternative to dense full-field or offset-vector-dependent approaches.

Abstract: Near-field to far-field transformation is central to large-aperture antenna testing, yet two coupled challenges remain: costly phase acquisition at millimeter-wave bands and violations of the centering assumption under offset mounting. Existing methods address these issues separately, requiring either dense full-field data or offset vectors. We tackle both jointly by exploiting a key observation: amplitude fields under different offsets are coordinate-transformed views of the same near field. The challenge is to recover the center-aligned field from offset amplitudes without a phase or offset vector. We propose AntennaFlow, a three-stage framework: a contrastively learned encoder that maps offset views to an offset-invariant embedding, a deterministic flow-matching transport that maps offset amplitudes to center-aligned ones, and the Simplified Extrapolation Technique, whose Green-function Taylor expansion is valid only for centered fields. Experiments show that AntennaFlow enables fast, phaseless, offset-vector-free NF--FF reconstruction from sparse amplitude-only measurements, consistently outperforming existing baselines while preserving physical consistency.

</details>


### [106] [Affect-Prototype Guided Fusion for Open-Vocabulary Incomplete Multi-modal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16962)
*Yichi Zhang,Shenyue Wang,Jing Luo,Chunyang Yu,Xinyu Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: APCF is a candidate-free generative framework for open-vocabulary multimodal emotion recognition under incomplete modalities, using an affect-prototype library to guide fusion of available modal features before LLM-based label generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-vocabulary multimodal emotion recognition (OV-MER) must often operate with missing or unsynchronized modalities due to device limits and privacy. Existing OV-MER methods assume full-modal input, while incomplete-modality fusion methods target fixed-label settings, so they cannot fuse emotional cues guided by arbitrary emotion semantics in an open-vocabulary context.

Method: The proposed Affect-Prototype-Conditioned Fusion (APCF) framework extends modal contribution learning to arbitrary emotional semantics. It builds an affect-prototype library that explicitly models multimodal contribution characteristics across diverse emotions, providing dynamic constraints for modal fusion from different semantic perspectives. Given available modal features, it performs conditional retrieval and feature aggregation, and feeds the refined fused affective representation into an LLM decoder to generate open-vocabulary emotion labels.

Result: Experiments on the OV-MERD+ and MER-FG datasets show that APCF substantially outperforms state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: APCF addresses incomplete open-vocabulary emotion recognition by conditioning modal fusion on emotion-aware affect prototypes, yielding a candidate-free generative pipeline that is robust to missing modalities and superior to existing methods.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary multimodal emotion recognition (OV-MER) aims to generate open natural-language emotion labels from multimodal affective cues. In real-world scenarios, however, complete and synchronized modal data are difficult to obtain due to limitations of acquisition devices and user privacy constraints. Existing OV-MER methods are largely designed for full-modal inputs, and fail to perform effective feature fusion under modal missing conditions. Meanwhile, current fusion approaches designed for incomplete modalities mainly focus on fixed-label recognition context, and cannot satisfy the demand for fuse emotional cues guided with arbitrary emotion semantics in OV-MER context. To tackle these challenges, this paper proposes an Affect-Prototype-Conditioned Fusion (APCF) framework for incomplete open-vocabulary emotion recognition. As a candidate-free generative framework, APCF extends modal contribution learning to scenarios guided by arbitrary emotional semantics. Specifically, we construct an affect-prototype library to explicitly model multimodal contribution characteristics corresponding to diverse emotions, which provides dynamic constraints for modal fusion under different emotional semantic perspectives. Conditional retrieval and feature aggregation are conducted based on available modal features. The refined fused affective representations are then fed into an LLM decoder to produce open-vocabulary emotion labels. Experiments on the OV-MERD+ and MER-FG datasets demonstrate that APCF substantially outperforms state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [107] [FlexEE: Self-Speculative and KV-Compatible Early Exiting for Offloading-Aware LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17008)
*Qihu Xie,Ziwei Li,Yi Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlexEE is an early exiting framework for resource-constrained, offloading-based LLM inference, using layer-wise exit supervision, self-speculative decoding over a Top-K local vocabulary, and dynamic hidden state management. It achieves up to 3.16x speedup on Llama2-7B and 2.83x on Llama3-8B with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM inference is constrained by both computation and memory, particularly in offloading-based deployments where model weights are transferred across memory hierarchies during autoregressive decoding. Reducing the number of executed layers can lower per-token latency and avoid costly weight movement, motivating an early exiting approach for this setting.

Method: FlexEE comprises three components: (1) layer-wise exit supervision for reliable intermediate-layer prediction, (2) self-speculative decoding over a Top-K local vocabulary for low-cost exit decisions, and (3) dynamic hidden state management for KV-cache-correct and memory-aware execution.

Result: Across generative and downstream tasks, FlexEE enables efficient early exit with minimal accuracy degradation, delivering up to 1.27x/3.16x and 1.25x/2.83x end-to-end speedups on Llama2-7B and Llama3-8B under 0%/50% weight offloading, respectively.

Conclusion: FlexEE demonstrates that early exiting can be made practical for resource-constrained and offloading-based LLM inference, providing substantial end-to-end speedups with minimal accuracy degradation.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) inference is often constrained by both computation and memory, especially in offloading-based deployments where model weights are transferred across memory hierarchies during autoregressive decoding. In this setting, reducing the number of executed layers can lower per-token latency while also avoiding costly weight movement. Motivated by this observation, we present FlexEE, an early exiting framework for resource-constrained and offloading-based LLM inference. FlexEE makes early exiting practical for LLM decoding through layer-wise exit supervision for reliable intermediate-layer prediction, self-speculative decoding over a Top-K local vocabulary for low-cost exit decisions, and dynamic hidden state management for KV-cache-correct and memory-aware execution. Across generative and downstream tasks, FlexEE enables efficient early exit with minimal accuracy degradation, delivering up to 1.27$\times$/3.16$\times$ and 1.25$\times$/2.83$\times$ end-to-end speedups on Llama2-7B and Llama3-8B under 0\%/50\% weight offloading, respectively.

</details>


### [108] [ThinkFlow: Self-Evolving Probabilistic Latent Memory for Lifelong Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17010)
*Cai Ke,Xin Liu,Han Zhang,Jiangyue Yan,Zike Yuan,Ling Deng,Yue Yu,Hui Wang,Ruifeng Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ThinkFlow is an end-to-end latent memory framework for lifelong conversational agents that replaces explicit textual memory with probabilistic latent memory skills, enabling label-free lifelong personalization via a test-time evolution paradigm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explicit textual memory pipelines for lifelong conversational agents suffer from a severe information bottleneck, losing subtle behavioral patterns and emotional shifts. They are also static post-deployment and cannot autonomously adapt to personal habits and preferences without manual feedback.

Method: Inspired by cognitive science accounts of latent mental models refined through predictive coding, ThinkFlow dynamically compresses conversational flows into probabilistic latent memory skills, consolidating complex user states into disentangled continuous vectors without semantic interference. It introduces a test-time evolution paradigm coupling teacher-guided latent alignment (to bootstrap the initial state) with a self-supervised next-user-utterance prediction task (for continuous refinement), overcoming cold-start and achieving label-free lifelong personalization.

Result: Extensive experiments on long-term conversation benchmarks show that ThinkFlow significantly outperforms prevailing memory systems, delivering highly personalized and contextually accurate responses over extended multi-session interactions.

Conclusion: Bypassing the text bottleneck via latent memory and test-time evolution offers an effective route to lifelong, label-free personalization in conversational agents.

Abstract: Lifelong conversational agents rely on memory systems to maintain deep, context-aware interactions with users. However, existing explicit textual memory pipelines suffer from a severe information bottleneck, often losing subtle behavioral patterns and emotional shifts. Furthermore, being typically static post-deployment, they cannot autonomously adapt to personal habits and preferences without manual feedback. Cognitive science, however, suggests that humans maintain mental models purely in a latent space and continuously refine them through predictive coding. Inspired by this, we propose \textbf{ThinkFlow}, a novel end-to-end latent memory framework for lifelong conversational agents. ThinkFlow bypasses the text bottleneck by dynamically compressing conversational flows into probabilistic latent memory skills, autonomously consolidating complex user states into disentangled, continuous vectors without semantic interference. To break this barrier, we introduce a test-time evolution paradigm. By coupling teacher-guided latent alignment to bootstrap the initial state with a self-supervised next-user-utterance prediction task for continuous refinement, the framework successfully overcomes cold-start challenges and achieves label-free lifelong personalization. Extensive experiments on long-term conversation benchmarks demonstrate that ThinkFlow significantly outperforms prevailing memory systems, providing highly personalized and contextually accurate responses over extended multi-session interactions.

</details>


### [109] [ORDER: Task-Conditioned Routing for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17012)
*Aurélien Pellet,Julien Perez,Marie Puren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ORDER is a query-conditioned RAG framework that jointly adapts indexing and retrieval to each incoming query, outperforming baselines and state-of-the-art RAG systems on large-scale heterogeneous historical archives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional RAG pipelines use a fixed indexing and retrieval configuration decided at preprocessing time. This one-size-fits-all design fails in domain-expert settings where heterogeneous queries demand different chunking granularities, metadata constraints, and source-selection strategies, causing configurations effective for one query family to underperform on others.

Method: ORDER (Optimal Routing for Dynamic Evidence Retrieval) discovers semantic clusters over questions associated with a corpus and learns, per cluster, a chunking strategy plus a suited metadata filtering and reranking configuration. At inference, queries are routed to the appropriate pre-built index via nearest-centroid assignment. It further employs a supervised query router (QRe) predicting which collections likely contain relevant evidence, combined with a Uniform Multi-source Sampler (UMS) that spreads the retrieval budget evenly across selected sources.

Result: On large-scale, heterogeneous historical archives, conditioning both indexing and retrieval on the query consistently outperforms naive baselines and strong state-of-the-art RAG systems in complex expert-domain environments.

Conclusion: Query-conditioned joint adaptation of indexing and retrieval is an effective strategy for RAG in expert-domain settings with heterogeneous queries, surpassing fixed-configuration and state-of-the-art alternatives.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines typically rely on a fixed indexing and retrieval configuration determined at preprocessing time. This one-size-fits-all design is ill-suited to domain-expert settings, where heterogeneous queries require different chunking granularities, metadata constraints, and source-selection strategies. As a result, configurations that are effective for one family of queries often perform poorly for others. In this paper, we introduce ORDER (Optimal Routing for Dynamic Evidence Retrieval), a query-conditioned RAG framework that jointly adapts indexing and retrieval to the incoming query. Our approach first discovers semantic clusters over a given set of questions associated to a corpus and learns, for each cluster, a chunking strategy together with a suited metadata filtering and reranking configuration. At inference time, queries are routed to the appropriate pre-built index through nearest-centroid assignment. To further improve retrieval, we propose a supervised query router (QRe) that predicts which collections are most likely to contain relevant evidence, coupled with a Uniform Multi-source Sampler (UMS) that allocates the retrieval budget evenly across the selected sources. We evaluate our framework on large-scale, heterogeneous historical archives and show that conditioning both indexing and retrieval on the query consistently outperforms both naive baselines and strong state-of-the-art RAG systems in complex expert-domain environments.

</details>


### [110] [SKIP: a Self-knowledge-guided Step-wise Preference Learning Framework for Concise Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17019)
*Qinhong Lin,Yuhao Zhang,Yinglun Feng,Zhongliang Yang,Linna Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SKIP is a self-knowledge-guided step-wise preference learning framework that compresses Chain-of-Thought reasoning in LLMs. It combines lightweight fine-tuning, a knowledge probing mechanism to elicit answers at each reasoning step, and DPO-based preference learning built from intermediate step correctness. It improves compression while mitigating the accuracy loss typical of concise reasoning methods, generalizes to out-of-distribution datasets, and includes ablation studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-Thought reasoning is effective but causes overthinking, leading to computational overhead, inference latency, and even degraded performance in LLMs. Existing concise reasoning frameworks reduce output length but substantially sacrifice accuracy. The paper aims to achieve efficient reasoning compression without the accompanying performance degradation.

Method: SKIP, a self-knowledge-guided step-wise preference learning framework. It (1) starts with lightweight fine-tuning to adjust the model's output style, (2) introduces a knowledge probing mechanism to guide the model to output an answer at each reasoning step, and (3) constructs preference data based on the correctness of intermediate steps, using DPO to steer the model toward more efficient and correct reasoning.

Result: Experimental results show that SKIP effectively improves reasoning compression while mitigating the performance degradation that follows fine-tuning. It also demonstrates strong generalization ability on out-of-distribution datasets, and ablation studies on the framework's component parameters are conducted.

Conclusion: SKIP provides an effective approach to concise reasoning by combining self-knowledge probing with step-wise preference learning, achieving a better trade-off between compression and accuracy and generalizing across out-of-distribution datasets.

Abstract: While Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has been proven to be effective, it often leads to overthinking, resulting in computational overhead, inference latency, and even degraded performance in large language models (LLMs). Existing concise reasoning frameworks significantly compromise accuracy while compressing the length of output. In this paper, we propose SKIP, a self-knowledge-guided step-wise preference learning framework. Starting with lightweight fine-tuning to adjust the model's output style, SKIP introduces a carefully designed knowledge probing mechanism to guide model to output an answer at each reasoning step. Based on the correctness of intermediate steps, we construct preference data that guide the model toward more efficient and correct reasoning by leveraging DPO. Experimental results demonstrate that our method effectively improves reasoning compression while mitigating performance degradation after fine-tuning. Besides, SKIP shows strong generalization ability on out-of-distribution datasets. We further conducted ablation studies on the component parameters of our framework.

</details>


### [111] [Sparse MLLM Anchors, Dense Adaptation: Breaking the Self-Referential Loop in Wild Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17040)
*Zhenbin Wang,Lei Zhang,Lituan Wang,Yan Wang,Zhao Zhang,Wei Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MASA complements model-internal adaptation signals in wild test-time adaptation with structured semantic descriptions from a frozen multimodal LLM, queried only for a few reliability-ranked anchors, then propagated to neighbors and stored in an online prototype memory to guide lightweight adaptation of normalization-affine parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most WTTA methods derive adaptation signals (predictive uncertainty, sample reliability, local feature geometry) from the model being adapted. When the source model is unreliable under shift, these self-referential signals reinforce its own errors, forming a closed loop.

Method: MASA queries a frozen MLLM only for a small set of diverse, reliability-ranked anchors to limit inference cost. The descriptions capture object family and nuisance factors (style, viewpoint, occlusion). MASA encodes these descriptions, propagates them to neighboring test samples, and stores the resulting visual-semantic information in an online prototype memory. Descriptor-aware retrieval from this memory provides an auxiliary target for lightweight adaptation of normalization-affine parameters.

Result: Evaluation is conducted on the WTTA ImageNet-C benchmark under limited-batch, mixed-domain, and imbalanced-label-shift settings with ResNet and ViT backbones. (Specific numerical results are not reported in the abstract.)

Conclusion: MASA addresses the self-referential error-reinforcement problem of model-internal WTTA signals by anchoring adaptation to external MLLM-derived semantic descriptions, while keeping MLLM inference cost low through selective anchoring and prototype-memory propagation.

Abstract: Wild test-time adaptation (WTTA) updates a source model online under small test batches, concurrent distribution shifts, and time-varying class imbalance. Most WTTA methods derive their adaptation signals, including predictive uncertainty, sample reliability, and local feature geometry, from the model being adapted. When the source model is unreliable under shift, these signals can reinforce its own errors, forming a self-referential loop. We introduce MASA (Multimodal-LLM-Anchored Semantic Adaptation), which complements model-internal evidence with structured semantic descriptions from a frozen multimodal large language model (MLLM). To limit inference cost, MASA queries the MLLM only for a small set of diverse, reliability-ranked anchors. The resulting descriptions capture the object family and nuisance factors such as style, viewpoint, and occlusion. MASA encodes these descriptions, propagates them to neighboring test samples, and stores the resulting visual-semantic information in an online prototype memory. Descriptor-aware retrieval from this memory provides an auxiliary target for lightweight adaptation of normalization-affine parameters. We evaluate MASA on the WTTA ImageNet-C benchmark under limited-batch, mixed-domain, and imbalanced-label-shift settings with ResNet and ViT backbones.

</details>


### [112] [Neuro-Symbolic Hierarchical Intention Anticipation in Human Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17064)
*Farnaz Soleimani,Abdelghani Chibani,Yacine Amirat,Ghazaleh Khodabandelou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formulates human behavior anticipation as hierarchical goal inference plus structured prediction, using a compact Hierarchical Planning Decoder (HPD) on top of a frozen neuro-symbolic encoder. Trained with soft neuro-symbolic regularization and decoded with hard reachability masks, HPD advances goal/activity/intention anticipation across four ontological levels, with gains that grow over the anticipation horizon and improve compositional generalization and logical validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assistive autonomous systems must anticipate human goals before an observed behavior is complete, but exact motor forecasting is not the right target. The paper argues for anticipating goals and remaining structured behavior from a partially observed multimodal episode, and for enforcing ontological/logical validity of hierarchical predictions.

Method: A compact Hierarchical Planning Decoder (HPD) is attached to a frozen neuro-symbolic recognition encoder. It predicts, at four ontological levels, next actions, remaining activities and low-level intentions, and the episode high-level intention (HLI). Training uses soft neuro-symbolic regularization combining transition-coherence and hierarchical continuity losses; inference uses hard reachability masks enforcing ontological validity. Evaluation is on a compositional four-level benchmark of 15,002 multimodal episodes built over NTU RGB+D 120 features, against sequential baselines.

Result: Advantage over the strongest sequential baseline grows with the anticipation horizon: +1.7 points at step 1 to +7.3 points at step 3 (top-5). Under compositional generalization (one parent association per multi-parent low-level intention held out), the advantage widens to +4.9 points at step 1. At episode level, 96.8% of anticipated trajectories satisfy joint logic constraints, versus 88.1% for the strongest baseline and a 73.9% ground-truth floor. Soft logic terms alone yield a 59.8–71.1% relative reduction of HLI-reachability violations, and hard masks eliminate them entirely.

Conclusion: Neural generation supplies predictive ranking while symbolic constraints supply ontological logical validity; their combination yields coherent hierarchical anticipation. Remaining challenges are compositional goal generalization and unordered set prediction.

Abstract: Assistive autonomous systems must anticipate human goals before an observed behavior is complete. This article formulates anticipation as goal inference from a partially observed multimodal episode together with structured prediction of the remaining behavior, rather than exact motor forecasting. A compact Hierarchical Planning Decoder (HPD) is attached to a frozen neuro-symbolic recognition encoder and predicts, at four ontological levels, the next actions, the remaining activities and low-level intentions, and the episode high-level intention(HLI). The decoder is trained with soft neuro-symbolic regularization combining transition-coherence and hierarchical continuity losses, and is decoded with hard reachability masks that enforce ontological validity at inference. On a compositional four-level benchmark of 15,002 multimodal episodes built over NTU RGB+D 120 features, three headline properties are observed together. The advantage over the strongest sequential baseline grows with the anticipation horizon, from +1.7 points at step 1 to +7.3 points at step 3 (top-5). Under compositional generalization, where one parent association per multi-parent low level intention is held out, this advantage widens to +4.9 points at step 1. At the episode level, 96.8% of anticipated trajectories satisfy the joint logic constraints, above the 88.1% strongest-baseline value and the 73.9% ground-truth floor; soft logic terms alone account for a 59.8 to 71.1% relative reduction of HLI-reachability violations, and the hard masks then eliminate them entirely. Neural generation supplies predictive ranking, symbolic constraints supply onto logical validity, and their combination yields coherent hierarchical anticipation while exposing remaining challenges in compositional goal generalization and unordered set prediction.

</details>


### [113] [Sample-Conditioned Representation Selection for Audio Few-Shot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17076)
*Fengrui Liu,Ningxin Shen,Yi Li,Yiwei Fu,Feng Liu,Jiangmeng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAMPLESELECT learns an input-adaptive, fixed-budget feature mask to mitigate concentrated, class-dependent representation shift in few-shot audio classification, achieving the best OOD accuracy and 4.90-8.38 point gains over a matched full-representation control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Few-shot audio classifiers may rely on foreground-background co-occurrences and degrade when these correlations shift. On SpurAudio, the resulting representation shift is concentrated and class dependent: for ResNet12, the top 10% of channels account for 82.80% of the null-corrected shift contribution.

Method: SAMPLESELECT predicts a fixed-budget feature mask independently per input while keeping the encoder and source classifier frozen. Training employs differentiable Gumbel Top-k selection with foreground classification and cross-background contrastive losses; inference uses deterministic Top-k masks and support-only linear adaptation.

Result: Across ResNet12 and Conv64 on 5-way 1-shot and 5-shot evaluation, SAMPLESELECT achieves the best OOD accuracy among compared methods and improves the matched full-representation control by 4.90-8.38 percentage points. Ablations and representation analyses support the learned selection mechanism.

Conclusion: Selecting a small, input-adaptive subset of channels effectively reduces sensitivity to shifted foreground-background co-occurrences, improving few-shot audio classification under distribution shift. Code is available at https://github.com/Cross-Innovation-Lab/SAMPLESELECT/.

Abstract: Few-shot audio classifiers may rely on foreground-background co-occurrences and fail when those correlations shift. On SpurAudio, the resulting representation shift is concentrated and class dependent: for ResNet12, the top 10 percent of channels explain 82.80 percent of the null-corrected shift contribution. We propose SAMPLESELECT, which predicts a fixed-budget feature mask independently for each input while keeping the encoder and source classifier frozen. Training uses differentiable Gumbel Top-k selection with foreground classification and cross-background contrastive losses; inference uses deterministic Top-k masks and support-only linear adaptation. Across ResNet12 and Conv64 in 5-way 1-shot and 5-shot evaluation, SAMPLESELECT gives the best OOD accuracy among the compared methods and improves the matched full-representation control by 4.90-8.38 percentage points. Ablations and representation analyses further support the learned selection mechanism. Code is available at https://github.com/Cross-Innovation-Lab/SAMPLESELECT/

</details>


### [114] [Interactive Memory Learning for Long-Term Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17088)
*Cai Ke,Jiangyue Yan,Han Zhang,Xin Liu,Zike Yuan,Yue Yu,Hui Wang,Ruifeng Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ICML is a multi-agent framework that turns long-term conversational memory from a static, passive archive into a learnable, interactive memory policy via a session synthesis pipeline for expert data and online reinforcement learning with Planner and Trigger agents co-evolving through delayed reward propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-term conversational agents rely on a static heuristic paradigm: information is passively archived without adaptive memory valuation, so the memory system cannot self-evolve or align its management with evolving user needs.

Method: ICML first uses a session synthesis pipeline to generate expert data for rapid test-time adaptation in unseen scenarios. It then applies an online reinforcement learning mechanism in which a Planner agent selectively encodes high-value information and a Trigger agent dynamically retrieves it to optimize response quality. The two agents co-evolve through continuous interaction feedback, synchronized by a delayed reward mechanism that propagates future feedback back to earlier storage decisions.

Result: ICML significantly outperforms strong baselines and shows a unique ability to continuously improve response quality as interactions accumulate.

Conclusion: Reframing memory as a learnable, interactive multi-agent policy enables adaptive memory valuation and self-evolution aligned with user expectations, yielding cumulative gains in long-term conversational quality.

Abstract: Recent advancements in large language models have significantly enhanced the capabilities of agents in modeling long-term conversations. Despite these successes, existing approaches typically adopt a static heuristic paradigm, where information is passively archived without adaptive memory valuation. Consequently, these methods fail to self-evolve or align their memory management with evolving user needs. To address this, we propose ICML (InteraCtive Memory Learning), a multi-agent framework that transforms the memory mechanism from a passive archive into a learnable, interactive memory policy. Specifically, we first employ a session synthesis pipeline to generate expert data, facilitating rapid test-time adaptation in unseen scenarios. Building on this, ICML utilizes an online reinforcement learning mechanism where a Planner agent selectively encodes high-value information and a Trigger agent dynamically retrieves it to optimize response quality, whereby the two agents co-evolve through continuous interaction feedback. Crucially, both agents are synchronized through a delayed reward mechanism that propagates future feedback back to earlier storage decisions, ensuring memory policies are precisely aligned with user expectations. Experimental results demonstrate that ICML significantly outperforms strong baselines, exhibiting the unique capability to continuously improve response quality as interactions accumulate.

</details>


### [115] [Scaling-Score Conformal Prediction for Multi-Target Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17091)
*Sylvain Rousseau,Soundouss Messoudi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes the scaling-score conformal method for multi-target regression, delivering model-agnostic, single-calibration-set joint prediction regions with four nested output types and finite-sample valid joint coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending conformal prediction to joint multi-dimensional regions in multi-target regression is difficult: max-aggregation ignores scale differences, copula-based methods are only asymptotically valid, rectangular methods split the calibration set, and quantile/density-based methods require training a specialized model beyond a plain point predictor.

Method: The scaling-score conformal method requires only component-wise absolute residuals and a single calibration set. It produces four nested output types: an outer rectangle (SCO) with valid joint coverage, the exact set R_alpha, a staircase (SC2) over-approximation of R_alpha, and an inner rectangle (SCI). A single hyperparameter gamma in (0,1) controls the base-rectangle quantile level independently of alpha. The authors prove downward-closedness and a rectangular sandwich bound, and derive a closed-form outer rectangle.

Result: Experiments on 29 real-world datasets confirm valid joint coverage; SC2 with gamma = 1 - alpha consistently achieves competitive volume relative to baselines, with the advantage growing with the output dimension d.

Conclusion: The scaling-score conformal method provides a model-agnostic, sample-efficient approach to joint conformal prediction in multi-target regression, offering valid joint coverage and competitive prediction-region volume that improves with higher output dimensionality.

Abstract: Multi-target regression requires a model to simultaneously predict several related outputs. Conformal prediction provides distribution-free, finite-sample marginal coverage guarantees, but extending these to joint multi-dimensional regions in a model-agnostic, sample-efficient manner remains challenging: max-aggregation ignores scale differences, copula-based methods are only asymptotically valid, rectangular methods typically split the calibration set, and quantile or density-based methods require training a specialised model beyond a plain point predictor. We propose the scaling-score conformal method, which is model-agnostic (requires only component-wise absolute residuals), uses a single calibration set, and yields four nested output types: an outer rectangle (SCO) with valid joint coverage, the exact set R $α$ , a staircase (SC 2 ) over approximation of R $α$ , and an inner rectangle (SCI). A single hyperparameter $γ$ $\in$ (0, 1) controls the base-rectangle quantile level independently of $α$. We prove downward-closedness and a rectangular sandwich bound and derive a closed-form outer rectangle. Experiments on 29 realworld datasets confirm valid joint coverage; SC 2 with $γ$ = 1-$α$ consistently achieves competitive volume relative to baselines, with the advantage growing with output dimension d.

</details>


### [116] [Semi-Supervised Learning-Based Genetic Biomarkers Dataset for Multiple-Stage Hepatocellular Carcinoma Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17100)
*Ahmed Ammar Kubba,Manar Abu Talib,Jibran Sualeh Muhammad,Ali Bou Nassif,Abdalla Sayed Mohamed,Darko Castven,Jens U. Marquardt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study builds a multi-stage hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) genomic dataset via XGBoost and semi-supervised learning, achieving 96.5% classification accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Liver cancer causes many global deaths, and HCC accounts for over 90% of cases, making automated classification urgent. However, there is a distinct lack of publicly available HCC datasets with genomic data needed to train AI models for automated HCC classification.

Method: The authors construct a multi-stage HCC dataset using XGBoost and semi-supervised learning across three separate genomic biomarker datasets. Existing labels are leveraged in the semi-supervised learning process to label the proposed dataset.

Result: The proposed dataset contains 770 patient samples across five classes representing normal tissue and different HCC stages, with each sample comprising 11,150 gene expression levels. The XGBoost model achieved a final classification accuracy of 96.5% during semi-supervised learning.

Conclusion: The study delivers a publicly oriented multi-stage HCC genomic dataset generated via XGBoost-based semi-supervised learning, demonstrating high classification accuracy and addressing the scarcity of genomic HCC datasets for AI model training.

Abstract: Liver cancer is a complex disease responsible for a high number of deaths across the globe each year, making automated solutions for liver cancer classification urgent. The most common form of liver cancer is hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC), accounting for over 90% of liver cancer cases. There is a distinct lack of publicly available HCC datasets utilizing genomic data, which is necessary for training artificial intelligence (AI) models for automated HCC classification. This study proposes constructing a multi-stage HCC dataset using XGBoost and Semi-Supervised learning on three separate datasets of genomic biomarkers, utilizing their existing labels in the Semi-Supervised learning process to label the proposed dataset. The proposed dataset consists of 770 patient samples in total, categorized into five classes that represent normal tissue alongside different stages of HCC. Each sample in the dataset consists of 11,150 different gene expression levels. The XGBoost model demonstrated a final classification accuracy of 96.5% during the Semi-Supervised learning process.

</details>


### [117] [Symbolic Separation: Grounding Deep Agents in Knowledge Graphs for Trustworthy Operational Data Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17107)
*Baibek Davletiyarov,Junaid Ahmed Khan,Andrea Bartolini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A deep agent that can only act on data through an ontology-constrained Virtual Knowledge Graph raises end-to-end task success on 49.9 TB of supercomputer telemetry from 43% to 86%, prevents silent data-integrity errors, and cuts token cost 2.4x, enabling a smaller on-premise model to beat a larger one.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
